Chambered invariants of real Cauchy–Riemann operators
Aleksander Doan, Thomas Walpuski

Abstract
Motivated by counting pseudo-holomorphic curves in symplectic Calabi–Yau 3 3 –folds, this article studies a chamber structure in the space of real Cauchy–Riemann operators on a Riemann surface, and constructs three chambered invariants associated with such operators: n Bl n_{\Bl} , n 1 , 2 n_{1,2} , n 2 , 1 n_{2,1} . The first of them is defined by counting pseudo-holomorphic sections of bundles whose fibre is the blow-up of 𝐂 2 / { ± 1 } {\mathbf{C}}^{2}/\{\pm 1\} . The other two are defined by counting solutions to the ADHM vortex equations. We conjecture that n 1 , 2 n_{1,2} and n 2 , 1 n_{2,1} are related to the Pandharipande–Thomas and rank 2 2 Donaldson–Thomas invariants in algebraic geometry.



\useosf

1
Introduction

1.1
Pseudo-holomorphic curves in dimension six

This article is motivated by counting pseudo-holomorphic curves in symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
–folds;
that is: symplectic manifolds
(X,\omega)
with
\dim_{\mathbf{R}}X=6
and
{\rm c}_{1}(X,\omega)=0
.
The standard approach in symplectic geometry is Gromov–Witten theory
[
LT98
,
FO99
,
Rua99
,
Par16
]
.
In contrast, algebraic geometry abounds with other curve counting invariants constructed using sheaf theory, such as Donaldson–Thomas
[
Tho00
,
MNOP06
,
MNOP06a
]
and Pandharipande–Thomas invariants
[
PT09
]
.
Physics also suggests the existence of the Gopakumar–Vafa BPS counts
[
GV98
,
GV98a
]
,
whose direct mathematical construction remains elusive despite numerous efforts
[
HST01
,
Kat08
,
KL12
,
MT18
]
.

The sheaf-theoretic methods used in algebraic geometry have no obvious counterpart in symplectic topology.
However, the recent proof of the MNOP conjecture
[
Par23
]
shows that the DT/PT invariants of projective Calabi–Yau
3
–folds are invariant under symplectomorphisms, suggesting the possibility of a purely symplectic construction.
Foundational ideas in this direction were introduced in
[
DT98
,
DS11
]
and developed in
[
Hay12
,
Wal13
]
and
[
DW19
]
.

The goal of the present article is to advance the proposal
[
DW19
]
.
Its basic idea is simple.
The DT/PT invariants in algebraic geometry are indexed by a homology class
A\in{\rm H}_{2}(X;\mathbf{Z})
but not the genus of curves like the Gromov–Witten invariants.
Thus, a naive approach to defining them in symplectic topology is to count embedded
J
–holomorphic curves
of arbitrary genus
in class
A
for an almost complex structure
J
tamed by
\omega
.
For a generic
J
, such a count is finite
[
DW21
]
and,
if
A
is a primitive class, independent of
J

[
Zin11
,
DW23
]
.
However, if
A
is not primitive, this count depends on
J
.
Indeed, suppose that
A
is divisible by
k\in{\mathbf{N}}
in
{\rm H}_{2}(X,\mathbf{Z})
.
As
J
varies in the space
{J}(X,\omega)
of tamed almost complex structures, a sequence of
J
–holomorphic curves
\tilde{C}
with
[\tilde{C}]=A
may collapse with multiplicity
k
to a curve
C
with
A=k[C]
, which changes the count of curves in class
A
.

This collapse phenomenon is related to the notion of
k
–rigidity
.
Recall that deformations of
C
are governed by a differential operator
{\mathfrak{d}}_{C,J}^{N}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Gamma(C,NC)\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,NC)
with
NC\to C
denoting the normal bundle.
This is a
real Cauchy–Riemann operator
: the sum of a
{\mathbf{C}}
–linear Dolbeault operator and a
{\mathbf{C}}
–anti-linear operator of order zero.
Let
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
be a holomorphic branched cover of degree
k
.
A collapse of the kind described above is infinitesimally modeled on a non-zero element of the kernel of
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}_{C,J}^{N}
, the pull-back operator.
If the curve
C
is
k
–rigid
in the sense that
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}_{C,J}^{N}
has trivial kernel for all
\pi
, then such a collapse cannot happen.

Denote by
{W}_{k}\subset{J}(X,\omega)
the
wall of failure of
k
–rigidity
: the set of those
J
for which at least one
J
–holomorphic curve fails to be
k
–rigid, and let
{W}_{\infty}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}{W}_{k}
.
The summary of the above discussion is that the count of
J
–holomorphic curves in class
A
depends only on the connected component of
J
in
{J}(X,\omega){\backslash}{W}_{\infty}
.
Wendl proved that this set has codimension one
[
Wen19
]
, so that a path of
{J}(X,\omega)
will, in general, intersect
{W}_{\infty}
, causing a jump in the number of curves.
In
[
DW19
]
, we proposed to replace the naive count by a weighted count:

(1.1)

N(X,\omega)=\sum_{A\in{\rm H}_{2}(X,\mathbf{Z})}\sum_{g=0}^{\infty}\sum_{[C]\in{M}_{A,g}^{*}(X,J)}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}n_{k}({\mathfrak{d}}_{C,J}^{N})\cdot q^{kA},

where
J
is generic,
{M}_{A,g}^{*}(X,J)
is the moduli space of simple
J
–holomorphic maps of genus
g
,
q
is a formal variable, and
n_{k}
are certain integer weights.
These weights themselves should depend on
{\mathfrak{d}}_{C,J}^{N}
and jump whenever there exists a branched cover
\pi\colon\tilde{C}\to C
such that
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}_{C,J}^{N}
has non-trivial kernel.
To obtain an invariant of
{J}(X,\omega)
from
(1.1)
, the wall-crossing formulae of the
n_{k}
and the wall-crossing arising from multiple-cover phenomena need to cancel precisely.

Ansatz
(1.1)
is inspired by similar ideas in higher-dimensional gauge theory
[
DS11
,
Wal13
,
HW15
,
Hay17
]
and low-dimensional topology
[
BH98
,
BHK01
,
CLM02
,
Lim00
,
Lim03
,
BZ20
]
.
More importantly, in the context of the present article,
Taubes’ Gromov invariant for symplectic
4
–manifolds
[
Tau96
]

is of the form
(1.1)
with a judicious choice of weights.
Recently,
[
BS23
]
defined new invariants of symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
–folds and homology classes
A
with
A/2
primitive.
Their invariant is also of the form
(1.1)
, with the key difference that the sum is taken over curves of genus
g\leqslant h
for some
h
, so that the resulting invariant is indexed by
A
and
h
. From the analytic point of view, this means that it suffices to understand the part of
{W}_{2}
corresponding to covers
\pi\colon\tilde{C}\to C
with
g(\tilde{C})\leqslant h
.

1.2
Walls in the space of real Cauchy–Riemann operators

In contrast to the situations considered by
[
Tau96
]
and Bai–Swaminathan, in the context of DT/PT theory it seems necessary to understand how a generic path in
{J}(X,\omega)
intersects
{W}_{\infty}
without any genus bound.
We conjecture that the number of such intersections is finite for a generic path.
If this is the case, the proof of independence of (
1.1
) on
J
can be reduced to studying local wall-crossing formulae.
Unfortunately, this conjecture does not follow from
[
Wen19
]
’s work
[
Wen19
]
, even when combined with the techniques of geometric measure theory used to prove similar statements in
[
DW21
,
DIW21
]
.
Proving the conjecture with current methods appears challenging, which reflects fundamental gaps in present understanding of degenerations of
J
–holomorphic curves to multiple covers without assuming a genus bound.

The first result of this article is a local version of the finiteness conjecture for
k=2
, i.e. for double covers.
Here, local means that we consider only a neighborhood of the curve
C
, in the following sense.
Given a complex vector bundle
V\to C
, denote by
{C}{R}(V)
the space of real Cauchy–Rieman operators on
V
.
The main example is
V=NC
as before, so that
{\mathfrak{d}}_{C,J}^{N}\in{C}{R}(NC)
.
In the local setting, the Calabi–Yau condition is replaced by

(1.2)

2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)=0.

The space
{C}{R}(V)
serves as a local version of
{J}(X,\omega)
.
Let
{W}_{2}\subset{C}{R}(V)
be the analogue of the wall of failure of
2
–rigidity, that is: the set of operators
{\mathfrak{d}}
for which there exists a double branched cover
\pi\colon\tilde{C}\to C
such that
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}
has non-trivial kernel.

1.3
see
Theorem 3.40
.

Let
C
be a closed connected Riemann surface.
Let
V
be a complex vector bundle over
C
satisfying
(1.2)
.
The set

{W}_{2}\subset{C}{R}(V)

is a proper wall in the sense of
2.6
.
In particular: a generic path in
{C}{R}(V)
intersects
{W}_{2}
in finitely many points.

The key difficulty is that
{W}_{2}
is a countable union of walls corresponding to covers
\pi\colon\tilde{C}\to C
of different genera.
Therefore, a priori, generic paths might intersect
{W}_{2}
in a countable dense set.
The proof of
Theorem 1.3
builds on ideas from
[
DW21
,
DW23
,
DIW21
]
and combines transversality techniques and geometric measure theory
[
All72
,
DSS18
,
DSS17
,
DSS20
]
.

1.3
Chambered invariants from blow-ups

In what follows,
V\to C
is a complex vector bundle satisfying
\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V=2
and
(1.2)
.
The remainder of this article is concerned with the construction of three weights

n_{\Bl},\quad n_{1,2},\quad\text{and}\quad n_{2,1}

which are candidates for
n_{2}
in
(1.1)
and can potentially be used to define curve counting invariants for homology classes
A
such that
A/2
is primitive.
The key property we require is that they depend on a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
and are constant on the connected components of
{C}{R}(V){\backslash}{W}_{2}
; we will refer to any function with this property as a
chambered invariant
.

The first of these chambered invariants counts pseudo-holomorphic sections of a non-compact fibration over
C
.
Let
\check{p}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V/\{\pm 1\}\to C
be the quotient of
V
by fibrewise multiplication by
\pm 1
..
Define a fibration
q\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\to C
by blowing-up each fibre
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}/\{\pm 1\}
of
\check{p}
.

1.4
see
Section 4
.

There is a chambered invariant

n_{\Bl}\in{\rm H}^{0}\lparen{C}{R}(V){\backslash}{W}_{2};\mathbf{Z}[[x]]\rparen

such that for generic
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V){\backslash}{W}_{2}
and generic
J\in{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))
compatible with
{\mathfrak{d}}
,

n_{\Bl}({\mathfrak{d}})=\sum_{d\in{\mathbf{N}}_{0}}\#{M}_{\Bl}(J,d)\cdot x^{d}.

Here

{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))
, the space of admissible almost complex structures on
{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)
, the compatibility condition means
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}(J)={\mathfrak{d}}
where

{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))\to{C}{R}(V)

is a map defined in
Section 4.2
, and

{M}_{\Bl}(J,d)
denotes the space of
J
–holomorphic sections of
q
of degree
d
.

This is reminiscent of the construction of the Gromov–Witten invariants of fibrations in
[
MS12
, §8.6]
.
However, a substantial novel complication arises because
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
is non-compact and there are no a priori
C^{0}
or energy bounds for
J
–holomorphic sections.
Indeed, sequences of
J
–holomorphic sections can escape to infinity,
introducing a non-compactness phenomenon in
{M}_{\Bl}(J,d)
different from the familiar bubbling phenomenon.

At the heart of
Theorem 1.4
lies a relationship between this non-compactness phenomenon and the failure of
2
–rigidity.
An operator
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V)
induces an almost complex structure
J_{\mathfrak{d}}
on
V/\{\pm 1\}
with the property that
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{W}_{2}
if and only if
V/\{\pm 1\}
admits a
J_{\mathfrak{d}}
–holomorphis section.
An admissible
J
agrees with
J_{\mathfrak{d}}
away from a compact set, and if a sequence of
J
–holomorphic sections of
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
escapes to infinity, then after rescaling it converges to a
J_{\mathfrak{d}}
–holomorphic section of
V/\{\pm 1\}
.
In particular: this cannot occur if
{\mathfrak{d}}\notin{W}_{2}
.
The key difficulty in the proof is extracting a convergent subsequence.
Since the rescaling causes the almost complex structure to degenerate,
Gromov’s compactness theorem cannot be applied.
The proof uses a combination of geometric measure theory and a twist on Radó’s theorem in complex analysis
[
Rad24
]
.

1.4
Chambered invariants from gauge theory

The chambered invariants
n_{1,2}
and
n_{2,1}
are constructed in a different way using gauge theory.
They are virtual counts of solutions of the
(r,k)
ADHM vortex equation
for
(r,k)=(1,2)
and
(r,k)=(2,1)
respectively.
For every
r,k\in{\mathbf{N}}
,
the
(r,k)
ADHM vortex equation is a system of partial differential equations for a
\mathrm{U}(k)
connection and a Higgs field on a Riemann surface.
For
r=k=1
this is the well-known vortex equation
[
Nog87
,
Bra90
,
Gar93
]
.
In general, it shares many features with the vortex equation and Hitchin equation and can be thought of as a generalization of the two.
The Higgs field takes values in a vector bundle associated with the quaternionic representation

\mathrm{U}(k)\circlearrowright S_{r,k}\coloneqq\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}\lparen{\mathbf{C}}^{r},\mathbf{R}^{4}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{C}}^{k}\rparen\oplus\mathbf{R}^{4}\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}{\mathfrak{u}}(k).

This representation appears in
[
ADHM78
]
’s construction of instantons.
For
r\geqslant 2
its hyperkähler quotient
S_{r,k}{/\!\!/\!\!/}\mathrm{U}(k)
is the Uhlenbeck completion of the moduli space of
\mathrm{SU}(r)
ASD instantons of charge
k\in{\mathbf{N}}
on
\mathbf{R}^{4}

[
ADHM78
,
DK90
]
.
For
r=1
it is the
k
–fold symmetric product
\Sym^{k}\mathbf{R}^{4}

[
Nak99
, Proposition 2.9]
.

The
(r,k)
ADHM vortex equations depend on a choice of
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V)
as well as an auxilliary
\mathrm{U}(k)
bundle determined by its degree
d\in\mathbf{Z}
.
For reasons that shall be explained in
Section 5
it is convenient to choose a
K_{C}
–valued complex symplectic form
\Omega\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Lambda_{\mathbf{C}}^{2}V\to K_{C}
and restrict attention to
{C}{R}(V,\gamma)
, the subspace of those real Cauchy–Riemann operators which are self-adjoint with respect to the isomorphism
\gamma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V\cong V^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}
induced by
\Omega
.

The moduli spaces
{M}_{r,k}({\mathfrak{d}},d)
of solutions to the
(r,k)
ADHM vortex equation are of virtual dimension zero,
but possibly plagued by reducibles and non-compactness.
Non-compactness arises from pseudo-holomorphic sections of bundles with fibres
S_{r,k}{/\!\!/\!\!/}\mathrm{U}(k)
[
Hay12
]
.
If
(r,k)=(1,2)
or
(r,k)=(2,1)
, then
S_{r,k}{/\!\!/\!\!/}\mathrm{U}(k)=\Sym^{2}\mathbf{R}^{4}
and, therefore, one might expect a relation with the failure of
2
–rigidity.
Indeed, in both cases, a variation of the compactness theorem in
[
WZ21
]
explained in
Appendix D
confirms this expectation, leading to the following result.

1.5
see
Theorem 5.35
.

Assume that
g(C)\geqslant 1
.
There is a chambered invariant

n_{1,2}\in{\rm H}^{0}\lparen{C}{R}(V,\gamma){\backslash}{W}_{2},\mathbf{Z}[[x,x^{-1}]]\rparen

such that for every
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V,\gamma){\backslash}{W}_{2}

n_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}})=\sum_{d\in\mathbf{Z}}\#{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},d)\cdot x^{d}

with
\#{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},d)
denoting a virtual count of
{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},d)
.

The count being virtual has to do with the fact
{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},d)
is only compact and of virtual dimension zero, but possibly obstructed and containing reducibles.
We conjecture that
n_{1,2}
is the weight
n_{2}
a symplectic version of Pandharipande–Thomas theory.
In fact, if
{\mathfrak{d}}
lies in the chamber containing Dolbeault operators,
then the correspondence between
(1,2)
ADHM vortices and local stable pairs
[
BGM03
,
“’AG03
,
DW19
,
Dia12
]
indicate a relationship between
n_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}})
and local Pandhariphande–Thomas invariants of
C
.

There is an analogous result for the
(2,1)
ADHM vortex equation.

1.6
see
Theorem 5.45
.

Assume that
g(C)\geqslant 1
.
There is a chambered invariant

n_{2,1}\in{\rm H}^{0}\lparen{C}{R}(V,\gamma){\backslash}{W}_{2},\mathbf{Z}[[x,x^{-1}]]\rparen

such that for every
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V,\gamma){\backslash}{W}_{2}

n_{2,1}({\mathfrak{d}})=\sum_{d\in\mathbf{Z}}\#{M}_{2,1}({\mathfrak{d}},d)\cdot x^{d}

with
\#{M}_{2,1}({\mathfrak{d}},d)
denoting a virtual count of
{M}_{2,1}({\mathfrak{d}},d)
.

We conjecture that
n_{2,1}
is related to a symplectic version of Donaldson–Thomas theory counting rank
2
instantons on symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
–folds;
this connection to
6
–dimensional gauge theory is discussed in
[
DS11
,
Wal13
,
Hay17
]
.
When
{\mathfrak{d}}
is a Dolbeault operator,
n_{2,1}({\mathfrak{d}},d)
has been computed in various examples in
[
Doa17
]
.
The precise relation of
n_{1,2}
and
n_{2,1}
to algebro-geometric invariants will be explored in future articles.

In addition to potential connections to enumerative geometry,
the motivation for
Theorem 1.5
and
Theorem 1.6
comes from gauge theory and low-dimensional topology.
The
(r,k)
ADHM vortex equations are dimensional reductions of generalized Seiberg–Witten equations on manifolds of dimension
3
and
4
.
The study of these equations has been an active area of research starting with Taubes’s work on the compactness problem for the moduli space of flat
\mathbf{P}\mathrm{SL}_{2}({\mathbf{C}})
connections
[
Tau13
]
and subsequent work
[
Tau13a
,
HW15
,
Tau16
,
Tau17
,
WZ21
]
.
The picture emerging from this work is that the count of solutions to generalized Seiberg–Witten invariant is not a topological invariant.
This is in stark contrast to the well-studied invariants defined using moduli spaces of instantons and Seiberg–Witten monopoles.
Instead, this count depends on the Riemannian metric on the manifold as well as other perturbations of the equations.
In dimension
3
and
4
, it is an open problem to show that in the space of such perturbations
{P}
there is a proper wall
{W}\subset{P}
such that the count of solutions is invariant in each connected component of
{P}{\backslash}{W}
.

Theorem 1.3
,
Theorem 1.5
, and
Theorem 1.6
establish this conjectural picture in dimension
2
for the Seiberg–Witten equations associated with the
(1,2)
and
(2,1)
ADHM representations.

Acknowledgements

The authors are indebted to Jacek Rzemieniecki for a careful proofreading of parts of this article and to Clifford Taubes for answering questions about his work on harmonic
\mathbf{Z}_{2}
spinors.
Aleksander Doan is supported by Trinity College, Cambridge, and was a MATH+ Visiting Scholar at Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin while writing this article.

2
Chambered invariants and wall-crossing formulae

The following concept is pervasive in many areas of geometry,
especially gauge theory and symplectic topology.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
G
be an abelian group.
Let
{P}
be a topological space.
A
G
–valued invariant of
{P}
is an element

{\rm I}\in{\rm H}^{0}({P};G)=\Hom(\pi_{0}({P});G)\subset\Map({P};G).

Of course, if
{P}
is path-connected,
then
{\rm H}^{0}({P};G)=G
;
therefore: a posteriori,
{\rm I}\in G
.
Typically, a choice of
p\in{P}
determines an elliptic partial differential equation and
{\rm I}(p)
is extracted from the moduli space of solutions.
Here is an abstract caricature.

2.2
.

Let
{M},{P}
be a Banach manifolds.
Let
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{M}\to{P}
be a
proper
Fredholm map of index
d\in{\mathbf{N}}_{0}
together with an orientation of its index bundle.
Denote by
\Omega_{d}^{\mathrm{SO}}
the oriented bordism group (of a point) in dimension
d
.
There is a unique

{\rm I}\in{\rm H}^{0}({P};\Omega_{d}^{\mathrm{SO}})\quad\text{with}\quad{\rm I}(p)=[\pi^{-1}(p)]

for every
p\in{P}{\backslash}\pi(\mathrm{Crit}(\pi))
.
If
{M}\subset{X}
,
then this can be promoted to
{\rm I}\in{\rm H}^{0}({P};\Omega_{d}^{\mathrm{SO}}({X}))
and combined with any
\alpha\in\Hom(\Omega_{d}^{\mathrm{SO}}({X}),G)
to obtain
\alpha_{*}{\rm I}\in{\rm H}^{0}({P};G)
.

In order to implement the above in relevant geometric situations,
issues with transversality, compactness, and symmetries need to be overcome.
Sometimes this is not quite possible,
but one can still obtain the following.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
G
be an abelian group.
Let
{P}
be a topological space.

(1)

A
G
–valued chambered invariant of
{P}
consists of a subset,

{W}\subset{P}
, the
wall
,
and an element

{\rm C}{\rm I}\in{\rm H}^{0}({P}{\backslash}{W};G).

(2)

The
wall-crossing formula
of
{\rm C}{\rm I}\in{\rm H}^{0}({P}{\backslash}{W};G)

is the equivalence class

[{\rm C}{\rm I}]\in\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)\coloneqq\coker\lparen{\rm H}^{0}({P};G)\to{\rm H}^{0}({P}{\backslash}{W};G)\rparen.

A chambered invariant lifts to an invariant if and only if its wall-crossing formula vanishes.
This applies, in particular, to the sum of two chambered invariants with opposite wall-crossing formulae.

The space of wall-crossing formulae
\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)
depends on the intersection behavior of
{W}
and paths
{\mathbf{p}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace[0,1]\to{P}
.
Indeed,
the long exact sequence of relative cohomology induces an isomorphism

\Delta\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)\cong\ker\lparen{\rm H}^{1}({P},{P}{\backslash}{W};G)\to{\rm H}^{1}({P};G)\rparen

such that

\Delta([{\rm C}{\rm I}])([{\mathbf{p}}])={\rm C}{\rm I}({\mathbf{p}}(1))-{\rm C}{\rm I}({\mathbf{p}}(0)),

identifying

{\rm H}^{1}({P},{P}{\backslash}{W};G)\cong\Hom({\rm H}_{1}({P},{P}{\backslash}{W});G)

via the universal coefficient theorem.

2.4
.

Let
{W}\subset{P}
is a closed subset and a codimension
1
submanifold.
Denote by
{\mathfrak{o}}
the coorientation local system of
{W}
.

(1)

There is a homomorphism

\sharp\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\rm H}^{0}({W};G\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathfrak{o}})\to\Hom({\rm H}_{1}({P},{P}{\backslash}{W}),G)\cong{\rm H}^{1}({P},{P}{\backslash}{W};G)

such that
if
{\mathbf{p}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace[0,1]\to{P}
is a
C^{1}
path with
{\mathbf{p}}(0),{\mathbf{p}}(1)\notin{W}
and transverse to
{W}
,
then

(2.5)

\sharp(\alpha)[{\mathbf{p}}]\coloneqq\sum_{t\in{\mathbf{p}}^{-1}({W})}\alpha({\mathbf{p}}(t)).

This sum is finite because
{W}
is closed.

Here
\alpha
is evaluated at
{\mathbf{p}}(t)
using the coorientation of
{W}
induced by
\dot{\mathbf{p}}(t)
.

(2)

If
{T}
is a closed tubular neighborhood
{W}
,
then excision and direct inspection produce an injective homomorphism

\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta_{\mathrm{loc}}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}\mathrm{WCF}({T},{W};G)\cong{\rm H}^{0}({W};G\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathfrak{o}}).

(This is an elementary instance of the Thom isomorphism.)
These assemble into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm H}^{0}({W};G\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathfrak{o}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm H}^{1}({P},{P}{\backslash}{W};G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathrm{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp}

(3)

As a consequence of the above, there is a short exact sequence

\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta_{\mathrm{loc}}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\rm H}^{0}({W};G\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathfrak{o}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sharp}}{{\to}}{\rm H}^{1}({P};G).

Unfortunately,
{W}\subset{P}
rarely is a codimension
1
submanifold.
The following weaker condition is a reasonable substitute and does hold in the situation considered in this article.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
{P}
be a Banach manifold.
A subset
{W}\subset{P}
is a
proper wall in
{P}
if the following hold:

(1)

{W}\subset{P}
is a closed subset.

(2)

There are a Banach manifold
{E}
and a Fredholm map
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}\to{P}
of index at most
-1
with

{W}=\im\pi.

Moreover:

(a)

The map
\pi
is
essentially injective
;
that is:
there are a Banach manifold
{N}
and a Fredholm map
\nu\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{N}\to{E}
of index at most
-1
such that

\pi|_{{E}{\backslash}\im\nu}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}{\backslash}\im\nu\to{P}{\backslash}\im\lparen\pi\circ\nu\rparen

is injective.

(b)

The map
\pi
is
essentially proper
;
that is: there are a Banach manifold
{S}
and a Fredholm map
\sigma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{S}\to{E}
of index at most
-1
such that

\pi|_{{E}{\backslash}\lparen\im\nu\cup\im\sigma\rparen}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}{\backslash}\lparen\im\nu\cup\im\sigma\rparen\to{P}{\backslash}\lparen\im\lparen\pi\circ\nu\rparen\cup\im\lparen\pi\circ\sigma\rparen\rparen

is proper.

Proposition 2.7
.

Assume the situation of
2.6
.
If
{\mathbf{p}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace[0,1]\to{P}
is a
C^{1}
path with
{\mathbf{p}}(0),{\mathbf{p}}(1)\notin{W}
and transverse to

\pi
,
\pi\circ\nu
, and
\pi\circ\sigma
,
then

{\mathbf{p}}^{*}{E}
is a finite set and the map
{\mathbf{p}}^{*}{E}\to(0,1)
is injective;
that is: along
{\mathbf{p}}
(potential) wall-crossing occurs in a finite subset
\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{N}\}\subset(0,1)
.

Proof.

Since
{\mathbf{p}}
is transverse to
\pi
,

{\mathbf{p}}^{*}{E}
is a
0
–dimensional manifold.
Since
{\mathbf{p}}
is transverse to
\pi\circ\nu
and
\pi\circ\nu
,
it
{\mathbf{p}}^{*}{E}\to[0,1]
is injective and proper.
In particular,
{\mathbf{p}}^{*}{E}
is compact; hence: finite.
∎

Appendix B
explains the analogue of
2.4
if
{W}
is a proper wall.

3
The wall of failure of
2
–rigidity

The purpose of this section is to recall (or introduce) the notion of
2
–rigidity,
define the wall of failure of
2
–rigidity in the space of real Cauchy–Riemann operators,
and to prove that it is a proper wall in the sense of
2.6
.

Much of the material in this section is similar to the discussion in
[
Wen19
,
DW23a
]
,
which itself is inspired by
[
Tau96
,
Eft16
]
.
The essential technical novelty is contained in establishing properties

(1)
and
(22.b)
in
2.6
.
This is achieved using tools from geometric measure theory:
an idea already employed in
[
DW21
,
DIW21
]
.

3.1
Real Cauchy–Riemann operators

Let
C
be a Riemann surface.
Let
V
be a complex vector bundle over
C
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
real Cauchy–Riemann operator
on
V
is an
\mathbf{R}
–linear differential operator

{\mathfrak{d}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Gamma\lparen C,V\rparen\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,V)=\Gamma\lparen C,V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\rparen

satisfying

{\mathfrak{d}}(f\cdot s)=s\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\bar{\partial}f+f\cdot{\mathfrak{d}}s

for every
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(C,\mathbf{R})
and
s\in\Gamma(C,V)
.
Here

\overline{K}_{C}\coloneqq\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(\overline{TC},{\mathbf{C}})
and

\bar{\partial}f\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}\lparen{\rm d}f+i\cdot{\rm d}f\circ j\rparen
with
j
denoting the complex structure on
C
.

3.2
.

A
Dolbeault operator
on
V
is a real Cauchy–Riemann operator
\bar{\partial}
on
V
which is also
{\mathbf{C}}
–linear.
The Koszul–Malgrange theorem
[
KM58
]
establishes a correspondence between
holomorphic vector bundles
{V}
over
C

and
pairs
(V,\bar{\partial})
consisting of
a complex vector bundle
V
over
C
and a Dolbeault operator
\bar{\partial}
on
V
.
If
{\mathfrak{d}}
is a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
,
then

{\mathfrak{d}}=\bar{\partial}+{\mathfrak{a}}\quad\text{with}\quad\bar{\partial}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen{\mathfrak{d}}-i{\mathfrak{d}}i\right\rparen\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathfrak{a}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\left\lparen{\mathfrak{d}}+i{\mathfrak{d}}i\right\rparen.

By construction,

\bar{\partial}
is Dolbeault operator,
and
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Gamma\lparen C,\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(\overline{V},V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C})\rparen
.

Proposition 3.3
.

The subspace

{C}{R}(V)\subset\Hom\lparen\Gamma(C,V),\Omega^{0,1}(C,V)\rparen

of real Cauchy–Riemann operators on
V
is an affine subspace modelled on

\Gamma\lparen C,{H}(V)\rparen\quad\text{with}\quad{H}(V)\coloneqq\Hom\lparen V,V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\rparen.\qed

3.4
.

If
C
is closed,
then
{\mathfrak{d}}
extends to a Fredholm operator
{\mathfrak{d}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W^{1,2}\Gamma(C,V)\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}(C,V)
with

\ind{\mathfrak{d}}=2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)

by Riemann–Roch.
Here
W^{1,2}
and
L^{2}
are with respect to arbitrary (and immaterial) choices of
a Riemannian metric on
C
and
a Hermitian inner product and a unitary covariant derivative on
V
.

Set

V^{\dagger}\coloneqq\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(V,K_{C})=V^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}

and
K_{C}\coloneqq\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(TC,{\mathbf{C}})
.
The isomorphism
K_{C}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\cong\Lambda^{2}T^{*}C\otimes{\mathbf{C}}
induces (complex) perfect pairings

(3.5)

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\lparen V^{\dagger}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\rparen\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}V\to\Lambda^{2}T^{*}C\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V^{\dagger}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\lparen V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\rparen\to\Lambda^{2}T^{*}C\otimes{\mathbf{C}}

with respect to which

\langle\lambda\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{\zeta},v\rangle=\langle\lambda,v\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{\zeta}\rangle

for every
\lambda\in V^{\dagger}
,
v\in V
, and
\overline{\zeta}\in\overline{K}_{C}
.

Definition 3.6
.

The
adjoint
of a real Cauchy–Riemann operator
{\mathfrak{d}}
on
V
is the real Cauchy–Riemann operator
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\dagger}
on
V^{\dagger}
over
C
characterised by

\int_{C}\operatorname{Re}\langle{\mathfrak{d}}^{\dagger}s,t\rangle=\int_{C}\operatorname{Re}\langle s,{\mathfrak{d}}t\rangle

for every
s\in\Gamma_{c}(C,V^{\dagger})
and
t\in\Gamma_{c}(C,V)
.

Proposition 3.7
.

The map

\cdot^{\dagger}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{C}{R}(V)\to{C}{R}(V^{\dagger})

is an isomorphism of affine spaces;
in fact:

\lparen{\mathfrak{d}}^{\dagger}\rparen^{\dagger}={\mathfrak{d}}

with respect to the identification
(V^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}=V
.
∎

The bulk of this article is concerned with the case

\ind{\mathfrak{d}}=2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)=0.

This is equivalent to
\deg(V)=\deg(V^{\dagger})
;
hence:
V\cong V^{\dagger}
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\gamma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V\cong V^{\dagger}
be an isomorphism.
A real Cauchy–Riemann operator
{\mathfrak{d}}
on
V
over
C
is
\gamma
–self-adjoint
if

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\dagger}={\mathfrak{d}}^{\gamma}\coloneqq\lparen\gamma\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{1}_{\overline{K}_{C}}\rparen\circ{\mathfrak{d}}\circ\gamma^{-1}.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
\gamma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V\cong V^{\dagger}
be an isomorphism.
The subspace

{C}{R}(V,\gamma)\subset\Hom\lparen\Gamma(C,V),\Omega^{0,1}(C,V)\rparen

of
\gamma
–self-adjoint real Cauchy–Riemann operators on
V
is an affine subspace modelled on

\Gamma\lparen C,{H}(V,\gamma)\rparen\quad\text{with}\quad{H}(V,\gamma)\coloneqq\{{\mathfrak{a}}\in{H}(V):{\mathfrak{a}}^{\dagger}={\mathfrak{a}}^{\gamma}\}.

Proof.

In light of
3.3
it suffices to prove that
{C}{R}(V,\gamma)
is non-empty.
A moment’s thought shows that
if
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V)
,
then

\frac{1}{2}\lparen{\mathfrak{d}}+\lparen\gamma\circ\mathbf{1}_{\overline{K}_{C}}\rparen^{-1}\circ{\mathfrak{d}}^{\dagger}\circ\gamma\rparen
is
\gamma
–self-adjoint.
In particular,
{C}{R}(V,\gamma)\neq\varnothing
.
∎

3.10
.

The isomorphism
\gamma
induces an isomorphism

V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\cong V^{\dagger}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\cong V^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{2}T^{*}C
.
This, in turn, induces an isomorphism

{H}(V,\gamma)\cong S^{2}V^{*}\otimes\Lambda^{2}T^{*}C.

Here
S^{2}V^{*}
denotes the second symmetric power of
V^{*}
.

3.11
.

Let
X
be a Calabi–Yau
3
–fold together with a choice of holomorphic volume form
\theta\in\Omega^{3,0}(X,{\mathbf{C}})
.
If
C\subset X
is a holomorphic curve,
then
\rho\coloneqq\operatorname{Re}\theta
induces an isomorphism
\gamma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace NC\cong NC^{\dagger}
and the Dolbeault operator
\bar{\partial}_{NC}
governing the infinitesimal deformation theory of
C
is
\gamma
–self-adjoint.
In fact,
\bar{\partial}_{NC}
is the (formal) Hessian at
C
of the (real part) of the Chern–Simons functional
\Psi
mentioned in
[
DT98
, §8]
.

If
(X,J)
is an almost complex
3
–fold with
c_{1}(X,J)=0
,
then there is a definite
3
–form
\rho\in\Omega^{3}(X)
in the sense of
[
Don18
, Definition 1]
;
this amounts to a reduction of structure group along
\mathrm{SL}_{3}({\mathbf{C}})\hookrightarrow\GL_{3}({\mathbf{C}})
.
If
C\subset X
is a
J
–holomorphic curve,
then
\rho
induces an isomorphism
\gamma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace NC\cong NC^{\dagger}
and the corresponding real Cauchy–Riemann operator
{\mathfrak{d}}_{NC}
on
NC
is
\gamma
–self-adjoint.
If
\rho
is closed, then
{\mathfrak{d}}_{NC}
is self-adjoint and
\operatorname{Re}\Psi
is defined.

Typically,
\rho
cannot be arranged to be to be closed.
However, there is an
h
–principle for closed definite
3
–forms
[
Don18
,
May23
]
.
In particular, there is a closed definite
3
–form
\rho^{\prime}
isotopic to
\rho
.
Unfortunately, the
C^{0}
–dense version of this
h
–principle does not hold.
In particular,
if
(X,\omega)
is a symplectic Calabi–Yau
3
–fold,
then it is not clear whether or not a closed definite
3
–form tamed by
\omega
exists.
It would be interesting to better understand this issue.

3.2
Homogeneous almost complex structures

This section reviews the correspondence between
real Cauchy–Riemann operators on
V
and
homogeneous almost complex structures on the total space of
V
.
Denote by
p\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V\to C
the projection map.

Definition 3.12
.

An almost complex structure
J\in\Gamma(V,\End(TV))
on the total space of
V
is
homogeneous

if:

(1)

The linear maps

p^{*}V\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\hookrightarrow}}TV\quad\text{and}\quad TV\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{*}}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}p^{*}TC

are complex linear.

(2)

For every
\lambda\in\mathbf{R}

the linear map
\lambda\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V\to V
given by multiplication with
\lambda
is
J
–holomorphic.

Lemma 3.13
.

Denote by
\tau\in\Gamma(V,p^{*}V)
the tautological section

\tau(v)=v.

Let
W
be a vector bundle over
C
.
Let
s\in\Gamma(V,p^{*}W)
.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The section
s
is
homogeneous
;
that is:
for every
\lambda\in\mathbf{R}

\lambda^{*}s=\lambda s

with respect to the identification
\Gamma(V,\lambda^{*}p^{*}W)=\Gamma(V,p^{*}W)
induced by
p\circ\lambda=p
.

(2)

There is an
\hat{s}\in\Gamma\lparen X,\Hom(V,W)\rparen
such that for every
v\in V

s(v)=\hat{s}(p(v))(v);

that is:

s\in\im[\langle p^{*}\cdot,\tau\rangle\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Gamma(X,\Hom(V,W))\hookrightarrow\Gamma(V,p^{*}W)].

Proof.

Evidently,
(2)
implies
(1)
.

To prove that
(1)
implies
(2)
it suffices to consider
V=\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}^{r}
and
W={\underline{\mathbf{R}}}^{s}
, by locality.
If
s\in C^{\infty}(C\times{\mathbf{C}}^{r},\mathbf{R}^{s})
is homogeneous,
then

s(x,v)={\rm d}_{(x,0)}s(v).

Therefore,
s\in\im\langle p^{*}\cdot,\tau\rangle
.
∎

3.13
and the inclusion

p^{*}\lparen\overline{K}_{C}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}V\rparen\subset p^{*}\Hom(TC,V)\xhookrightarrow{\iota\circ\cdot\circ p_{*}}\End(TV)

induce an inclusion

\Gamma\lparen C,\Hom(V,V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C})\rparen\hookrightarrow\Gamma(V,\End(TV)).

Proposition 3.14
.

The subspace

{J}_{h}(V)\subset\Gamma(V,\End(TV))

of homogeneous almost complex structures on the total space of
V
is an affine subspace modelled on
\Gamma\lparen C,\Hom(V,V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C})\rparen
.

Proof.

Evidently,
{J}_{h}(V)
is non-empty.

A moment’s thought shows that
if
J_{0}\in{J}_{h}(V)
and
{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Gamma\lparen C,\Hom(V,V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C})\rparen
,
then
J_{0}+{\mathfrak{a}}\in{J}_{h}(V)
.

Let
J_{0},J\in{J}_{h}(V)
.
Set
{\mathfrak{a}}\coloneqq J-J_{0}\in\Gamma(V,\End(TV))
.
By
3.12

(1)
,

{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Gamma(V,p^{*}\Hom(TC,V))
.
Since
J^{2}=(J_{0}+{\mathfrak{a}})^{2}
and also using
3.12

(1)
,

{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Gamma(V,p^{*}\lparen V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\rparen)
.
By
3.12

(2)
,

{\mathfrak{a}}
is homogeneous.
Therefore, by
3.13
,

{\mathfrak{a}}\in\Gamma\lparen C,\Hom(V,V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C})\rparen
.
∎

Proposition 3.15
.

There is an isomorphism of affine spaces

{\mathfrak{d}}_{\cdot}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{J}_{h}(V)\to{C}{R}(V)

such that for every
J\in{J}_{h}
and
s\in\Gamma(C,V)

{\mathfrak{d}}_{J}s=\tfrac{1}{2}\lparen Ts-J\circ Ts\circ j\rparen\in\Omega^{0,1}(C,V)\subset\Omega^{0,1}(C,s^{*}TV);

in particular:

s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{J}
if and only if
s
is
J
–holomorphic.

Proof.

Let
s\in\Gamma(C,V)
.
Define
\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{J}s\in L^{2}\Gamma\lparen C,s^{*}TV\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\rparen
by the above formula.

By
3.12

(1)
,
p
is
J
–holomorphic.
Therefore and since
p\circ s=\mathrm{id}_{C}
,

\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{J}s\in\Gamma\lparen C,V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}\rparen
.

To show that
\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}_{J}
is a real Cauchy–Riemann operator, by locality, it suffices to consider
V=\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}^{r}
.
In this case,
TV=p^{*}(TC\oplus V)
and, by
3.14
,

J=\begin{pmatrix}j&0\\
{\mathfrak{a}}&i\end{pmatrix}.

A direct computation shows that
{\mathfrak{d}}_{J}=\bar{\partial}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathfrak{a}}
.

This constructs the map
J\mapsto{\mathfrak{d}}_{J}
.
Evidently, it is affine and its underlying linear map is the isomorphism
-\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

3.3
Four perspectives on
2
–rigidity

The purpose of this section is to introduce the concept of
2
–rigidity and present four points of view on it.
Henceforth, assume that
C
is closed and connected (unless said otherwise).
Moreover, choose a Hermitian metric on
V
.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
.
Let
\tilde{C}
be a Riemann surface.
Let
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
be a holomorphic map.
There is a unique real Cauchy–Riemann operator
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}
on
\pi^{*}V
,
the
pull-back
of
{\mathfrak{d}}
along
\pi
,
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(C,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0,1}(C,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\tilde{C},\pi^{*}V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0,1}(\tilde{C},\pi^{*}V)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{d}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}}

commutes.

Proof.

By locality,
it suffices to prove this for
V=\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}^{r}
.
In this case,
\pi^{*}V=\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}^{r}
and
the assertion is a consequence of the isomorphism
C^{\infty}(C,{\mathbf{C}}^{r})\otimes_{C^{\infty}(C)}C^{\infty}(\tilde{C})\cong C^{\infty}(\tilde{C},{\mathbf{C}}^{r})
.
∎

Definition 3.17
.

Suppose that
C
is closed.
A real Cauchy–Riemann operator
{\mathfrak{d}}
on
V
is
2
–rigid
if
for every closed, connected Riemann surface
\tilde{C}
and
every non-constant holomorphic map
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
of degree at most two

\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}=0.

3.18
.

There are, of course, concepts of
k
–rigidity for
k\in{\mathbf{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
;
cf.
[
BP01
,
Eft16
,
Wen19
]
.

3.74
contains an attempt to justify the restriction to
k=2
in the present article.

The second perspective on
2
–rigidity is a translation of the above into homogeneous almost complex structures using the correspondence reviewed in
Section 3.2
.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
J\in{J}_{h}(V)
.
Let
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
be holomorphic.
The following hold:

(1)

\pi^{*}V\subset\tilde{C}\times V
is an almost complex submanifold, and

\pi^{*}J\coloneqq\lparen\tilde{j}\times J\rparen|_{\pi^{*}V}
is homogeneous.

(2)

{\mathfrak{d}}_{\pi^{*}J}=\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}_{J}
.

Proof.

Since
p
is
J
–holomorphic and
\pi
is holomorphic,

\pi^{*}V\subset\tilde{C}\times V
is an almost complex submanifold.
Since
J
is homogeneous, so is
\pi^{*}J
.
This proves
(1)
.

Let
s\in\Gamma(C,V)
.
Inspection of the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

reveals that

Tp^{*}\pi\circ\lparen T\pi^{*}s-\pi^{*}J\circ T\pi^{*}s\circ\tilde{j}\rparen=\lparen Ts-J\circ Ts\circ j\rparen\circ T\pi.

This proves
(2)
.
∎

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
J\in{J}_{h}(V)
.
Set
{\mathfrak{d}}\coloneqq{\mathfrak{d}}_{J}
.
The following hold:

(1)

If
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
is holomorphic and
s\in\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}
,
then
u\coloneqq(p^{*}\pi)\circ s\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
is
J
–holomorphic.

(2)

If
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
is
J
–holomorphic,
then
\pi\coloneqq p\circ u
is holomorphic and

s\coloneqq(\mathrm{id}_{\tilde{C}},u)\in\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}
.

The above directly leads to the third perspective,
since
J
–holomorphic maps induce
J
–holomorphic cycles.
This is spelled out in detail in
[
DW21
, §4,§5]
.
Here is a brief summary.

Definition 3.21
.

Let
X
be a smooth manifold equipped with
an almost complex structure
J
,
a Hermitian form
\sigma
, and
the Riemannian metric
g\coloneqq\sigma(\cdot,J\cdot)
.
A
J
–holomorphic cycle
is
a closed integral
2
–current
T\in\Hom\lparen\Omega_{c}^{2}(X),\mathbf{R}\rparen
which is semi-calibrated by
\sigma
and has finite mass:
{\mathbf{M}}(T)<\infty
.

3.22
.

The choice of a Hermitian form
\sigma
is somewhat of a red herring:
if
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}
are Hermitian forms (with respect to
J
),
then
T\in\Hom\lparen\Omega_{c}^{2}(X),\mathbf{R}\rparen
is a closed integral current which is semi-calibrated by
\sigma
if and only if the same holds for
\sigma^{\prime}
.
However, the mass
{\mathbf{M}}(T)
depends on
\sigma
.

3.23
.

A
J
–holomorphic cycle
T
with compact support
\supp(T)
represents a homology class
[T]\in{\rm H}_{2}(X,\mathbf{Z})
.
If
X
is the total space of
V
,
then
{\rm H}_{2}(V,\mathbf{Z})\cong{\rm H}_{2}(C,\mathbf{Z})\cong\mathbf{Z}
is generated by
[0]
, the fundamental class of the zero section.
Therefore,
[T]
is determined by

\deg(T)\coloneqq[T]/[0]\in\mathbf{Z}.

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
X
be a smooth manifold equipped with an almost complex structure
J
and a Hermitian form
\sigma
.
If
C
is a not necessarily closed Riemann surface and
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C\to X
is a proper
J
–holomorphic map,
then the closed integral
2
–current
T_{u}\in\Hom\lparen\Omega_{c}^{2}(X),\mathbf{R}\rparen
defined by

T_{u}(\alpha)\coloneqq\int_{C}u^{*}\alpha

is a
J
–holomorphic cycle with

{\mathbf{M}}(T_{u})=T_{u}(\sigma)\quad\text{and}\quad\supp(T_{u})=\im u;

moreover,
if
C
is closed,
then
[T_{u}]=u_{*}[C]
.
∎

3.25
[
DSS17a
,
DSS18
,
DSS17
,
DSS20
]
; cf.
[
DW21
, Lemma 5.5]
.

Let
X
be a smooth manifold equipped with an almost complex structure
J
and a Hermitian form
\sigma
.
If
T
is a
J
–holomorphic cycle,
then there are a not necessarily closed Riemann surface
C
and a proper
J
–holomorphic map
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C\to X
with
T=T_{u}
;
moreover: if
\supp T
is compact, then
C
is closed.
∎

Here is the fourth, more involved, perspective on
2
–rigidity.
It is based on the following concept.

Definition 3.26
.

A
ramified Euclidean line bundle
over
C

consists of:

(1)

a finite subset
\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\subset C
, the
branching locus
, and

(2)

a Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}
over
\mathring{C}\coloneqq C{\backslash}\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})

such that:

(3)

the monodromy representation
\mu\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\pi_{1}(\mathring{C},*)\to\mathrm{O}(1)=\{\pm 1\}
restricts to a non-trivial homomorphism on

\ker\lparen\pi_{1}(\mathring{C},*)\to\pi_{1}(\mathring{C}\cup\{b\},*)\rparen\cong\mathbf{Z}

for every
b\in\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
.

3.27
.

By Seifert–van Kampen,

\pi_{1}(\mathring{C},*)\cong\langle\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{g},\beta_{g},x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\mid[\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}]\cdots[\alpha_{g},\beta_{g}]=x_{1}\cdots x_{k}\rangle

with
g
denoting the genus of
C
and
k\coloneqq\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
.
In particular,

(3)
enforces that
\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
is even.

Definition 3.28
.

Two ramified Euclidean line bundles
{\mathfrak{l}}_{1},{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}
over
C
are
isomorphic
if
\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{1})=\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{2})
and
{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}\cong{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}
as Euclidean line bundles over
\mathring{C}
.

3.29
.

If
{\mathfrak{l}}_{1},{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}
are Euclidean line bundles over
\mathring{C}
,
then they are isomorphic if and only if their monodromy representations

\mu({\mathfrak{l}}_{i})\in\Hom(\pi_{1}(\mathring{C},*),\{\pm 1\})

or, equivalently, their first Stiefel–Whitney classes

w_{1}({\mathfrak{l}}_{i})\in{\rm H}^{1}(\mathring{C},\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})

agree.

Proposition 3.30
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
.
Let
{\mathfrak{l}}
be a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
.
There is a unique real Cauchy–Riemann operator

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})\to\Omega^{0,1}(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})

on
V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}
over
\mathring{C}
,
the
twist
of
{\mathfrak{d}}
by
{\mathfrak{l}}
,
satisfying

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}(s\otimes t)={\mathfrak{d}}s\otimes t+(s\otimes\nabla t)^{0,1}

for every
s\in\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\rparen
and
t\in\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
.
Here
\nabla
denotes the unique orthogonal covariant derivative on
{\mathfrak{l}}
.

Proof.

By locality, it suffices to consider
{\mathfrak{l}}={\underline{\mathbf{R}}}
.
In this case
V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}=V
and
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}={\mathfrak{d}}
.
∎

3.31
.

Evidently,
the restriction of
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
to
\Gamma_{c}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
extends to a bounded linear operator

(3.32)

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen.

Here
W^{1,2}
and
L^{2}
are with respect to (immaterial) choices of
a Riemannian metric on
C
,
a Hermitian inner product and a unitary covariant derivative on
V
; and
the Euclidean inner product and unique orthogonal covariant derivative on
{\mathfrak{l}}
.
In fact,
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
is Fredholm; cf.
Section 3.4.2
.

Proposition 3.33
.

If
{\mathfrak{l}}
is a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
,
then the following hold:

(1)

The smooth map

\mathring{\pi}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathring{\tilde{C}}\coloneqq\{(x,v)\in{\mathfrak{l}}:\lvert v\rvert=1\}\to\mathring{C}

is a
\{\pm 1\}
–principal covering map, and

{\mathfrak{l}}\cong\mathring{\tilde{C}}\times_{\{\pm 1\}}\mathbf{R}
.

(2)

The covering map
\mathring{\pi}
extends to a holomorphic map
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
(unique up to unique biholomorphism).

Moreover,
every holomorphic map
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
of degree two arises in this way.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{l}}
be a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
.

(1)
is obvious.
To verify
(2)
it suffices to consider the following ramified Euclidean line bundle over
D\coloneqq\{z\in{\mathbf{C}}:\lvert z\rvert<1\}
:

(3.34)

{\mathfrak{m}}\coloneqq\{(z,w)\in\mathring{D}\times{\mathbf{C}}:w^{2}\in\mathbf{R}_{\geqslant 0}\cdot z\}\to\mathring{D}\coloneqq D{\backslash}\{0\}

with the Euclidean inner product

\langle(z,w_{1}),(z,w_{2})\rangle\coloneqq w_{1}w_{2}z^{-1}
.
For
{\mathfrak{l}}={\mathfrak{m}}
,

\mathring{\tilde{C}}=\{(z,w)\in\mathring{D}\times\mathring{D}:w^{2}=z\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathring{\pi}(z,w)=w^{2}.

Since
\mathring{\tilde{C}}\cong\mathring{D}
via
(z,w)\mapsto w
,
the double cover
\mathring{\pi}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathring{\tilde{C}}\cong\mathring{D}\to\mathring{D}
extends to the holomorphic map
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace D\to D,w\mapsto w^{2}
.

Let
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
be a holomorphic map of degree two.
Denote by
\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\subset C
the set of critical values of
\pi
.
Set
\mathring{C}\coloneqq C{\backslash}\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
and
\mathring{\tilde{C}}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(\mathring{C})
.
The restriction
\mathring{\pi}\coloneqq\pi|_{\mathring{\tilde{C}}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathring{\tilde{C}}\to\mathring{C}
is a double covering;
hence, a
\{\pm 1\}
–principal covering map.
The associated Euclidean line bundle

{\mathfrak{l}}\coloneqq\lparen\mathring{\tilde{C}}\times\mathbf{R}\rparen/\{\pm 1\}

is a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
with branching locus
\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
.
Evidently, the above construction applied to
{\mathfrak{l}}
yields
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
.
∎

3.35
.

Assume the situation of
3.33
.
Let
b\in\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
.
The Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}
determines a square root of
T_{b}^{*}C
:

(1)

Set
\{\tilde{b}\}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(b)
.
Denote by
{O}_{b}
and
{O}_{\tilde{b}}
the local ring of germs of holomorphic functions at
b\in C
and
\tilde{b}\in\tilde{C}
respectively.
Denote by
{\mathfrak{m}}_{b}\subset{O}_{b}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\tilde{b}}\subset{O}_{\tilde{b}}
the maximal ideals (of germs of holomorphic functions vanishing at
b\in C
and
\tilde{b}\in\tilde{C}
respectively).
The holomorphic map
\pi
induces an injective ring homomorphism
\pi^{*}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{b}\hookrightarrow{O}_{\tilde{b}}
satisfying

\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{m}}_{b}={\mathfrak{m}}_{\tilde{b}}^{2}
.
Therefore,

T_{b}^{*}C={\mathfrak{m}}_{b}\otimes_{{O}_{b}}\lparen{O}_{b}/{\mathfrak{m}}_{b}\rparen=\frac{{\mathfrak{m}}_{b}}{{\mathfrak{m}}_{b}^{2}}\cong\Big\lparen\frac{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\tilde{b}}}{{\mathfrak{m}}_{\tilde{b}}^{2}}\Big\rparen^{\otimes 2}=\lparen T_{\tilde{b}}^{*}\tilde{C}\rparen^{\otimes 2}.

(2)

Set

({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}(-b))_{b}\coloneqq\varinjlim_{U\ni b}L^{\infty}{\rm H}^{0}\lparen U{\backslash}\{b\},{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\rparen

with
L^{\infty}{\rm H}^{0}
denoting bounded holomorphic sections.
(In the situation discussed in the proof of
3.33
,
such a section has an asymptotic expansion

a_{0}z^{1/2}+a_{1}z^{3/2}+\ldots
at
0
.)

({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}(-b))_{b}
is an
{O}_{b}
–module.
Define the
\tfrac{1}{2}
–jet space of
{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}
at
b
by

J_{b}^{1/2}({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})\coloneqq({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}(-b))_{b}\otimes_{{O}_{b}}\lparen{O}_{b}/{\mathfrak{m}}_{b}\rparen.

The canonical isomorphism
{\mathfrak{l}}^{\otimes 2}\cong{\underline{\mathbf{R}}}
induces a canonical isomorphism
({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}(-b))_{b}^{\otimes 2}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}_{b}
.
Therefore,
\lparen J_{b}^{1/2}({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})\rparen^{\otimes 2}\cong T_{b}^{*}C
.

Of course, these constructions are related.
The holomorphic map
\pi
induces an
{O}_{b}
–module isomorphism
\pi^{*}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\lparen{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\rparen_{b}\cong{\mathfrak{m}}_{\tilde{b}}
;
therefore:

J_{b}^{1/2}({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})\cong T_{\tilde{b}}\tilde{C}.

3.36
.

The notation
({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}(-b))_{b}
is motivated by
A.2
.

Proposition 3.37
.

Assume the situation of
3.33
.
Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
.
The following hold:

(1)

The pull-back
\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}
is
\{\pm 1\}
–equivariant.
In particular,

\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}=(\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}})^{+}\oplus(\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}})^{-}

with
(\cdot)^{+}
and
(\cdot)^{-}
denoting the invariant and anti-invariant subspaces respectively.

(2)

The isomorphism
\pi^{*}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Gamma(C,V)\cong\Gamma\lparen\tilde{C},\pi^{*}V\rparen^{+}
induces an isomorphism

\pi^{*}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\ker{\mathfrak{d}}\cong\lparen\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}\rparen^{+}.

(3)

The inclusion
\mathring{\pi}_{!}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Gamma\lparen\tilde{C},\pi^{*}V\rparen^{-}\hookrightarrow W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
induces an isomorphism

\mathring{\pi}_{!}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace(\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}})^{-}\cong\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}.

Here (and in the following)
\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
denotes the kernel of the extension
(3.32)
.

Proof.

(1)
and
(2)
are immediate from
3.16
.

A moment’s thought shows that
if
s\in(\ker{\mathfrak{d}})^{-}
,
then its descend
\mathring{\pi}_{!}s\in W^{1,2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
and satisfies
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mathring{\pi}_{!}s=0
;
therefore,
\mathring{\pi}_{!}s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
.
Hence,
\mathring{\pi}_{!}
induces an inclusion
\mathring{\pi}_{!}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace(\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}})^{-}\hookrightarrow\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
.

Let
s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
.
By elliptic regularity,

s\in\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
;
indeed, for
B_{R}(x)\subset\mathring{C}
with
R\ll 1

\lvert s\rvert^{2}(x)\lesssim R^{-2}\int_{B_{R}(x)}\lvert s\rvert^{2}.

It follows from
3.26

(3)
that
if
B_{R}(b)\subset C
with
B_{R}(b)\cap\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})=\{b\}
and
R\ll 1
,
then

R^{-2}\int_{\partial B_{R}(b)}\lvert s\rvert^{2}\lesssim\int_{\partial B_{R}(b)}\lvert\nabla s\rvert^{2},

and, therefore,

(3.38)

\int_{B_{R}(b)}d(\cdot,b)^{-2}\lvert s\rvert^{2}\lesssim\int_{B_{R}(b)}\lvert\nabla s\rvert^{2}.

Consequently,
s\in L^{\infty}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
.

Evidently,
\mathring{\pi}^{*}s
satisfies
(\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}})(\mathring{\pi}^{*}s)=0
on
\mathring{\tilde{C}}
.
Since
\mathring{\pi}^{*}s\in L^{\infty}\Gamma(\tilde{C},\pi^{*}V)
, it also satisfies
(\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}})(\mathring{\pi}^{*}s)=0
in the sense of distributions.
Therefore, by elliptic regularity,
\mathring{\pi}^{*}s\in\lparen\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}\rparen^{-}
.
This finishes the proof of
(3)
.
∎

3.39
.

As a consequence of
(3.38)
,

W^{1,2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})=W_{0}^{1,2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
.

To summarise the above discussion,
the following four are equivalent pieces of evidence for the failure of
2
–rigidity of a real Cauchy–Riemann operator
{\mathfrak{d}}={\mathfrak{d}}_{J}
on
V
:

(1)

A closed Riemann surface
\tilde{C}
, a holomorphic map
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
of degree at most two, and
s\in\ker\pi^{*}{\mathfrak{d}}{\backslash}\{0\}
.

(2)

A closed Riemann surface
\tilde{C}
and a
J
–holomorphic map
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
which does not factor through the zero section and with
\pi\circ u
of degree at most two.

(3)

A
J
–holomorphic cycle
T
in
V
whose
support is not contained in the zero section and with
\deg(T)
at most two.

(4)

A ramified Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}
over
C
and
s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}{\backslash}\{0\}
.

3.4
{W}
is a proper wall

Recall the standing assumption that
C
is closed and connected.
Henceforth, assume that

2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)=0.

The aim of the upcoming sections is to prove that
wall of failure of
2
–rigidity
,
defined by

{W}\coloneqq\big\{{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V):{\mathfrak{d}}\textnormal{ fails to be $2$--rigid}\big\},

is a proper wall in the sense of
2.6
.

Of course,
{C}{R}(V)
is not a Banach manifold but an affine space modelled on the Frechet space
\Gamma\lparen C,{H}(V)\rparen
;
see
3.3
.
To rectify this,
choose
K\gg 1
and set

C^{K}{C}{R}(V)\coloneqq{C}{R}(V)+C^{K}\Gamma\lparen C,\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(\overline{TC},{\mathbf{C}})\rparen,

and similarly define
C^{K}{C}{R}(V,\gamma)
and
C^{K}{J}_{h}(V)
.
The results discussed so far
continue to hold with these spaces—with minimal cosmetic modifications.
To ease notation,
henceforth, the prefix
C^{K}
shall be omitted;
in other words,
{C}{R}(V)
,
{C}{R}(V,\gamma)
, and
{J}_{h}(V)
are
redefined
to be the aforementioned spaces.
With this technicality out of the way,
the aim of the remainder of this section is to prove the following.

3.40
.

The wall of failure of
2
–rigidity
{W}
is a proper wall in
{C}{R}(V)
.

The upcoming discussion also establishes the following variant.

3.41
.

If
\gamma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V\cong V^{\dagger}
is an isomorphism,
then
{W}\cap{C}{R}(V,\gamma)
is a proper wall in

{C}{R}(V,\gamma)
.

3.4.1
{W}
is closed

Proposition 3.42
.

The wall of failure of
2
–rigidity
{W}\subset{C}{R}(V)
is closed.

The proof of
3.42
relies on the following.

3.43
[
FF60
, §8]
;
[
Fed69
,
Sim83
,
Whi89
]
.

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian manifold.
Let
(T_{n})
be a sequence of closed integral
k
–currents.
If there is a
\Lambda>0
such that

{\mathbf{M}}(T_{n})\leqslant\Lambda,

then a subsequence of

(T_{n})
converges to a closed integral
k
–current
T_{\infty}
in the weak–
*
–topology.
∎

Proof of
3.42
.

Suppose that
({\mathfrak{d}}_{n})\in{W}^{\mathbf{N}}
converges to
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}\in{C}{R}(V)
.
For
n\in{\mathbf{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
set
J_{n}\coloneqq J_{{\mathfrak{d}}_{n}}
.
Define
\rho^{2}\in C^{\infty}(V)
by
\rho^{2}(v)\coloneqq\lvert v\rvert^{2}
(with respect to the chosen Hermitian metric).
For every
n\in{\mathbf{N}}

choose a
J_{n}
–holomorphic cycle
T_{n}
in
V
whose support is not contained in the zero section and with
\deg(T_{n})
at most two;
and, moreover:

\max_{\supp T_{n}}\rho^{2}=1.

Set
U\coloneqq\{v\in V\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\lvert v\rvert<2\}
.
Choose a Kähler form
\omega_{C}
on
C
.
For
\Lambda\gg 1
,
for every
n\in{\mathbf{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
,

\omega_{n}\coloneqq\Lambda\cdot\omega_{C}-\tfrac{1}{4}{\rm d}({\rm d}\rho^{2}\circ J_{n})\in\Omega^{2}(U)

is symplectic and tames
J_{n}|_{U}
;
that is:
\sigma_{n}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}[\omega_{n}+\omega_{n}(J_{n}\cdot,J_{n}\cdot)]
is a Hermitian form;
cf.
[
DW21
, Proof of Lemma 3.6 (5)]
.
Since
T_{n}
is
J_{n}
–holomorphic,

{\mathbf{M}}(T_{n})=T_{n}(\sigma_{n})
;
therefore,

{\mathbf{M}}(T_{n})=T_{n}(\sigma_{n})=T_{n}(\omega_{n})\leqslant 2\Lambda\langle[C],[\omega_{C}]\rangle.

By
Theorem 3.43
,
after passing to a subsequence,

(T_{n})
converges to a closed integral
2
–current
T_{\infty}
in the weak–
*
–topology.
Since
J_{n}\to J_{\infty}
(and
\sigma_{n}\to\sigma_{\infty}
),

T_{\infty}
is a
J_{\infty}
–holomorphic cycle.
Moreover,
T_{\infty}
is of degree

\deg(T_{\infty})=T_{\infty}(\omega_{C})=\lim_{n\to\infty}T_{n}(\omega_{C})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\deg(T_{n})

at most two.
By the monotonicity formula (cf.
[
DW21
, Lemma 5.6]
),

(\supp T_{n})
converges to
\supp T_{\infty}
in the Hausdorff distance;
hence: the support of
T_{\infty}
is not contained in the zero section.
Therefore,
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}\in{W}
.
∎

3.4.2
Fredholm extensions and index formulae

This section provides an elementary and self-contained proof of the following result and its higher regularity analogue
Theorem 3.58
.
Of course,
these results could be derived from
[
Wen19
, Theorem 4.1]
or
[
DW23a
, Proposition 2.8.6]
.
They are needed in the construction of the Fredholm maps
\pi
,
\nu
, and
\sigma
in
2.6
.

3.44
.

If

{\mathfrak{d}}
is a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
and

{\mathfrak{l}}
is a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
,
then

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
is Fredholm with

\ind{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}=-\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V.

3.45
.

Theorem 3.44
assumes
2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)=0
.
The operator
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
continues to be Fredholm if this assumption is dropped,
but
\ind{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}=2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)-\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V
; see
Theorem A.1
.

Throughout the remainder of the section,
assume the situation of
Theorem 3.44
.

Proposition 3.46
.

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
is left semi-Fredholm; that is:

\dim\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}<\infty
and
\im{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\subset L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}(C,V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
is closed.

Proof.

If
\nabla
is the unitary covariant derivative on
V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}
from
3.30
,
then, by the Kähler identities,

2(\nabla^{0,1})^{*}\nabla^{0,1}=\nabla^{*}\nabla-i\Lambda F_{\nabla}.

Therefore,

\|\nabla s\|^{2}=2\|\nabla^{0,1}s\|_{L^{2}}^{2}-\langle i\Lambda F_{\nabla}s,s\rangle_{L^{2}}

for every
s\in W_{0}^{1,2}(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
.
Since
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}=\nabla^{0,1}+{\mathfrak{a}}
with
{\mathfrak{a}}\in L^{\infty}\Gamma(\mathring{C},{H}(V))
,

\|s\|_{W^{1,2}}\lesssim\|{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s\|_{L^{2}}+\|s\|_{L^{2}}.

By Rellich’s Theorem and
(3.38)
,

W_{0}^{1,2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})\hookrightarrow L^{2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
is compact.
Therefore, by
[
BS18
, Lemma 4.3.9]
,
it follows that
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
is left-semi-Fredholm.
∎

Consider the extension

{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace L^{2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})\to W^{-1,2}\Omega^{0,1}(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}).

Because of
3.39
,

{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}
is the adjoint of
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
.
In particular,

\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}\cong\lparen\coker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen^{*}.

Therefore, it remains to prove that
\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}
is finite-dimensional, and
to determine
\ind{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}=\dim\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}-\dim\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}
(for a judicious choice of
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V)
).
The key to the former is to understand

\coker(\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\hookrightarrow\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}).

To this end, it is useful to extract the leading order terms at
b\in\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
of elements of the following superspace of
\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
:

\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{\max}^{\mathfrak{l}})\coloneqq\big\{s\in L^{2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}):{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s\in L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})\big\}.

This is, of course, the domain of the maximal extension of
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
considered as an unbounded operator
L^{2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
.

Definition 3.47
.

Let
b\in\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
.
Set

({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})_{b}\coloneqq\varinjlim_{U\ni b}L^{2}{\rm H}^{0}\lparen U{\backslash}\{b\},{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\rparen

with
L^{2}{\rm H}^{0}
denoting
L^{2}
holomorphic sections.
(In the situation discussed in the proof of
3.33
,
such a section has an asymptotic expansion

a_{0}z^{-1/2}+a_{1}z^{1/2}+\ldots
at
0
.)

({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})_{b}
is an
{O}_{b}
–module.
The
\tfrac{1}{2}
–residue space of
{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}
at
b
is

{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}({\mathfrak{l}})\coloneqq({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})_{b}\otimes_{{O}_{b}}\lparen{O}_{b}/{\mathfrak{m}}_{b}\rparen.

3.48
.

The pairing
({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}(-b))_{b}\otimes_{{O}_{b}}({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})_{b}\to{O}_{b}
induces an isomorphism

{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}({\mathfrak{l}})\cong\lparen J_{b}^{1/2}({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})\rparen^{*}
.

Proposition 3.49
.

Let
b\in\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
.
There is a unique linear map

\Res_{b}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{\max}^{\mathfrak{l}})\to{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}({\mathfrak{l}},V)\coloneqq{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}({\mathfrak{l}})\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}V_{b},

the
residue
at
b
,
such that the following holds:
if

s\in\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{\max}^{\mathfrak{l}})
,

\Res_{b}(s)=[\rho]\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}v

with
\rho\in L^{2}{\rm H}^{0}\lparen\mathring{U},{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\rparen
and
v\in V_{b}
, and

\tau\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace U\times V_{b}\to V|_{U}
is an isometry with
\tau_{b}=\mathrm{id}_{V_{b}}
,
then

s-\tau_{*}v\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\rho\in W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{U},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen.

The proof reduces to the following model case.

Proposition 3.50
.

Consider the ramified Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{m}}
over
D
defined in
(3.34)
.
Choose
z^{-1/2}\in\Gamma(\mathring{D},{\mathbf{C}}\otimes{\mathfrak{m}})
.
If
s\in L^{2}\Gamma(\mathring{D},{\mathbf{C}}^{r}\otimes{\mathfrak{m}})
satisfies

\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{m}}s\in L^{2}\Gamma(\mathring{D},{\mathbf{C}}^{r}\otimes{\mathfrak{m}})
,
then there is a unique
v\in{\mathbf{C}}^{r}
such that

s-v\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}z^{-1/2}\in W^{1,2}\lparen\tfrac{1}{2}\mathring{D},{\mathbf{C}}^{r}\otimes{\mathfrak{m}}\rparen.

Here
\tfrac{1}{2}\mathring{D}\coloneqq\{z\in{\mathbf{C}}:0<\lvert z\rvert<1/2\}
.

The proof relies on the following elementary observation.

Lemma 3.51
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
.
Let
\phi\in L^{2}((0,1),r{\rm d}r)
with
\lparen\partial_{r}-\lambda/r\rparen\phi\in L^{2}((0,1),r{\rm d}r)
.

(1)

If
\lambda=-1/2
,
then there is a unique
a\in\mathbf{R}
such that

\phi(r)=ar^{-1/2}+o(1)
as
r\downarrow 0
.

(2)

If
\lambda\neq-1/2
,
then
\phi(r)=o(1)
as
r\downarrow 0
.

Proof.

Set
\psi\coloneqq(\partial_{r}-\lambda/r)\phi
.
There is a unique
a\in\mathbf{R}
such that

\tilde{\phi}(r)\coloneqq\phi(r)-ar^{\lambda}=\begin{cases}r^{\lambda}\int_{0}^{r}s^{-(\lambda+1)}\psi(s)\,s{\rm d}s&\text{if}\penalty\ \lambda<0\\
-r^{\lambda}\int_{r}^{1}s^{-(\lambda+1)}\psi(s)\,s{\rm d}s&\text{if}\penalty\ \lambda>0.\end{cases}

If
\lambda<0
,
then, by Cauchy–Schwarz and monotone convergence,

\lvert\tilde{\phi}(r)\rvert^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2\lvert\lambda\rvert}\int_{0}^{r}\lvert\psi(s)\rvert^{2}\,s{\rm d}s=o(1).

Moreover,
if
\lambda\leqslant-1
,
then
a=0
because
\phi\in L^{2}((0,1),r{\rm d}r)
.

If
\lambda>0
, then,
by Cauchy–Schwarz,
for
r\leqslant\varepsilon\leqslant 1

\lvert\tilde{\phi}(r)\rvert^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}\lvert\psi(s)\rvert^{2}\,s{\rm d}s+\frac{(r/\varepsilon)^{2\lambda}}{\lambda}\int_{\varepsilon}^{1}\lvert\psi(s)\rvert^{2}\,s{\rm d}s\eqqcolon{\rm I}(\varepsilon)+{\rm II}(r,\varepsilon).

By monotone convergence,

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}{\rm I}(\varepsilon)=0
.
Evidently,

\lim_{r\downarrow 0}{\rm II}(r,\varepsilon)=0
.
Therefore,
\tilde{\phi}(r)=o(1)
as
r\downarrow 0
.
∎

Proof of
3.50
.

In polar coordinates
z=re^{i\alpha}
,

\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{m}}f=(\partial_{r}+ir^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{m}})f\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}({\rm d}r-ir\cdot{\rm d}\alpha).

By Fubini’s theorem,

L^{2}\Gamma(\mathring{D},{\mathbf{C}}^{r}\otimes{\mathfrak{m}})=L^{2}((0,1),r{\rm d}r;L^{2}\Gamma(S^{1},{\mathbf{C}}^{r}\otimes{\mathfrak{m}})).

By Fourier analysis,

s=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}s_{\lambda}(r)e^{i\lambda\alpha}

with
s_{\lambda},(\partial_{r}+\lambda/r)s_{\lambda}\in L^{2}\lparen(0,1),r{\rm d}r;{\mathbf{C}}^{r}\rparen
.

By
3.51
,

s_{-1/2}=vr^{-1/2}+o(1),

and
s_{\lambda}=o(1)
for every
\lambda\neq-\frac{1}{2}
.
Since
v\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}z^{-1/2}\in\ker\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{m}}
,

s
may be replaced by
s-v\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}z^{-1/2}
.
Therefore,
without loss of generality
s_{\lambda}(0)=s_{\lambda}(1)=0
for every
\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
.

It remains to prove that
s\in W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{D},{\mathbf{C}}^{r}\otimes{\mathfrak{m}}\rparen
.
By direct computation,

\displaystyle 2\int_{\mathring{D}}\lvert\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{m}}s\rvert^{2}\,r{\rm d}r{\rm d}\alpha

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\lvert(\partial_{r}+\tfrac{\lambda}{r})s_{\lambda}\rvert^{2}r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\big\lparen\lvert\partial_{r}s_{\lambda}\rvert^{2}r+\tfrac{\lambda^{2}}{r}\lvert s_{\lambda}\rvert^{2}+\lambda\partial_{r}\lvert s_{\lambda}\rvert^{2}\big\rparen{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\lparen\lvert\partial_{r}s\rvert^{2}+\lvert\nabla_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{m}}s\rvert^{2}\rparen\,r{\rm d}r

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathring{D}}\lvert\nabla^{\mathfrak{m}}s\rvert^{2}\,r{\rm d}r{\rm d}\alpha.\qed

Proof of
3.49
.

Since
{\mathfrak{d}}=\bar{\partial}+{\mathfrak{a}}
with
\lvert{\mathfrak{a}}\rvert\in L^{\infty}(C)
,
the assertion follows from
3.50
.
∎

The maps
\Res_{b}
constructed in
3.49
assemble into

\Res\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{\max}^{\mathfrak{l}})\to{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{b\in B}.{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}({\mathfrak{l}},V)

The following is immediate from the construction.

Corollary 3.52
.

The sequence

W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\hookrightarrow\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Res}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)

is exact.
In particular,

\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}=\ker\lparen\Res\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)\rparen.

Proof of
Theorem 3.44
: Fredholm property.

By
3.46
and the discussion following its proof,
to prove that
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
is Fredholm
it remains to establish that
\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}
is finite-dimensional.
This, however, is a consequence of
3.52
since
{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)
and
\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}
are finite-dimensional.
∎

The proof of the index formula assuming
V\cong V^{\dagger}
relies on the following.

Proposition 3.53
.

Let
\gamma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V\cong V^{\dagger}
be an isomorphism.

(1)

There is a non-degenerate alternating form
G\in\Hom(\Lambda^{2}{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V),\mathbf{R})
on
{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)
,
the
Green’s form
,
such that

\int_{C}\operatorname{Re}\langle\lparen\gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}\rparen\circ{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s,t\rangle-\operatorname{Re}\langle s,\lparen\gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}\rparen\circ{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}t\rangle=G(\Res(s),\Res(t))

for every
s,t\in\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}})
.

(2)

The subspace
\im\lparen\Res\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)\rparen\subset{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)
is Lagrangian with respect to
G
.

Proof.

The unbounded operator

\lparen\gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}\rparen\circ{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\subset L^{2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V^{\dagger}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen

is symmetric (with respect to the perfect pairings induced by
(3.5)
) and closed.
Its maximal extension is

\lparen\gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}\rparen\circ{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}})\subset L^{2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V^{\dagger}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
.

By
[
MS98
, Exercise 2.17]
,
the
Green’s form

\hat{G}\in\Hom(\Lambda^{2}\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}),\mathbf{R})
defined by

\hat{G}(s,t)\coloneqq\int_{C}\operatorname{Re}\langle\lparen\gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}\rparen\circ{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s,t\rangle-\operatorname{Re}\langle s,\lparen\gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}\rparen\circ{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}t\rangle

descends to a symplectic form on the
Gelfand–Robbin quotient

{\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}})}/{W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Res}}{{\cong}}{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V).

This proves
(1)
.

It immedately follows that
\im\lparen\Res\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)\rparen
is isotropic.
To prove that it is coisotropic,
let
v\in{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)
such that
G(v,w)=0

for every
w\in\im\lparen\Res\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)\rparen
.
Construct
s_{0}\in\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}})
with
\Res(s_{0})=v
.
Since

\hat{G}(s_{0},t)=\int_{C}\operatorname{Re}\langle\lparen\gamma\otimes\mathbf{1}\rparen\circ{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s_{0},t\rangle=0

for every
t\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
,

{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s_{0}\in\im{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
.
Choose
s_{1}\in W_{0}^{1,2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
with
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s_{1}=-{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s_{0}
.
Finally,

s\coloneqq s_{0}+s_{1}\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
and
\Res(s)=\Res(s_{0})=v
.
This proves
(2)
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 3.44
: index formula.

Choose
\gamma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V\cong V^{\dagger}
.
By
3.3
and
3.9
,
it suffices to prove the index formula for
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V,\gamma)
.
In this case,

\displaystyle\ind{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}

\displaystyle=\dim\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}-\dim\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}

\displaystyle=-\dim\im\lparen\Res\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)\rparen=-\frac{1}{2}\dim{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{l}},V)=-\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V

by
3.53
.
∎

For parts of the discussion in
Section 3.4.6

a higher regularity version of
Theorem 3.44
is necessary.
It turns out that the correct generalisation of
W^{1,2}
is not
W^{k,2}
but instead the following variant.

Definition 3.54
.

Denote by
r\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathring{C}\to(0,\infty)
the distance to
\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
.
Set
g_{{\rm{cyl}}}\coloneqq r^{-2}g
.
Let
k\in{\mathbf{N}}
.
For
s\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{k,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
set

\|s\|_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}}\coloneqq\left\lparen\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\int_{\mathring{C}}\lvert\nabla^{\ell}s\rvert_{g_{\rm{cyl}}}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g_{\rm{cyl}}}\right\rparen^{1/2}.

This defines a complete norm on

W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\coloneqq\left\{s\in W_{\mathrm{loc}}^{k,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen:\|s\|_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}}<\infty\right\}.

Similarly, define
W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
using the Euclidean inner product on
\overline{K}_{C}
induced by
g_{\rm{cyl}}
.

3.55
.

If
r=e^{-t}
, then

{\rm d}r^{2}+r^{2}{\rm d}\alpha^{2}=r^{2}({\rm d}t^{2}+{\rm d}\alpha^{2}).

Therefore, with respect to
g_{\rm{cyl}}
every puncture
b\in\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
corresponds to an asymptotically cylindrical end of
\mathring{C}

The following is an immediate consequence of
(3.38)
and conformal invariance.

Proposition 3.56
.

W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen=W_{\rm{cyl}}^{1,2}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen

and

L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen=L_{\rm{cyl}}^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
.
∎

The bounded operator
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
(co)restricts to a bounded operator

{\mathfrak{d}}_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\to W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen.

Proposition 3.57
.

For every
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}

if
s\in L^{2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
,

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s\in W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
, and

\Res(s)=0
,
then

s\in W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
and

\|s\|_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}\lesssim_{k}\|{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s\|_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}}+\|s\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

For
k=0
,
this is a consequence of
3.49
;
in particular:

\|s\|_{L_{\rm{cyl}}^{2}}\lesssim\|{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s\|_{L_{\rm{cyl}}^{2}}+\|s\|_{L^{2}}.

By interior elliptic regularity and estimates,
if
s\in L_{\rm{cyl}}^{2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
and

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s\in W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
,
then
s\in W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
and

\|s\|_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}\lesssim_{k}\|{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s\|_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}}+\|s\|_{L_{\rm{cyl}}^{2}}.

This proves the assertion.
∎

3.58
.

Let
k\in\{0,\ldots,K\}
.
If

{\mathfrak{d}}
is a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
and

{\mathfrak{l}}
is a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
,
then

{\mathfrak{d}}_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
is Fredholm with

\ind{\mathfrak{d}}_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}=-\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V;

moreover,

\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}=\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}

and the map

\coker{\mathfrak{d}}_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\to\coker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\cong\lparen\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}\rparen^{*}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

By
3.57
,

\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}=\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
;
moreover:
the linear map

\frac{W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen}{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen}\to\frac{L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen}{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen}

induced by
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
is injective.
Therefore, by the Snake Lemma,
the canonical map

\coker{\mathfrak{d}}_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\to\coker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}

is injective.

Since
W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
is dense in
L^{2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
,
the map

W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\to\lparen\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}\rparen^{*}

is surjective.
Since it factors through
\coker{\mathfrak{d}}_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\to\coker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
,
the latter must be surjective.
∎

3.4.3
The space of ramified Euclidean line bundles

The following preparation is needed in the construction of the Banach manifolds
{E}
,
{N}
, and
{S}
which appear in
2.6
.

Definition 3.59
.

The
space of ramified Euclidean line bundles
over
C
is constructed as follows:

(1)

The
configuration space
is the smooth manifold

\mathrm{Conf}(C)\coloneqq\coprod_{k\in{\mathbf{N}}_{0}}\mathrm{Conf}_{k}(C)\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{Conf}_{k}(C)\coloneqq(C^{k}{\backslash}\Delta_{k})/S_{k}

and
\Delta_{k}\coloneqq\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in C^{k}:\#\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}<k\}
.
Identify
[x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}]\in\mathrm{Conf}(C)
with
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\}
, and
regard
\mathrm{Conf}(C)
as the space of finite subsets of
C
.

(2)

Denote by

\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)

the
set
of equivalence classes of ramified Euclidean line bundles over
C
.
Consider the map
\mathrm{Br}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)\to\mathrm{Conf}(C)
.

(3)

For every open subset
U\subset C
,
denote by
{U}\subset\mathrm{Conf}(C)
the open subset of those
B\subset U
such that
{\rm H}^{1}(C{\backslash}U,\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})\to{\rm H}^{1}(C{\backslash}B,\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})
is an isomorphism.
The monodromy representation defines an injection
\tau_{U}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathrm{Br}^{-1}({U})\hookrightarrow{U}\times{\rm H}^{1}(C{\backslash}U,\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})
;
cf.
3.27
.

(4)

Equip
\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)
with the coarsest
topology
with respect to which the maps
\tau_{U}
are continuous.

Proposition 3.60
.

The map
\mathrm{Br}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)\to\mathrm{Conf}(C)
is a covering map with finite fibers.
In particular,
\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)
inherits the structure of a smooth manifold from
\mathrm{Conf}(C)
.

Proof.

To prove that
\mathrm{Br}
is a covering map it suffices to verify that the transition maps
