\tau_{U}^{-1}\circ\tau_{V}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace({U}\cap{V})\times{\rm H}^{1}(C{\backslash}U,\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})\to({U}\cap{V})\times{\rm H}^{1}(C{\backslash}V,\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})
are continuous.
This is an easy exercise;
cf.
[
tDie08
, Proof of (3.3.2) Theorem]
.

Since
\mathrm{Br}^{-1}(B)
is in bijection with a subset the finite set
{\rm H}_{1}(C{\backslash}B,\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z})
,
\mathrm{Br}
has finite fibers.
∎

Proposition 3.61
.

\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)
carries a
twisted universal ramified Euclidean line bundle
in the following sense:

(1)

Consider the
universal punctured curve

\mathring{{C}}\coloneqq\{(B,x)\in\mathrm{Conf}(C)\times C:x\notin B\}.

The projection map

P\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathring{}{C}\to\mathrm{Conf}(C)

is a fibre bundle.

(2)

Denote by
\{{U}_{i}:i\in I\}
the set of open subsets
{U}\subset\mathrm{Conf}(C)
such that
P|_{{U}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathring{{C}}|_{U}\to{U}
is trivial.
Set
{V}_{i}\coloneqq\mathrm{Br}^{-1}({U})
.
Consider
\mathrm{Br}^{*}P\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathrm{Br}^{*}\mathring{{C}}\to\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)
.

For every
i\in I
there are a Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{L}}_{i}
over
\mathrm{Br}^{*}\mathring{{C}}|_{{V}_{i}}
and for every ramified Euclidean local system
{\mathfrak{l}}
with
\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\in{U}_{i}
an isomorphism

{\mathfrak{L}}_{i}|_{[{\mathfrak{l}}]\times\lparen C{\backslash}\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\rparen}\cong{\mathfrak{l}}

unique up to
\Aut({\mathfrak{l}})=\{\pm 1\}
.

(3)

There are a Čech
2
–cocycle
\lambda\in\check{\rm C}^{2}(\{{V}_{i}:i\in I\},\underline{\{\pm 1\}})

and, for every
i,j\in I
, an isomorphism

\phi_{j}^{i}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{L}}_{i}|_{\mathrm{Br}^{*}\mathring{{C}}|_{{V}_{i}\cap{V}_{j}}}\cong{\mathfrak{L}}_{j}|_{\mathrm{Br}^{*}\mathring{{C}}|_{{V}_{i}\cap{V}_{j}}}

such that

(\mathrm{Br}^{*}P)^{*}\lambda_{ijk}\coloneqq\phi_{i}^{k}\phi_{k}^{j}\phi_{j}^{i}\in C^{0}(\mathrm{Br}^{*}\mathring{{C}}|_{{V}_{i}\cap{V}_{j}\cap{V}_{k}},\{\pm 1\}).

Proof.

(1)
is an easy exercise.

(2)
is trivial,
and so is the existence of the isomorphisms in
(3)
.
A priori, these define a cocycle

\tilde{\lambda}\in\check{\rm C}^{2}(\{\mathrm{Br}^{*}\mathring{{C}}|_{{V}_{i}}:i\in I\},\underline{\{\pm 1\}})
.
However,
since
\mathrm{Br}^{*}P
has connected fibres,
\tilde{\lambda}
descends to
\lambda
.
∎

3.62
.

The use of twisted universal objects goes back (at least to)
[
Cal00
]
.

Of course,
[\lambda]\in\check{\rm H}^{2}\lparen\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C),\underline{\{\pm 1\}}\rparen
is the obstruction to
\{{\mathfrak{L}}_{i}:i\in I\}
gluing to a
universal ramified Euclidean line bundle

{\mathfrak{L}}
over
\mathrm{Br}^{*}\mathring{{C}}
.
If
{\mathfrak{L}}

did exists
,
then it would be straight-forward to construct
Hilbert space bundles
{\mathbf{E}}
,
{\mathbf{F}}
over
\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)
such that

{{\mathbf{E}}}_{[{\mathfrak{l}}]}\cong{\mathbf{E}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}\coloneqq W^{1,2}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}{\mathfrak{l}})\quad\text{and}\quad{{\mathbf{F}}}_{[{\mathfrak{l}}]}\cong{\mathbf{F}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}\coloneqq L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}(\mathring{C},V\otimes_{\mathbf{R}}{\mathfrak{l}}).

These in turn would give rise to
the relative Grassmann bundle
\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})
of
d
–planes in
{\mathbf{E}}_{[{\mathfrak{l}}]}
,
a Banach space bundle
{L}({\mathbf{E}},{\mathbf{F}})
of bounded linear maps from
{\mathbf{E}}_{[{\mathfrak{l}}]}
to
{\mathbf{F}}_{[{\mathfrak{l}}]}
,
etc.
Although
{\mathbf{E}}
,
{\mathbf{F}}

do not exist
in an untwisted sense,
various objects derived from
{\mathbf{E}}
and
{\mathbf{F}}
do.
Indeed,
the construction of these objects over
{V}_{i}
as in
3.61
is straight-forward.
The induced
2
–cocycles measuring the obstruction to gluing are induced by
\lambda
.
Indeed,
by the nature of the objects to be constructed, these cocycles are obtained from
\lambda
via the trivial homomorphism
(\cdot)^{2}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\{\pm 1\}\to\{\pm 1\}
.
Therefore, the obstruction vanishes.
An alternative way to understand this is that the automorphism group
\Aut({\mathfrak{l}})=\{\pm 1\}
acts trivially on
\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}}_{\mathfrak{l}})
,
{L}({\mathbf{E}}_{\mathfrak{l}},{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathfrak{l}})
, etc.

3.4.4
Failure of
2
–rigidity: construction of
\pi

Proposition 3.63
.

Let
d\in\{1,2\}
.
Consider the Grassmann bundle
\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})
over
\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)
.
The following hold:

(1)

There are a Hilbert space bundle
\Hom({S},{\mathbf{F}})\to{C}{R}(V)\times\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})
and
for every
({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])\in{C}{R}(V)\times\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})
canonical isomorphisms

\eta_{({\mathfrak{d}};{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda)}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Hom({S},{\mathbf{F}})_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])}\cong\Hom\lparen\Lambda,{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen.

(2)

\Hom({S},{\mathbf{F}})\to{C}{R}(V)\times\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})
has a smooth section
\mathrm{TwistRes}_{d}
such that

\mathrm{TwistRes}_{d}({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])=\eta_{({\mathfrak{d}};{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda)}^{-1}\lparen{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}|_{\Lambda}\rparen.

(3)

\mathrm{TwistRes}_{d}({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])=0
if and only if
\Lambda\subset\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
.

(4)

\mathrm{TwistRes}_{d}
intersects the zero section transversely;
in particular,

{E}_{d}\coloneqq\mathrm{TwistRes}_{d}^{-1}(0)

is a Banach submanifold.

(5)

The projection map
\pi_{d}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}_{d}\to{C}{R}(V)
is Fredholm of index

\lparen 2-d\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\rparen\cdot\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})-d^{2}

at
({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])
.

The extension of
3.63
to
d\in{\mathbf{N}}
does hold,
but the above is sufficient for the purpose of this article.
The restriction to
d\in\{1,2\}
allows for a drastic simplification of the proof:
it can be based on the following observation instead of the the subtle results on Petri’s condition obtained in
[
Wen19
, Section 5]
.

Proposition 3.64

(cf.
[
Eft16
, Proof of Lemma 4.4]
)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
over
C
.
If
s_{1},s_{2}\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}
are linearly-independent,
then

U\coloneqq\{x\in C:s_{1}(x),s_{2}(x)\penalty\ \textnormal{are linearly-independent}\}

is open and dense.

Proof.

Since
C{\backslash}U\coloneqq\{x\in C:\dim\mathbf{R}\langle s_{1}(x),s_{2}(x)\rangle\leqslant 1\}
is closed,
U
is open.

To prove that
U
is dense,
assume (by contradiction) that
C{\backslash}U
contains a non-empty open subset
O
.
By unique continuation, neither
s_{1}
nor
s_{2}
can vanish on
O
.
Without loss of generality,
s_{1}(x)\neq 0
for every
x\in O
.
Therefore and since
O\subset C{\backslash}U

s_{2}=f\cdot s_{1}
.
Since
{\mathfrak{d}}s_{1}={\mathfrak{d}}s_{2}=0
,

f
is holomorphic.
In fact,
f
is constant because it is
\mathbf{R}
–valued.
By unique continuation,

s_{2}-fs_{1}
vanishes on
C
—a contradiction.
∎

Proof of
3.63
.

(1)
and
(2)
are evident from the discussion in
Section 3.4.3
.

(3)
holds by construction.

(4)
asserts that for every
({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])\in\mathrm{TwistRes}_{d}^{-1}(0)
the composition

T_{\mathfrak{d}}{C}{R}(V)\oplus T_{[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda]}\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})\xrightarrow{T_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])}\mathrm{TwistRes}_{d}}T_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda];0)}\Hom({S},{\mathbf{F}})\twoheadrightarrow\Hom(\Lambda,{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathfrak{l}})

is surjective.
By
3.3
,

T_{\mathfrak{d}}{C}{R}(V)=C^{K}\Gamma(C,{H}(V))
.
Furthermore,

T_{[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda]}\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})
fits into the
short exact sequence

\Hom(\Lambda,{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathfrak{l}}/\Lambda)\cong T_{\Lambda}\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})_{{\mathfrak{l}}}\hookrightarrow T_{[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda]}\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})\twoheadrightarrow T_{\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})}\mathrm{Conf}(C).

A moment’s thought shows that the above composition restricts to the map

{\mathbf{L}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C^{K}\Gamma(C,{H}(V))\oplus\Hom(\Lambda,{\mathbf{E}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}/\Lambda)\to\Hom(\Lambda,{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathfrak{l}})

defined by

{\mathbf{L}}({\mathfrak{a}},M)s\coloneqq({\mathfrak{a}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{l}})s+{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}(Ms).

Therefore, it suffices to show that
{\mathbf{L}}
is surjective.

Since
\coker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\cong\lparen\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}\rparen^{*}
,

{\mathbf{L}}
is surjective if and only if the map

{\mathbf{M}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C^{K}\Gamma(C,{H}(V))\to\lparen\Lambda\otimes\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}\rparen^{*}
defined by

{\mathbf{M}}({\mathfrak{a}})(s\otimes t)\coloneqq\langle t,({\mathfrak{a}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{l}})s\rangle

is surjective.

{\mathbf{M}}
is surjective if and only if the
Petri map

\varpi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Lambda\otimes\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}\to L^{1}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes V^{\dagger})

defined by

\varpi\lparen s\otimes t\rparen(x)\coloneqq s(x)\otimes t(x)

is injective.
Since
d\in\{1,2\}
,
by
3.64
,

\varpi
is injective.
This proves
(4)
.

To prove
(5)
,
consider the projection
\overline{\pi}_{d}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}_{d}\to{C}{R}(V)\times\mathrm{Conf}(C)
.
Evidently,
\pi_{d}
is Fredholm if and only if
\overline{\pi}_{d}
is, and

\ind T_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])}\pi_{d}=\ind T_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])}\overline{\pi}_{d}+\dim T_{\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})}\mathrm{Conf}(C)=\ind T_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])}\overline{\pi}_{d}+2\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}).

The Snake Lemma applied to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])}{E}_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{K}\Gamma(C,{H}(V))\oplus T_{[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda]}\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\Lambda,{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathfrak{l}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{K}\Gamma\lparen C,{H}(V)\rparen\oplus T_{\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})}\mathrm{Conf}(C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{K}\Gamma(C,{H}(V))\oplus T_{\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})}\mathrm{Conf}(C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])}\overline{\pi}}

yields an exact sequence

\ker T_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])}\overline{\pi}_{d}\hookrightarrow\Hom(\Lambda,{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathfrak{l}}/\Lambda)\xrightarrow{{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\circ\cdot}\Hom(\Lambda,{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathfrak{l}})\twoheadrightarrow\coker T_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda])}\overline{\pi}_{d}.

By
Theorem 3.44
,

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
is Fredholm of index
-\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V
.
Therefore,

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\circ\cdot\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Hom(\Lambda,{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathfrak{l}}/\Lambda)\to\Hom(\Lambda,{\mathbf{F}}_{\mathfrak{l}})
is Fredholm of index

d\cdot\lparen-\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V-d\rparen
.
This proves
(5)
.
∎

Set

{E}\coloneqq{E}_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\pi\coloneqq\pi_{1}.

The wall of failure of
2
–rigidity is parameterised as

{W}=\im\pi.

3.4.5
Failure of injectivity: construction of
\nu

Here is the proof that
\pi
is essentially injective;
that is:
2.6

(22.a)
holds.
The map
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}\to{C}{R}(V)
fails to be injective if there are distinct
({\mathfrak{d}},[{\mathfrak{l}}_{i},\Lambda_{i}])\in{E}
(
i=1,2
).
If
{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}={\mathfrak{l}}_{2}\eqqcolon{\mathfrak{l}}
,
then
({\mathfrak{d}},[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda_{1}+\Lambda_{2}])\in{E}_{2}
.
The following describes these failures of injectivity.

Proposition 3.65
.

Consider the flag manifold bundle
\mathrm{Fl}_{1,2}({\mathbf{E}})
over
\mathrm{RamLinBun}(C)
.
For
d\in\{1,2\}
consider the projection maps

p_{d}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\mathrm{Fl}_{1,2}({\mathbf{E}})\to\Gr_{d}({\mathbf{E}})
.
Set

{N}_{2}\coloneqq(\mathrm{id}\times p_{2})^{*}{E}_{2}\subset{C}{R}(V)\times\mathrm{Fl}_{1,2}({\mathbf{E}}).

The map

\nu_{2}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{N}_{2}\to{E}_{1}
induced by
p_{1}
is Fredholm of index

-\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})-2.

Proof.

The map
\rho\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{N}_{2}\to{E}_{2}
induced by
p_{2}
is Fredholm of index
1
.
Therefore,
by
3.63
,

\pi_{2}\circ\rho=\pi_{1}\circ\nu_{2}
is Fredholm of index
\lparen 2-2\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\rparen\cdot\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})-3
.
This implies the assertion.
∎

Failures of injectivity with
{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}\not\cong{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}
are described as follows.

Proposition 3.66
.

Set
\mathrm{RamLinBun}^{(2)}\coloneqq\mathrm{RamLinBun}^{2}{\backslash}\Delta
(with
\Delta
denoting the diagonal).
Consider
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\Gr_{1}({\mathbf{E}})\times\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\Gr_{1}({\mathbf{E}})
over
\mathrm{RamLinBun}^{(2)}
.
Denote by
\mathrm{TwistRes}_{1}^{(2)}
the smooth section of
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\Hom({S},{\mathbf{F}})\oplus\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\Hom({S},{\mathbf{F}})\to{C}{R}(V)\times\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\Gr_{1}({\mathbf{E}})\times\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\Gr_{1}({\mathbf{E}})
induced by
\mathrm{TwistRes}_{1}
.
The following hold:

(1)

\mathrm{TwistRes}_{1}^{(2)}({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}}_{1},\Lambda_{1}];[{\mathfrak{l}}_{2},\Lambda_{2}])=0
if and only if
\Lambda_{i}\subset\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}}
(
i=1,2
).

(2)

\mathrm{TwistRes}_{1}^{(2)}
intersects the zero section transversely.

(3)

The projection
\pi_{1}^{(2)}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}_{1}^{(2)}\coloneqq\lparen\mathrm{TwistRes}_{1}^{(2)}\rparen^{-1}(0)\to{C}{R}(V)
is Fredholm of index

\lparen 2-\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\rparen\cdot\lparen\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{1})+\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{1})\rparen-2

at
({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}}_{1},\Lambda_{1}];[{\mathfrak{l}}_{2},\Lambda_{2}])
.

Proposition 3.67
.

Set
{N}_{1}^{(2)}\coloneqq{E}_{1}^{(2)}
.
The map
\nu_{1}^{(2)}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{N}_{1}^{(2)}\to{E}_{1}
is Fredholm of index

\lparen 2-\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\rparen\cdot\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})-1

Proof.

The proof is straight-forward and similar to that of
3.65
.
∎

The proof of
3.66

requires the following preparation.

Proposition 3.68
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
.
Let
{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}
be a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
and
s_{i}\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}}{\backslash}\{0\}
(
i=1,2
).
If
{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}
and
{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}
are not isomorphic,
then there are a non-empty open subset
U\subset C{\backslash}\lparen\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{1})\cup\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{2})\rparen
and trivialisations
\tau_{i}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}|_{U}\cong{\underline{\mathbf{R}}}
(
i=1,2
) such that

\tau_{1}s_{1},\tau_{2}s_{2}\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}|_{U}
are linearly-independent.

Proof.

Suppose not.
Choose a open cover
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in A}
of
C{\backslash}\lparen\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{1})\cup\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{2})\rparen
and
trivialisations
\tau_{i}^{\alpha}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}|_{U_{\alpha}}\cong{\underline{\mathbf{R}}}
(
i=1,2
,
\alpha\in A
).
Define
\lambda_{\alpha}\in\mathbf{R}^{\times}
by
\tau_{2}^{\alpha}s_{2}=\lambda_{\alpha}\cdot\tau_{1}^{\alpha}s_{1}
.
A moment’s thought shows that

(\tau_{2}^{\alpha})^{-1}\circ\lambda_{\alpha}\circ\tau_{1}^{\alpha}=(\tau_{2}^{\beta})^{-1}\circ\lambda_{\beta}\circ\tau_{1}^{\beta}

on
U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}
.
Therefore,
{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}\cong{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}
as line bundles over
C{\backslash}\lparen\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{1})\cup\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}_{2})\rparen
.
This implies that
{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}\cong{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}
as ramified Euclidean line bundles over
C
.
∎

Proof of
3.66
.

(1)
is obvious.

As in the proof of
3.63
,

(2)
reduces to proving that the
Petri map

\varpi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Lambda_{1}\otimes\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}_{1}}\oplus\Lambda_{2}\otimes\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}_{2}}\to L^{1}\Gamma(C,V\otimes V^{\dagger})

defined by

\varpi(s_{1}\otimes t_{1},s_{2}\otimes t_{2})(x)\coloneqq s_{1}(x)\otimes t_{1}(x)+s_{2}(x)\otimes t_{2}(x)

is injective.
This is a consequence of

3.68
and
3.64
.

The proof of
(3)
is similar to that of
3.63

(5)

and,
therefore, omitted.
∎

Set
{N}\coloneqq{N}_{2}\amalg{N}_{1}^{(2)}
and
\nu\coloneqq\nu_{2}\amalg\nu_{1}^{(2)}
.
The above discussion shows that
\nu\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{N}\to{E}
is Fredholm of index at most
-1
and
\pi|_{{E}{\backslash}\im\nu}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}{\backslash}\im\nu\to{W}
is injective.

3.4.6
Failure of smoothness: construction of
\sigma

The crucial step in the proof of
Theorem 3.40
is to establish that
\pi
is essentially proper;
that is:
2.6

(22.b)
holds.
A naive hope might be that
Theorem 3.43
implies outright properness.
Indeed:
if
({\mathfrak{d}}_{n};[{\mathfrak{l}}_{n},\Lambda_{n}=\langle s_{n}\rangle])\in{E}^{\mathbf{N}}
is such that
({\mathfrak{d}}_{n})\in{W}^{\mathbf{N}}
converges to
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}\in{W}
,
then it can be arranged that the sequence of pseudo-holomorphic cycles
(T_{n})
corresponding to
(s_{n})
converges to a
J_{\infty}
–holomorphic cycle
T_{\infty}
.
This, however, does not imply that
([{\mathfrak{l}}_{n},\Lambda_{n}])
converges to
[{\mathfrak{l}}_{\infty},\Lambda_{\infty}]
—
unless:

T_{\infty}
is smooth and

\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty})=\{[{\mathfrak{l}}_{\infty},\Lambda_{\infty}=\langle s_{\infty}\rangle]\}
.
Fortunately,
it is straight-forward to describe when
T_{\infty}
fails to be smooth or, equivalently, when the corresponding
J_{\infty}
–holomorphic map
u_{\infty}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
fails to be an injective immersion.

Definition 3.69
.

Assume the situation of
3.33
.
Let
b\in\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
.
Set

J_{b}^{1/2}(V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})\coloneq V_{b}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}J_{b}^{1/2}({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})
.
Set
\{\tilde{b}\}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(b)
.
Denote by
\pi^{*}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace J_{b}^{1/2}(V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})\cong V_{b}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}T_{\tilde{b}}^{*}\tilde{C}
the isomorphism induced by
\pi
(see
3.35
).
The
\tfrac{1}{2}
–jet evaluation map at
b
is the linear map
j_{b}^{1/2}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\to J_{b}^{1/2}(V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
defined by

\pi^{*}j_{b}^{1/2}(s)\coloneqq\partial_{\tilde{b}}u\in V_{b}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}T_{\tilde{b}}^{*}\tilde{C}

with
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
denoting the
J
–holomorphic map corresponding to
s
(according to
Section 3.3
).

Proposition 3.70
.

Let
{\mathfrak{l}}
be a ramified Euclidean line bundle.
Let
s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
.
Let
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
be the corresponding
J
–holomorphic map (according to
Section 3.3
).
The following are equivalent:

(1)

The map
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
is an injective immersion.

(2)

The section
s
is nowhere vanishing in the following sense:

(a)

For every
x\in\mathring{C}
,

\mathrm{ev}_{x}(s)\coloneqq s(x)\neq 0
.

(b)

For every
b\in\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
,

j_{b}^{1/2}(s)\neq 0
.

Proof.

The map
u
is injective if and only if
(22.a)
holds.

T_{\tilde{x}}\tilde{u}\neq 0
for every
\tilde{x}\in\mathring{\tilde{C}}
.
If
\tilde{b}\in\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}))
,
then
T_{\tilde{b}}u=\partial_{\tilde{b}}u
.
Therefore,
u
is an immersion if and only only if
(22.b)
holds.
∎

The failure of

(22.a)

is described by the following.

Proposition 3.71
.

Consider the fibre product
{E}\times_{\mathrm{Conf}(C)}\mathring{{C}}
.
The following hold:

(1)

There are
a vector bundle
\Hom({S},V\otimes{\mathfrak{L}})\to{E}\times_{\mathrm{Conf}(C)}\mathring{{C}}
and
for every
({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda];x)\in{E}\times_{\mathrm{Conf}(C)}\mathring{{C}}

(canonical) isomorphisms

\eta_{({\mathfrak{d}};{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda;x)}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Hom({S},V\otimes{\mathfrak{L}})_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda];x)}\cong\Hom\lparen\Lambda,V_{x}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}_{x}\rparen.

(2)

\Hom({S},V\otimes{\mathfrak{L}})\to{E}\times_{\mathrm{Conf}(C)}\mathring{{C}}
has a
C^{1}
section
\mathrm{ev}
such that

\lparen\mathrm{ev}({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda];x)\rparen=\eta_{({\mathfrak{d}};{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda;x)}^{-1}\lparen\mathrm{ev}_{x}\rparen

with
\mathrm{ev}_{x}\in\Hom\lparen\Lambda,V_{x}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}_{x}\rparen
defined by
\mathrm{ev}_{x}(s)\coloneqq s(x)
.

(3)

\mathrm{ev}({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\mathbf{R}\langle s\rangle],x)=0
if and only if
s(x)=0
.

(4)

\mathrm{ev}
intersects the zero section transversely;
in particular,
{S}^{\mathrm{Reg}}\coloneqq\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(0)
is a Banach submanifold.

(5)

The projection map
\sigma^{\mathrm{Reg}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{S}^{\mathrm{Reg}}\to{E}
is Fredholm of index

2-\rk V.

Proof.

(1)
and
(2)
are evident from the discussion in
Section 3.4.3
.

(3)
holds by construction.

To prove
(4)
it suffices to show that for
({\mathfrak{d}},[{\mathfrak{l}},\mathbf{R}\langle s\rangle],x)\in\mathrm{ev}^{-1}(0)
the map

\mathrm{ev}_{x}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\ker\big\lparen W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\oplus C^{K}\Gamma\lparen X,{H}(V)\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\big\rparen\to V_{x}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}_{x}

is surjective.
Let
k\in\{1,\cdots,K\}
.
By
3.57
,

\ker\big\lparen W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\oplus C^{K}\Gamma\lparen X,{H}(V)\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\big\rparen\\
=\ker\big\lparen W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\oplus C^{K}\Gamma\lparen X,{H}(V)\rparen\to W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\big\rparen.

The significance of the above is that

W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\hookrightarrow C^{k-1}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
;
hence,
\mathrm{ev}_{x}
is defined on
W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
.
By the Snake Lemma,
it suffices to prove that the map
{\mathbf{L}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\oplus C^{K}\Gamma(X,{H}(V))\to W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\oplus V_{x}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}_{x}

defined by

{\mathbf{L}}(\hat{s},{\mathfrak{a}})\coloneqq\big\lparen{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\hat{s}+({\mathfrak{a}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{l}})s,\hat{s}(x)\big\rparen

is surjective.
Choose
{\mathfrak{V}}\subset\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
such that
\mathrm{ev}_{x}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{V}}\to\lparen V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen_{x}
is an isomorphism.
It suffices to prove that the map

{\mathbf{M}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C^{K}\Gamma(X,{H}(V))\to\coker\big\lparen{\mathfrak{d}}_{W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k+1,2}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{V}}^{\perp}\to W_{\rm{cyl}}^{k,2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\big\rparen\cong\big\lparen\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}+{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}({\mathfrak{V}})\big\rparen^{*}

defined by

\langle{\mathbf{M}}({\mathfrak{a}}),t\rangle\coloneqq\langle t,({\mathfrak{a}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{l}})s\rangle

is surjective.

{\mathbf{M}}
is surjective,
because the Petri map

\varpi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\ker{\mathfrak{d}}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}+{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}({\mathfrak{V}})\to L^{1}\Gamma(\mathring{C},V\otimes V^{\dagger})
defined by
\varpi(t)\coloneqq s\otimes t
is injective.

(5)
is obvious.
∎

The failure of

(22.b)

is described by the following.

Proposition 3.72
.

Consider the
universal branch points

{B}\coloneqq\{(B,b)\in\mathrm{Conf}(C)\times C:b\in B\}.

Consider the fibre product
{E}\times_{\mathrm{Conf}(C)}{B}
.
The following hold:

(1)

There are
a vector bundle
\Hom\lparen{S},J^{1/2}(V\otimes{\mathfrak{L}})\rparen\to{E}\times_{\mathrm{Conf}(C)}{B}
and
for every
({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda];b)\in{E}\times_{\mathrm{Conf}(C)}{B}

(canonical) isomorphisms

\eta_{({\mathfrak{d}};{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda;b)}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\Hom\lparen{S},J^{1/2}(V\otimes{\mathfrak{L}})\rparen_{({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda];b)}\cong\Hom\lparen\Lambda,J_{b}^{1/2}(V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})\rparen.

(2)

\Hom\lparen{S},J^{1/2}(V\otimes{\mathfrak{L}})\rparen\to{E}\times_{\mathrm{Conf}(C)}\mathring{{B}}
has a
C^{1}
section
j^{1/2}
such that

\lparen j^{1/2}({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda];b)\rparen=\eta_{({\mathfrak{d}};{\mathfrak{l}},\Lambda;x)}^{-1}\lparen j_{b}^{1/2}\rparen.

(3)

j^{1/2}({\mathfrak{d}};[{\mathfrak{l}},\mathbf{R}\langle s\rangle],b)=0
if and only if
j_{b}^{1/2}(s)=0
.

(4)

j^{1/2}
intersects the zero section transversely;
in particular,
{S}^{\mathrm{Br}}\coloneqq\lparen j^{1/2}\rparen^{-1}(0)
is a Banach submanifold.

(5)

The projection map
\sigma^{\mathrm{Br}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{S}^{\mathrm{Br}}\to{E}
is Fredholm of index

-\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V.

Proof.

The crucial point is to extend
j_{b}^{1/2}
from
\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
to
W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
.
By
3.35

(2)
and

3.48
,

\lparen J_{b}^{1/2}({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})\rparen^{\otimes 2}=T_{b}^{*}C
and

{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}({\mathfrak{l}})\cong\lparen J_{b}^{1/2}({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})\rparen^{*}
.
Therefore,

J_{b}^{1/2}({\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}})\cong{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}({\mathfrak{l}})\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}T_{b}^{*}C.

Let
\zeta\in C^{\infty}({\mathbf{C}},C)
with

\zeta^{-1}(0)=\{b\}
,

T_{b}\zeta\neq 0
, and

\lvert\bar{\partial}\zeta\rvert\lesssim\lvert\zeta\rvert
.
Since
W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\hookrightarrow rL^{2}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
,
for every
s\in W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
,

\zeta^{-1}s\in\mathrm{dom}({\mathfrak{d}}_{\max}^{\mathfrak{l}})
;
moreover:
if
s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
,
then

\Res_{b}(\zeta^{-1}s)\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\partial_{b}\zeta=j_{b}^{1/2}(s).

This provides the extension of
j_{b}^{1/2}
.

The remainder of the proof is analogous to that of that of
3.71
and, therefore, omitted.
∎

Set
{S}\coloneqq{S}^{\mathrm{Reg}}\amalg{S}^{\mathrm{Br}}
and
\sigma\coloneqq\sigma^{\mathrm{Reg}}\amalg\sigma^{\mathrm{Br}}
.
The above discussion shows that
\sigma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{S}\to{E}
is Fredholm of index at most
-1
.

3.4.7
Proofs of
Theorem 3.40
and
Theorem 3.41

Here is the final ingredient for the proofs.

Proposition 3.73
.

The map

\pi|_{{E}{\backslash}\lparen\im\nu\cup\im\sigma\rparen}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}{\backslash}\lparen\im\nu\cup\im\sigma\rparen\to{C}{R}(V){\backslash}\lparen\im\lparen\pi\circ\nu\rparen\cup\im\lparen\pi\circ\sigma\rparen\rparen

is proper.

Proof.

Let
({\mathfrak{d}}_{n};[{\mathfrak{l}}_{n},\Lambda_{n}=\langle s_{n}\rangle])\in\lparen{E}{\backslash}\lparen\im\nu\cup\im\sigma\rparen\rparen^{\mathbf{N}}
.
Suppose that
({\mathfrak{d}}_{n})
converges to
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}\in{C}{R}(V){\backslash}\lparen\im\lparen\pi\circ\nu\rparen\cup\im\lparen\pi\circ\sigma\rparen\rparen
.
Set
({\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty};[{\mathfrak{l}}_{\infty},\Lambda_{n}=\langle s_{\infty}\rangle])\coloneqq\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty})
.
For
n\in{\mathbf{N}}\cup\{\infty\}

set
J_{n}\coloneqq J_{{\mathfrak{d}}_{n}}
and
denote by
u_{n}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}_{n}\to V
the
J_{n}
–holomorphic map corresponding to
s_{n}
,
normalised such that

\max_{\tilde{C}_{n}}\rho^{2}\circ u_{n}=1.

As in the proof of
3.42
,
after passing to a subsequence,

(T_{n}\coloneqq T_{u_{n}})
converges to a
J_{\infty}
–holomorphic cycle
T_{\infty}
in the weak–
*
–topology.
By injectivity,
T_{\infty}=T_{u_{\infty}}
.

Since
u_{n}
(
n\in{\mathbf{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
) is an injective immersion,
it follows from
[
DIW21
, Corollary 2.29]
that
(u_{n})
converges to
u_{\infty}
;
therefore,
([{\mathfrak{l}}_{n},\Lambda_{n}])
converges to
({\mathfrak{l}}_{\infty},\Lambda_{\infty})
.
(The proof of
[
DIW21
, Corollary 2.29]
itself relies crucially on an observation due to
[
Whi05
]
regarding a simple version of Allard’s Regularity Theorem
[
All72
]
.)
∎

Proof of
Theorem 3.40
.

This is an immediate consequence of the above discussion.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 3.41
.

The proof is nearly identical to that of
Theorem 3.40
.
The constructions of
\pi
,
\nu
, and
\sigma
have to be adapted sightly to account for the
\gamma
–self-adjointness;
cf.
[
DW23a
, §1.A]
.
As a result of this index formula in
3.63

(5)
changes to

\lparen 2-d\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\rparen\cdot\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})-\binom{d+1}{2}.\qed

3.74
.

The astute reader may have noticed that for most of section the restriction to
k
–rigidity with
k=2
was unnecessary.
Here is why this restriction was made.
For
k>2
the translation between the perspectives laid out in
Section 3.3
becomes more subtle.
In particular, if
{\mathfrak{L}}
is a higher rank branched local system,
then
s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{L}}
does not directly correspond to a
J
–holomorphic map
\tilde{u}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
;
cf.
[
DW23a
, §1.5]
.
As a consequence of this, evidence of the failure of
k
–rigidity in the form of a
J
–holomorphic map
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
no longer typically is an injective immersion (except for rather small
k
and/or under additional hypothesis).
This breaks the above proof strategy.

Nevertheless,
the restriction to
k=2
may not be necessary.
Moreover,
the use of Allard’s Regularity Theorem and the perspective of
J
–holomorphic maps
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to V
might well be red herring.
Roughly speaking,
the above argument proceeds as follows.
Equip
{E}
with a topology that is coarser than the one discussed in
Section 3.4.3
.
The coarse topology on
{E}
allows for the branch locus of the Euclidean local system
{\mathfrak{l}}
to jump.
This is an essential ingredient to prove that the coarse topology has better compactness properties.
The existence of a non-zero
s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
tames
{\mathfrak{l}}
.
A typical
s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
vanishes only where geometry forces it to.
As a consequence,
typical sequences with typical limits in the coarse topology converge in the fine topology.
Its possible that this strategy extends to branched local systems
{\mathfrak{L}}
of higher rank,
provided an appropriate extension of the coarser topology to this situation is found.

4
Chambered invariants from blow-ups

Let
C
be a closed connected Riemann surface.
Let
V
be a complex vector bundle over
C
with

\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V=2\quad\text{and}\quad 2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)=0.

This section constructs a chambered invariant

n_{\Bl}\in{\rm H}^{0}({C}{R}(V){\backslash}{W};\mathbf{Z}[[x]])

by counting
J
–holomorphic sections
of
q\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\to C
,
the blow-up of the
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}/\{\pm 1\}
–bundle
\check{p}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V/\{\pm 1\}\to C
,
with respect to an admissible almost complex structure on
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
which is homogeneous near infinity and, therefore, induces a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
.

The construction of
n_{\Bl}
is reminiscent of the construction in
[
MS12
, §8.6]
.
In fact, various aspects are simpler in the present situation.
However, a substantial novel complication arises because
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
is non-compact:
sequences of
J
–holomorphic sections might escape to infinity.
A combination of geometric measure theory (again) and a twist on Radó’s theorem in complex analysis
are used to prove that this phenomenon can only occur if the real Cauchy–Riemann operator associated with
J
fails to be
2
–rigid.

4.1
The blow-up:
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)

Here is a summary of
the construction of the blow-up of
\check{p}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V/\{\pm 1\}\to C
,
and some of its properties relevant to the further discussion.

Definition 4.1
.

The blow-up of
\check{p}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V/\{\pm 1\}\to C
is constructed as follows:

(1)

The
projectivisation of
V
is the
{{\mathbf{C}}P}^{1}
–bundle

\varpi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\mathbf{P}}V\coloneqq V^{\times}/{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}\to C.

Here
V^{\times}\coloneqq V{\backslash}0
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}
acts with weight
1
.

(2)

Consider the complex line bundle

\varsigma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)\coloneqq\lparen V^{\times}\times{\mathbf{C}}\rparen/{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}\to{\mathbf{P}}V

with
{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}
acting with weight
(1,-2)
.
The
blow-up of
\check{p}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V/\{\pm 1\}\to C
is the fibre bundle

q\coloneqq\varpi\circ\varsigma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\to C.

(3)

The
blow-down map
is the continuous map

\beta\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)\to V/\{\pm 1\}

defined by

\beta([v,\lambda])\coloneqq[\pm\sqrt{\lambda}v].

Of course,
the blow-down map
\beta

satisfies
q=\check{p}\circ\beta
, and
induces a diffeomorphism

{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)^{\times}\coloneqq{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2){\backslash}0\cong V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}.

This shall be regarded as an identification.

If
I\in{J}_{h}(V)
is
{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}
–invariant,
then it is integrable,
and descends to a complex structure
\check{I}
on
V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
;
moreover:
\check{I}
extends to a complex structure
\tilde{I}
on
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
.
(If
J\in{J}_{h}(V)
is not
{\mathbf{C}}^{\times}
–invariant,
then
\check{J}
does not extend from
V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
to
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
.)

Choose a Hermitian metric on
V
.
Define
\rho^{2}\in C^{\infty}(V)
by
\rho^{2}(v)\coloneqq\lvert v\rvert^{2}
.
Denote the induced smooth maps on
V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
and
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
by
\check{\rho}^{2}
and
\tilde{\rho}^{2}
respectively.

Proposition 4.2
.

The complex fibre bundle
q\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\to C
admits the following family of fibrewise Kähler forms:

(1)

Let
t\geqslant 0
.
Define
f\in C^{\infty}\lparen\mathbf{R}_{>0}\rparen
by

f_{t}(u)\coloneqq\sqrt{u^{2}+t^{4}}+t^{2}\log u-t^{2}\log\lparen\sqrt{u^{2}+t^{4}}+t^{2}\rparen

and set

\Omega_{I,t}\coloneqq-\frac{1}{4}{\rm d}[{\rm d}(f_{t}\circ\rho^{2})\circ I]\in\Omega^{2}(V^{\times}).

\Omega_{I,t}
is
\{\pm 1\}
–invariant and descends to
\check{\Omega}_{I,t}\in\Omega^{2}(V^{\times}/\{\pm\})
;
moreover:

\check{\Omega}_{I,t}
extends to a closed
2
–form

\tilde{\Omega}_{I,t}\in\Omega^{2}\lparen{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)\rparen.

(2)

(a)

For every
t>0
,

\tilde{\Omega}_{I,t}
is a fiberwise Kähler form with respect to
\tilde{I}
;
that is:
for every
x\in C
,

\tilde{\Omega}_{I,t}|_{{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V_{x}}(-2)}
is a Kähler form with respect to
\tilde{I}|_{{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V_{x}}(-2)}
;
moreover:

\tilde{\Omega}_{I,t}|_{\mathbf{P}V_{x}}
is a positive multiple of the Fubini–Study form on
\mathbf{P}V_{x}
.

(b)

\Omega_{I,0}
is a fibrewise Kähler form with respect to
I
on
V^{\times}
;
moreover:
the fibrewise Kähler metric is the one induced by the Hermitian metric on
V
.

(3)

For every
t\geqslant 0
and
R>0
,

R^{-2}\cdot R^{*}\Omega_{I,t}=\Omega_{I,t/R}.

Proof.

(1)
and
(22.a)
are verified by straight-forward computations.
Indeed, this is nothing but (a family version of) the construction due to
[
EH79
]
; cf.
[
Lye23
]
.

(22.b)
and
(3)
are obvious.
∎

Here are two observations regarding the topology of
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
,
which are required in the following.

Proposition 4.3
.

The
vertical tangent bundle

T^{\mathrm{vert}}{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\coloneqq\ker Tq\subset T{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)

satisfies

c_{1}(T^{\mathrm{vert}}{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))=q^{*}c_{1}(V).

Proof.

Since

\varsigma^{*}{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\hookrightarrow T^{\mathrm{vert}}{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\twoheadrightarrow\varsigma^{*}T^{\mathrm{vert}}\mathbf{P}V

is exact,

c_{1}(T^{\mathrm{vert}}{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))=\varsigma^{*}\big\lparen c_{1}(T^{\mathrm{vert}}\mathbf{P}V)-2c_{1}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(1))\big\rparen.

Since the (relative) Euler sequence

{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}\hookrightarrow{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(1)\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\varpi^{*}V\twoheadrightarrow T^{\mathrm{vert}}\mathbf{P}V

is exact,

c_{1}(T^{\mathrm{vert}}\mathbf{P}V)=\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot c_{1}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(1))+\varpi^{*}c_{1}(V).

Since
\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V=2
,
these combine to prove the assertion.
∎

Proposition 4.4
.

If
s\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C\to{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
is a section of
q\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\to C
,
then
s_{*}[C]\in{\rm H}_{2}\lparen{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2);\mathbf{Z}\rparen
is determined by the
degree

\deg(s)\coloneqq\langle s^{*}\varsigma^{*}c_{1}\lparen{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(1)\rparen,[C]\rangle\in\mathbf{Z};

indeed:

s_{*}[C]=S+\lparen\deg V+\deg(s)\rparen\cdot F\in{\rm H}_{2}\lparen\mathbf{P}V;\mathbf{Z}\rparen\cong{\rm H}_{2}\lparen{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2);\mathbf{Z}\rparen

with
S\coloneqq\mathrm{PD}[c_{1}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(1))]
and
F
denoting the homology class of a fibre of
\mathbf{P}V
.

Proof.

{\rm H}_{2}\lparen\mathbf{P}V;\mathbf{Z}\rparen=\langle S,F\rangle
with

S\cdot S=-\deg V,\quad S\cdot F=1,\quad\text{and}\quad F\cdot F=0.

Since

s_{*}[C]\cdot S=\deg(s)\quad\text{and}\quad s_{*}[C]\cdot F=1,

the assertion follows.
∎

4.5
.

Let
s\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C\to{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)
be a section of
q
.
Define
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
by the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V/\{\pm 1\}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\circ s}

If
s
is transverse to
{\mathbf{P}}V\subset{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)
,
then
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
is a double cover branched over
s^{-1}({\mathbf{P}}V)
.
Therefore, by the Riemann–Hurwitz formula,

g(\tilde{C})=2g(C)-1+\lvert s^{-1}({\mathbf{P}}V)\rvert.

If, moreover,
s
intersects
\mathbf{P}V
positively,
then
\lvert s^{-1}({\mathbf{P}}V)\rvert=\deg(s)
.
In this sense,

\deg(s)
relates to the genus of
\tilde{C}
.

4.2
Admissible almost complex structures on
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)

Here is a description of the subspace of

{J}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))
,
the space of
C^{K}
almost complex structures on
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
employed in the construction of
n_{\Bl}
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\omega_{C}
be a Kähler form on
C
.
There are
a subspace

{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))\subset{J}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))
,
the structure of a Banach manifold on
{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))
, and
a submersion

{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))\to{C}{R}(V)

such that the following hold:

(1)

Every
J\in{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))
is
admissible
;
that is: the following hold:

(a)

The map
q\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)\to C
is
J
–holomorphic.

(b)

If
{\mathfrak{d}}\coloneqq{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}(J)
,
then

J=\check{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}\quad\text{on}\quad\{\tilde{\rho}^{2}\geqslant 1\}=\{\check{\rho}^{2}\geqslant 1\}\subset{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)^{\times}=V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}.

(c)

There is a
\Lambda=\Lambda(J)>0
such that
for every
R>0

the restriction of

\Omega_{\tilde{I},1}+\Lambda(1+R^{2})\cdot q^{*}\omega_{C}

to
\{\tilde{\rho}^{2}<2R^{2}\}\subset{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
is symplectic and tames
J
.
Here
I\coloneqq J_{\bar{\partial}}
with
\bar{\partial}
denoting the Dolbeault operator induced by
{\mathfrak{d}}
.

(2)

For every
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V)
,

{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}^{-1}({\mathfrak{d}})
is contractible; in particular: non-empty.

(3)

For every
J\in{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))
there is an
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(J)>0
such that
if

{\mathfrak{a}}\in B_{\varepsilon}(0)\subset C_{c}^{K}\Gamma\lparen{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2),\overline{\End}_{\mathbf{C}}(T{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2),J)\rparen

satisfies
Tq\circ{\mathfrak{a}}=0
and vanishes on
\{\rho^{2}\geqslant 1\}\subset{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
,
then

J({\mathfrak{a}})\coloneqq\lparen\mathbf{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}J{\mathfrak{a}}\rparen J\lparen\mathbf{1}+\tfrac{1}{2}J{\mathfrak{a}}\rparen^{-1}\in{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2));

moreover:

{\mathfrak{a}}\mapsto J({\mathfrak{a}})
is smooth.

Proof.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V)
.
Denote by
\bar{\partial}
the Dolbeault operator induced by
{\mathfrak{d}}
.
Set
I\coloneqq J_{\bar{\partial}}
and
{\mathfrak{a}}\coloneqq J_{\bar{\partial}}
.
Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}\lparen[0,\infty),0\rparen
with
\chi|_{[0,1/2]}=0
and
\chi|_{[1,\infty)}=1
.
The almost complex structure

J_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\chi}\coloneqq I+\lparen\chi\circ\rho^{2}\rparen\cdot{\mathfrak{a}}

is
\{\pm 1\}
–invariant, and

\check{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\chi}
extends to an almost complex structure
\tilde{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\chi}
on
{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
.

By construction,

\tilde{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\chi}
satisfies
(11.a)
and
(11.b)
with
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}(\tilde{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\chi})\coloneqq{\mathfrak{d}}
.
Moreover, it satisfies

(11.c)
;
indeed:
for
\Lambda\gg_{\mathfrak{d}}1
,

[\Omega_{\tilde{I},1}+\Lambda\lparen 1+\rho^{2}\rparen\cdot q^{*}\omega_{C}](v,\tilde{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\chi}v)>0

for every non-zero
v\in T{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)
;
cf.
[
DW21
, Proof of Lemma 3.6 (5)]
.

Evidently,
for
\varepsilon\ll_{\mathfrak{d}}1
and
{\mathfrak{a}}
as in
(3)
,

\tilde{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\chi}({\mathfrak{a}})
satisfies
(1)
.
The choice of
\varepsilon=\varepsilon({\mathfrak{d}})
can be arranged to depend continuously on
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V)
.
Set

{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))\coloneqq\{\tilde{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\chi}({\mathfrak{a}}):{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(V),{\mathfrak{a}}\penalty\ \text{as in \autoref{Prop_BlowUpAlmostComplexStructures_IsAmple} with}\penalty\ \varepsilon=\varepsilon({\mathfrak{d}})\}

and define
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}(\tilde{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\chi}({\mathfrak{a}}))\coloneqq{\mathfrak{d}}
.
There is an obvious Banach manifold structure on
{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))
with respect to which the above assertions hold.
∎

For the remainder of this section
choose
an incarnation of

{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))\to{C}{R}(V)

as in
4.6
.

4.3
The space of pseudo-holomorphic sections

Set

{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))^{\times}\coloneqq{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)){\backslash}{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}^{-1}({W}).

The chambered invariant
n_{\Bl}
is extracted from the following space of pseudo-holomorphic sections.

Proposition 4.7
.

Let
p>2
.
Denote by
{B}\coloneqq W^{1,p}\Gamma\lparen C,{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)\rparen
the Banach manifold of
W^{1,p}
sections of the fibre bundle
q\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)\to C
.
Denote by

{E}\to{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))^{\times}\times{B}

the Banach space bundle whose fibre over
(J,s)\in{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))^{\times}{B}
is
L^{p}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen C,s^{*}T^{\mathrm{vert}}{{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)}\rparen
.
The following hold:

(1)

The section
\bar{\partial}
of
{E}
defined by
\bar{\partial}(J,s)\coloneqq\bar{\partial}_{J}s
intersects the zero section transversely;
in particular,
{M}\coloneqq\bar{\partial}^{-1}(0)
is a Banach submanifold.

(2)

The map

\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\bar{\partial}^{-1}(0)\to{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))^{\times}
is Fredholm of index
0
.

Proof.

If
(J,s)\in\bar{\partial}^{-1}(0)
,
then
\im s
must intersect
\{\tilde{\rho}^{2}<1\}\subset{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)
;
otherwise:
s
defines a
\check{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}
–holomorphic section of
V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
for
{\mathfrak{d}}\coloneqq{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}(J)
,
contradicting
J_{\mathfrak{d}}\notin{W}
.
Therefore and by
4.6

(3)
,
the transversality argument from the proof of
[
MS12
, Theorem 8.3.1]
carries over to prove
(1)
.

The Snake Lemma implies that
\ind T_{J,s}\pi
agrees with the index of a real Cauchy–Riemann operator

{\mathfrak{d}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen C,s^{*}T^{\mathrm{vert}}{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen C,s^{*}T^{\mathrm{vert}}{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\rparen.

By Riemann–Roch and
4.3
, and
since

\rk_{\mathbf{C}}T^{\mathrm{vert}}{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)=\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V=2
and

2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)=0
,

\displaystyle\ind{\mathfrak{d}}

\displaystyle=2\deg\lparen s^{*}T^{\mathrm{vert}}{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}}(-2)\rparen+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)

\displaystyle=2(\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V-2)\cdot\deg(s)=0.

This proves
(2)
.
∎

The index bundle of
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{M}\to{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))^{\times}
is oriented in the standard fashion; cf.
[
MS12
, Theorem 8.3.1]
.

4.4
(\pi,\deg)
is essentially proper

Proposition 4.8
.

There are a Banach manifold
{S}
and a Fredholm map
\sigma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{S}\to{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))^{\times}
of index at most
-2

such that

(\pi,\deg)\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{M}{\backslash}\pi^{-1}(\im\sigma)\to\lparen{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))^{\times}{\backslash}\im\sigma\rparen\times\mathbf{Z}

is proper.

There are two potential source of non-properness of
(\pi,\deg)
:
the formation of bubbles and
the non-compactness of
{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)
.
Here is how to deal with the former.

Proposition 4.9
.

Define
\max\tilde{\rho}^{2}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{M}\to[0,\infty)
by
\max\tilde{\rho}^{2}(s)\coloneqq\max_{C}\tilde{\rho}^{2}\circ s
.
There are a Banach manifold
{S}
and a Fredholm map
\sigma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{S}\to{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))^{\times}
of index at most
-2

such that

(\pi,\deg,\max\tilde{\rho}^{2})\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{M}{\backslash}\pi^{-1}(\im\sigma)\to\lparen{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2))^{\times}{\backslash}\im\sigma\rparen\times\mathbf{Z}\times[0,\infty)

is proper.

Proof.

Let
d\in\mathbf{Z}
and
R>0
.
Let
(J_{n},s_{n})\in{M}^{\mathbf{N}}
with
J_{n}\to J_{\infty}
,
\deg s_{n}=d
and
\max_{C}\rho^{2}\circ s\leqslant R
.
By
4.6

(11.c)
,
for every
n\in{\mathbf{N}}\cup\{\infty\}

there is a symplectic form
\omega_{n}
taming
J_{n}
on
\{\tilde{\rho}^{2}\leqslant R\}\subset{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2)
with
\omega_{n}\to\omega_{\infty}
.
Therefore, by Gromov compactness,
a subsequence of
(s_{n})
converges (a priori) to a stable nodal
J_{\infty}
–holomorphic map

s_{\infty}\vee\bigvee_{\alpha\in T}b_{\alpha}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C\vee\bigvee_{\alpha\in T}{{\mathbf{C}}P}^{1}\to{O}_{{\mathbf{P}}V}(-2).

Here
s_{\infty}
is a
J_{\infty}
–holomorphic section of
q\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)\to C
,
and every bubble
b_{\alpha}
of the bubble tree
T
maps into a fibre of
q
.

The appearance of a non-trivial bubble tree is a codimension two phenomenon.
This is a consequence of the discussion in
[
MS12
, §8.4 and §8.5]
.
The salient point is that the existence of a (simple)
J
–holomorphic sphere in a fibre of
q
is a codimension zero phenomenon and, therefore,
such a sphere intersecting a
J
–holomorphic section of
q
is a codimension two phenomenon.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
d\in\mathbf{Z}
.
If
(J_{n},s_{n})\in{M}
with
\deg s_{n}=d
and
J_{n}\to J
,
then

\sup_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}\max_{C}\tilde{\rho}^{2}\circ s_{n}<\infty.

Proof.

If not,
then, after passing to a subsequence,

R_{n}^{2}\coloneqq\max_{C}\tilde{\rho}^{2}\circ s_{n}\to\infty.

The following five steps lead to a contradiction.

Step 1
.

There is a proper
\check{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}
–holomorphic map
u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C^{\times}\to V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
satisfying

\max_{C^{\times}}\check{\rho}^{2}\circ u=1

and such that,
after passing to a subsequence,
for every
\alpha\in\Omega_{c}^{2}(V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\})

\int_{C^{\times}}u^{*}\alpha=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{C}s_{n}^{*}(R_{n}^{-1})^{*}\alpha.

For every
n\in{\mathbf{N}}
,

T_{n}\in\Hom(\Omega_{c}^{2}(V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}),\mathbf{R})
defined by

T_{n}(\alpha)\coloneqq\int_{C}s_{n}^{*}(R_{n}^{-1})^{*}\alpha

is a
(R_{n})_{*}J_{n}
–holomorphic cycle in
V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
.

By
4.6

(11.c)
,
for
\Lambda\gg 1
,
n\in{\mathbf{N}}
,

\omega_{n}\coloneqq R_{n}^{-2}R_{n}^{*}[\Omega_{\tilde{I}_{n},1}+\Lambda(1+R_{n}^{2})\cdot q^{*}\omega_{C}]

is symplectic and tames
(R_{n})_{*}J_{n}

on
\{\tilde{\rho}^{2}<2\}\subset{O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)
.
By
4.6

(11.b)
,

(R_{n})_{*}J_{n}
converges to
\check{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}
with
{\mathfrak{d}}\coloneqq{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}(J_{\infty})
on compact subsets of
V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
.
By

4.2

(22.b)
and
(3)
,

\omega_{n}
converges to a symplectic form
\omega_{\infty}
on compact subsets of
\{\check{\rho}^{2}<2\}
;
moreover:
\omega_{\infty}
tames
\check{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}
.
Since

\sup_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}{\mathbf{M}}(T_{n})\leqslant\sup_{n\in{\mathbf{N}}}\int_{C}s_{n}^{*}(R_{n}^{-1})^{*}\omega_{n}<\infty,

by
Theorem 3.43
,
after passing to a subsequence,

(T_{n})
converges to a
\check{J}_{\mathfrak{d}}
–holomorphic cycle
T\in\Hom(\Omega_{c}^{2}(V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}),\mathbf{R})
.

Since
\max_{\supp T_{n}}\check{\rho}^{2}=1
,
by the monotonicity formula
[
DW21
, Lemma 5.6]
,

\max_{\supp T}\check{\rho}^{2}=1
.
The assertion follows from
Theorem 3.25
.

Step 2
.

The holomorphic map
\iota\coloneqq\check{p}\circ u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C^{\times}\to C
is open.

Since
\iota
is holomorphic,
if its fails to be open,
then it is constant on a component of
C^{\times}
.
Therefore,
by
Step 1
,

u
exhibits a bounded proper holomorphic map from this component to
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}/\{\pm 1\}
.
This contradicts the maximum principle.

Step 3
.

The holomorphic map
\iota\coloneqq\check{p}\circ u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C^{\times}\to C
is an embedding;
in particular:
\iota
identifies
C^{\times}
with the open subset
\im\iota\subset C
.

By
Step 2
,

\iota\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C^{\times}\to\im\iota
is a finite branched covering map.
If
z\in\im\iota
is not a branch point,
then it admits an open neighborhood

U\subset\im\iota
such that

\iota\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\iota^{-1}(U)\to U
is a covering map and

K\coloneqq\overline{\iota^{-1}(U)}\subset V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
is compact.
Choose
\eta\in\Omega_{c}^{2}(U)
with
\int_{U}\eta=1
,
and
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(V^{\times}/\{\pm 1\})
with
\chi|_{K}=1
.
By
Step 1
and since
s_{n}
is a section of
q
,

\displaystyle\deg_{z}\iota

\displaystyle=\int_{C^{\times}}u^{*}\check{p}^{*}\eta=\int_{C^{\times}}u^{*}\lparen\chi\cdot\check{p}^{*}\eta\rparen=T(\chi\cdot\check{p}^{*}\eta)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{C}s_{n}^{*}(R_{n}^{-1})(\chi\cdot\check{p}^{*}\eta)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{C}s_{n}^{*}(R_{n}^{-1})^{*}q^{*}\eta

\displaystyle=\int_{C}\eta=1.

Therefore,
\deg\iota=1
and
\iota
is an embedding.

Step 4
.

The map
\check{\rho}^{2}\circ u\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C^{\times}\to(0,1]
extends to a continuous function
C\to[0,\infty]
vanishing on
B\coloneqq C{\backslash}C^{\times}
.

Since
u
is proper,
for every
\varepsilon>0
,

K_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\{\check{\rho}^{2}\circ u\geqslant\varepsilon\}\subset C^{\times}\subset C
is compact;
hence:

B\cup\{\check{\rho}^{2}\circ u<\varepsilon\}=C{\backslash}K_{\varepsilon}
is open.

Step 5
.

{\mathfrak{d}}
fails to be
2
–rigid: the desired contradiction.

Set
B\coloneqq C{\backslash}C^{\times}
.
By the discussion in
Section 3.3
,

u
defines a Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}
over
C{\backslash}B
and
a non-zero
s\in\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
.
If
B
was finite,
then
{\mathfrak{l}}
is (or extends to) a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
;
therefore:
{\mathfrak{d}}
fails to be
2
–rigid.
Indeed,
B
is finite because
\lvert s\rvert
extends to a continuous map on
C
vanishing on
B
.
This is proved as
4.11
below.
∎

Proof of
4.8
.

This is an immediate consequence of
4.9
and
4.10
.
∎

4.5
A twist on Radó’s theorem

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
.
Let
B\subset C
be a closed subset.
Let
{\mathfrak{l}}\to C{\backslash}B
be a Euclidean line bundle.
If
s\in\Gamma(C{\backslash}B,V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})

is non-vanishing,
satisfies
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}s=0
, and

\lvert s\rvert
extends to a continuous map on
C
vanishing on
B
,
then
B
is finite.

Since no a priori control on the size of
B
is assumed,
this cannot be derived via the usual removable singularity methods
(at least those known to the authors).
Instead,
the proof relies on the following classical result in complex analysis.

4.12
[
Rad24
]
,
[
BS51
, Satz 1]
,
[
Car52
]
;
[
Hei56
,
Rud87
]
.

Let
C
be a Riemann surface.
If
f\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace C\to{\mathbf{C}}
is a continuous map which is holomorphic on
C{\backslash}f^{-1}(0)
,
then
f
is holomorphic.
∎

Proof of
4.11
.

If
{\mathfrak{d}}=\bar{\partial}
is a Dolbeault operator,
then
s\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}s\in\Gamma(C{\backslash}B,V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}V)
is holomorphic,
and extends to a continuous section of
V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}V
vanishing on
B
.
By
Theorem 4.12
,

s\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}s
is holomorphic.
Therefore,
\lvert s\rvert^{-1}(0)=\lparen s\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}s\rparen^{-1}(0)
is finite.

If
{\mathfrak{d}}
is a real Cauchy–Riemann operator,
then the assertion follows from the above and the Carleman similarity principle
[
MS12
, §2.3]
.
Decompose
{\mathfrak{d}}=\bar{\partial}+{\mathfrak{a}}
as in
3.2
and
define
\tilde{a}\in L^{\infty}(C,\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(V,V\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\overline{K}_{C}))
by

\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{a}}s\cdot\lvert s\rvert^{-2}\langle s,\cdot\rangle&\textnormal{if }x\notin B,\\
0&\textnormal{if }x\in B.\end{cases}

By construction
\lparen\bar{\partial}+\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}\rparen^{\mathfrak{l}}s=0
.
Let
p>2
.
By
[
MS12
, Step 3 in the proof of Theorem 2.3.5]
,
for every
x\in C
there are
\delta>0
and a
g\in W^{1,p}\lparen B_{\delta}(x),\End_{\mathbf{C}}(V)\rparen
satisfying
g(\bar{\partial}+\tilde{\mathfrak{a}})g^{-1}=\bar{\partial}
;
in particular:
\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{l}}(gs)=0
.

Therefore,
\lvert s\rvert^{-1}(0)\supset B
is discrete;
hence: finite.
∎

4.6
Construction of
n_{\Bl}

The construction of
n_{\Bl}
is now straight-forward;
cf.
2.2
.
By

4.7
and

4.9
,
if
J\in{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)){\backslash}{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}^{-1}({W})

is a regular value of
\pi
and
J\notin\im\sigma
,
then for every
d\in\mathbf{Z}

{M}_{\Bl}(J,d)\coloneqq(\pi,\deg)^{-1}(J,d)

is a compact oriented
0
–manifold.
By Sard–Smale, the subset of these
J
is comeager.
Also by
4.7
,

4.9
,
and Sard–Smale,
the oriented bordism class

[{M}_{\Bl}(J,d)]\in\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{SO}}

only depends of the path-component of
{J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)){\backslash}{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}({W})
.
Since
\Omega_{0}^{\mathrm{SO}}=\mathbf{Z}
,
therefore, there is an invariant

\widetilde{n}_{\Bl}\in{\rm H}^{0}({J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)){\backslash}{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}({W});\mathbf{Z}[[x]])

with

\widetilde{n}_{\Bl}(J)=\sum_{d\in{\mathbf{N}}_{0}}\#{M}_{\Bl}(J,d)\cdot x^{d}.

Since
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}
has path-connected fibres,

{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}^{*}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\rm H}^{0}({C}{R}(V){\backslash}{W};\mathbf{Z}[[x]])\to{\rm H}^{0}({J}_{\varheartsuit}({O}_{\mathbf{P}V}(-2)){\backslash}{\mathfrak{d}}_{\infty}^{-1}({W});\mathbf{Z}[[x]])

is an isomorphism.
Therefore,

\widetilde{n}_{\Bl}
descends to the desired
n_{\Bl}
.

4.13
.

If
d\in-{\mathbf{N}}
,
then
[{M}_{\Bl}(J,d)]=0
.
This can be proved by a suitable choice of
J
.

4.14
.

There is a variant of the above construction for
\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V>2
.
The essential difference is that
\pi
in
4.7

(2)
is Fredholm of index
2(\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V-2)\cdot\deg(s)
at
(J,d)
.
As a consequence,
the variant of
n_{\Bl}
is constructed using
{M}_{\Bl}(J,0)
only and, therefore,
\mathbf{Z}
–valued.

5
Chambered invariants from gauge theory

This section constructs chambered invariants of real Cauchy–Riemann operators by counting solutions to the
quaternionic vortex equations
: gauge-theoretic equations on Riemann surfaces associated with quaternionic representations.
These equations arise from a dimensional reduction of generalized Seiberg–Witten equations introduced in
[
Tau99
,
Pid04
]
and studied in
[
Tau13
,
Tau16
,
HW15
,
DW20
,
DW21a
,
WZ21
]
.

We focus on the
(r,k)
ADHM vortex equations
associated with the quaternionic representations appearing in the ADHM construction of instantons on
\mathbf{R}^{4}
.
Non-compactness phenomena for the
(r,k)=(2,1)
and
(r,k)=(1,2)
versions of the equations are closely related to
2
–rigidity of real Cauchy–Riemann operators.
These equations are used to construct chambered invariants from
Theorem 1.5
and
Theorem 1.6
.

5.1
Quaternionic vortex equations

Definition 5.1
.

Let
G
be a compact connected Lie group.
Let
V
a quaternionic vector space equipped with a Euclidean inner product with respect to which unit quaternions act by isometries.
Denote by
\mathrm{Sp}(V)
the group of quaternion-linear isometries of
V
.
A
quaternionic representation
of
G
on
V
is a homomorphism
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{Sp}(V)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be the Lie algebra of
G
.
By abuse of notation we denote the action of
{\mathfrak{g}}
on
V
by
\rho
.
Choose a
G
–invariant inner product of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{Sp}(V)
be a quaternionic representation.
Define moment maps
\mu_{i},\mu_{j},\mu_{k}\colon V\to{\mathfrak{g}}
by

\displaystyle\langle\mu_{i}(\phi),\xi\rangle=\langle i\rho(\xi)\phi,\phi\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\mu_{j}(\phi),\xi\rangle=\langle j\rho(\xi)\phi,\phi\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\mu_{k}(\phi),\xi\rangle=\langle k\rho(\xi)\phi,\phi\rangle

for all
\phi\in V
and
\xi\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The
\mathrm{Sp}(1)
–equivariant
hyperkähler moment map
is

\mu=(\mu_{i},\mu_{j},\mu_{k})\colon V\to{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\mathbf{R}^{3}.

The
hyperkähler quotient
of
V
by
G
is the hyperkähler variety defined by

X=\mu^{-1}(0)/G.

The complex structures
i,j,k
and the Euclidean metric induce three non-degenerate
2
–forms
\omega_{i},\omega_{j},\omega_{k}
on
V
.
From now on, we think of
V
as a complex vector space with a distinguished complex structure given by multiplication by
i
.
The remaining complex structures
j
and
k
are equivalent to an
i
–bilinear
2
–form

\Omega=\omega_{j}+i\omega_{k}.

With respect to the splitting
\mathbf{R}^{3}=\mathbf{R}\oplus{\mathbf{C}}
,

\mu=\mu_{\mathbf{R}}\oplus\mu_{\mathbf{C}}

where
\mu_{\mathbf{R}}=\mu_{i}
is the
real moment map
and
\mu_{\mathbf{C}}=\mu_{j}+i\mu_{k}
is the
complex moment map
.
The latter is the moment map for the action of the complex group
G_{\mathbf{C}}
on the complex symplectic vector space
(V,i,\Omega)
.

In what follows, consider the following situation.

Hypothesis 5.3
.

Let
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{Sp}(V)
be a quaternionic representation.
Assume that the center of
G
contains a subgroup isomorphic to
\{\pm 1\}
such that
\rho(-1)=-\mathrm{id}
.

In the situation of
5.3
, let

\mathrm{Spin}^{G}(2)=\mathrm{Spin}(2)\times_{\{\pm 1\}}G,

so that
\rho
extends to a unitary representation

\rho\colon\mathrm{Spin}^{G}(2)\to\mathrm{U}(V)

by

\rho([\lambda,g])v=\lambda\rho(g)v.

We have isomorphisms
\mathrm{Spin}(2)\cong\mathrm{U}(1)
and
\mathrm{SO}(2)\cong\mathrm{U}(1)
under which
\mathrm{Spin}(2)\to\mathrm{SO}(2)
is given by
z\mapsto z^{2}
.
Let
L={\mathbf{C}}
be the standard representation of
\mathrm{U}(1)
, so that the induced representation of
\mathrm{Spin}(2)
is
L^{2}
.
The maps
\Omega
and
\mu_{\mathbf{C}}
are
\mathrm{Spin}^{G}(2)
–equivariant maps

\Omega\colon\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbf{C}}V\to L^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{\mathbf{C}}\colon V\to L^{2}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}.

Let
C
be a surface equipped with a Riemannian metric.
Let
{\mathfrak{w}}\to C
be a principal
\mathrm{Spin}^{G}(2)
bundle together with an isomorphism of the frame bundle of
K_{C}
, the canonical bundle of
C
, with the principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
bundle induced by
{\mathfrak{w}}
and homomorphism
\mathrm{Spin}^{G}(2)\to\mathrm{U}(1)
.
Denote by
P
the
G/\{\pm 1\}
–principal bundle associated with
{\mathfrak{w}}
and the projection
\mathrm{Spin}^{G}(2)\to G/\{\pm 1\}
.
Let

{\mathbf{V}}={\mathfrak{w}}\times_{\mathrm{Spin}^{G}(2)}V

be the associated vector bundle with complex structure induced by
i
.
The algebraic structures associated with
\rho
gives rise to the following bundle structures: the real and complex moment maps

\mu_{\mathbf{R}}\colon{\mathbf{V}}\to\Ad P\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{\mathbf{C}}\colon{\mathbf{V}}\to K_{C}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}},

where
\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}}
is the complexification of
\Ad P
,
and the complex symplectic form

\Omega\colon\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbf{C}}{\mathbf{V}}\to K_{C},

which induces an isomorphism
\gamma\colon{\mathbf{V}}\cong{\mathbf{V}}^{\dagger}={\mathbf{V}}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}
as in
Section 3.1
.

For every connection
A
on
P
there is a unique connection on
{\mathfrak{w}}
such that the induced connections on
K_{C}
and
P
are the Levi–Civita connection and
A
.
Thus, for every
A
we have the induced Cauchy–Riemann operator

\bar{\partial}_{A}\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,{\mathbf{V}}).

This operator is self-adjoint with respect to
\Omega
in the sense of
3.8
:

\bar{\partial}_{A}^{\dagger}=\bar{\partial}_{A}^{\gamma}

or equivalently

\int_{C}\Omega(\bar{\partial}_{A}\Phi\wedge\Psi)=\int_{C}\Omega(\Phi\wedge\bar{\partial}_{A}\Psi)\quad\text{for all }\Phi,\Psi\in\Gamma({\mathbf{V}}).

Definition 5.4
.

The
quaternionic vortex equation
for a connection
A
on
P
and a section
\Psi\in\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})
is

(5.5)

\begin{split}\bar{\partial}_{A}\Psi=0,\\
\mu_{\mathbf{C}}(\Psi)=0,\\
F_{A}+\ast\mu_{\mathbf{R}}(\Psi)=0.\end{split}

Let
{A}(P)
be the space of connections on
P
.
The gauge group
{G}(P)
of
P
acts on
{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})
preserving the quaternionic vortex equations.

5.6
.

Solutions to the quaternionic vortex equations are critical points of a holomorphic functional on
{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})
.
The space of connections
{A}(P)
is an affine space over
\Omega^{1}(C,\Ad P)=\Omega^{0,1}(C,\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}})
where
P_{\mathbf{C}}
is the complexification of
P
.
This makes the configuration space
{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})
into an infinite-dimensional Kähler manifold with a Hamiltonian action of the gauge group
{G}(P)
.
The moment map for this action is

\displaystyle m\colon{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\Gamma(\Ad P),

\displaystyle m(A,\Psi)=\ast F_{A}+\mu_{\mathbf{R}}(\Psi).

We also have the action of the complex gauge group
{G}_{\mathbf{C}}(P)
which preserves the complex structure but not the symplectic form on
{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})
.
The holomorphic Chern–Simons functional

\displaystyle{F}\colon{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to{\mathbf{C}},\

\displaystyle{F}(A,\Psi)=\int_{C}\Omega(\bar{\partial}_{A}\Psi\wedge\Psi)

is invariant under the action of
{G}_{\mathbf{C}}(P)
.
The equation for critical points is

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{A}\Psi=0,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{C}}(\Psi)=0.

Therefore, the quaternionic vortex equation can be interpreted as the critical point equation for
{F}
, together with the moment map equation
m(A,\Psi)=0
.
The Hitchin–Kobayashi correspondence implies that the
{G}(P)
orbits of solutions correspond to
{G}_{\mathbf{C}}(P)
orbits of solutions to the critical point equation satisfying an appropriate stability condition.

5.2
Generalizations

The main results on counting solutions to quaternionic vortex equations
Theorem 5.35
and

Theorem 5.45
, concern certain modifications to these equations.
The first modification is to allow what physicists refer to as flavor symmetry.

Hypothesis 5.7
.

Let
H
be a compact Lie group.
Assume that
G
is a normal subgroup of
H
and that
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{Sp}(V)
extends to a quaternionic representation
\rho\colon H\to\mathrm{Sp}(V)
satisfying
5.3
.

In this situation,
G
is called the
gauge symmetry group
and
I=H/G
the
flavor symmetry group
.
As before, extend
\rho\colon H\to\mathrm{Sp}(V)
to a unitary representation

\rho\colon\mathrm{Spin}^{H}(2)\to\mathrm{U}(V).

Let
{\mathfrak{w}}\to C
be a principal
\mathrm{Spin}^{H}(2)
bundle together with an isomorphism between the frame bundle of
K_{C}
and the principal
\mathrm{U}(1)
bundle induced by
{\mathfrak{w}}
and homomorphism
\mathrm{Spin}^{H}(2)\to\mathrm{U}(1)
.
Define

{\mathbf{V}}={\mathfrak{w}}\times_{\mathrm{Spin}^{H}(2)}V.

We have a short exact sequence

(5.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

so that
{\mathfrak{w}}
induces a principal
I
–bundle
Q\to C
.
Fix a connection on
Q
.
If
H=G\times I
is a product, then there is a
G/\{\pm 1\}
principal bundle
P\to C
associated with
{\mathfrak{w}}
.
In that case, given a connection
A\in{A}(P)
, there is a unique connection on
{\mathfrak{w}}
which induces
A
on
P
, the fixed connection on
I
, and the Levi–Civita connection on
K_{C}
.
This connection induces a twisted Dolbeault operator

\bar{\partial}_{A}\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,{\mathbf{V}}),

and we can consider the quaternionic vortex equation
(5.5)
with respect to this operator.

In general, if
(5.8)
does not split, such a bundle
P
might not exist.
However, we can still consider the space of
\mathrm{Spin}^{H}(2)
connections on
{\mathfrak{w}}
which induce the given connection on
Q
and the Levi–Civita connection on
K_{C}
.
By abuse of notation, denote this space by
{A}(P)
as before.
Moreover, the associated bundle of Lie algebras
\Ad(P)
and the group of
G
–gauge transformations
{G}(P)
still exist.
With this adjustment of notation, we define
\bar{\partial}_{A}
and consider
(5.5)
in the general case.

The second modification is to allow zeroth order perturbations of the Cauchy–Riemann operator.
For the discussion of compactness, it is important to restrict ourselves to perturbations which are compatible with the hyperkähler quotient construction.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
{H}({\mathbf{V}},\gamma)
be the vector spaces modeling the affine space of
\gamma
–self-adjoint real Cauchy–Riemann operators on
{\mathbf{V}}
, see
3.9
.
Denote by
{H}({\mathbf{V}},\rho)\subset{H}({\mathbf{V}},\gamma)
the subbundle of those
a\in{H}({\mathbf{V}},\gamma)
which commute with
G
–automorphisms of
{\mathbf{V}}
and for every
\Phi\in{\mathbf{V}}
with
\mu(\Phi)=0
,

a\Phi\in\ker{\rm d}_{\Phi}\mu\cap(\rho({\mathfrak{g}})\Phi)^{\perp}.

A
homogeneous perturbaton
is a section
\Upsilon\in\Gamma({H}({\mathbf{V}},\rho))
.
Given such
\Upsilon
, define

\bar{\partial}_{A,\Upsilon}=\bar{\partial}_{A}+\Upsilon\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,{\mathbf{V}}).

5.10
.

We can interpret elements of
{H}({\mathbf{V}},\rho)
as linear vector fields on
V
which descend to a vector field on the hyperkähler quotient
\mu^{-1}(0)/G
.

5.11
.

In the situation of
5.7
, varying the
I
–connection on the flavor symmetry bundle
Q
can be incorporated into a homogeneous perturbation.

The third modification is to allow inhomogeneous perturbations.
As in classical Seiberg–Witten theory, such perturbations are useful for ruling out reducible solutions

Definition 5.12
.

An
inhomogeneous perturbation
is a pair
\eta=(\eta_{\mathbf{C}},\eta_{\mathbf{R}})
where

(1)

\eta_{\mathbf{C}}\colon{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\Omega^{1,0}(C,\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}})
is an
{G}(P)
–equivariant map satisfying

\bar{\partial}_{A}(\eta_{\mathbf{C}}(A,\Psi))=0,

(2)

\eta_{\mathbf{R}}\colon{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\Omega^{0}(C,\Ad P)
is an
{G}(P)
–equivariant map,

such that the linearizations of
\eta
and
\tau
are pseudo-differential operators of order zero.

For example,
\eta_{\mathbf{C}}
and
\eta_{\mathbf{R}}
can be holonomy perturbations, or can be independent of
(A,\Psi)
and take values in the center of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Definition 5.13
.

Assume
5.3
and
5.7
.
Fix a Riemannian metric on
C
and a connection on the flavor symmetry bundle
Q
.
Let
\Upsilon
and
\eta
be homogeneous and inhomogeneous perturbations.
The
(\Upsilon,\eta)
–pertubed quaternionic vortex equation
for
A\in{A}(P)
and
\Psi\in\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})
is

(5.14)

\begin{split}\bar{\partial}_{A,\Upsilon}\Psi=0,\\
\mu_{\mathbf{C}}(\Psi)=\eta_{\mathbf{C}}(A,\Psi),\\
\ast F_{A}+\mu_{\mathbf{R}}(\Psi)=\eta_{\mathbf{R}}(A,\Psi).\end{split}

5.15
.

The assumptions that
\Upsilon
is
\gamma
–self-adjoint and
\eta_{\mathbf{C}}
satisfies
\bar{\partial}_{A}\eta_{\mathbf{C}}=0
are technical and probably not necessary.
However, without these assumptions
(5.14)
has to be replaced by more general equations
(5.18)
to make the equations elliptic, see
5.19
below.
It is likely that compactness theory for generalized Seiberg–Witten equations can be extended to
(5.18)
but we will not consider this question here.

5.3
Moduli spaces of quaternionic vortices

Definition 5.16
.

In the situation of
5.13
, the
moduli space of
(\Upsilon,\eta)
–perturbed quaternionic vortices
is

{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)=\{(A,\Psi)\in{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}}):(A,\Psi)\text{ satisfies \autoref{Eq_PerturbedQuaternionicVortex}}\}/{G}(P).

Equip
{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
with the topology of
C^{\infty}
convergence.

5.17
.

In practice, it is convenient to consider perturbations
\Upsilon
,
\eta_{\mathbf{R}}
,
\eta_{\mathbf{C}}
of lower regularity.
In that case, we consider solutions to
(5.14)
of lower regularity, such as
C^{K}
for some
K\in{\mathbf{N}}
, and equip the moduli space with the
C^{K}
topology.

Equation
(5.14)
arises from gauge theory over the
3
–manifold
C\times S^{1}
(this is explained in
Appendix D
).
Therefore, deformation theory of solutions to
(5.14)
should be controlled by an elliptic complex of index zero.
The first and third equation in
(5.14)
already form an elliptic system modulo gauge.
However, the index of the linearization of this elliptic is not zero.
To obtain correct deformation theory with index zero, introduce the following extension of
(5.14)
.
Denote by

\overline{\gamma}\colon{\mathbf{V}}\to{\mathbf{V}}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}^{*}

the complex anti-linear map obtained by composing
\gamma\colon{\mathbf{V}}\to{\mathbf{V}}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}
with the anti-linear isomorphism
{\mathbf{V}}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}\to{\mathbf{V}}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}^{*}
given by the Hermitian inner product.
Consider the following equations for a triple
(A,\Psi,\zeta)
where
\zeta\in\Gamma(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}})
:

(5.18)

\begin{split}(\bar{\partial}_{A,\Upsilon}+\overline{\gamma}(\rho(\zeta)\Psi)=0,\\
\partial_{A}\zeta+\mu_{\mathbf{C}}(\Psi)=\eta_{\mathbf{C}}(A,\Psi),\\
\ast F_{A}+\mu_{\mathbf{R}}(\Psi)=\eta_{\mathbf{R}}(A,\Psi).\end{split}

The next proposition, which follows from a standard calculation in Seiberg–Witten theory, see
D.4
, shows that the additional field
\zeta
can be ignored.

Proposition 5.19
.

If
(A,\Psi,\zeta)
is a solution to
(5.18)
, then

\bar{\partial}_{A}\zeta=0\quad\text{and}\quad\rho(\zeta)\Psi=0.

In particular, if the stabilizer of
(A,\Psi)
in
{G}
is trivial, then
\zeta=0
.

By linearizing
(5.18)
at
(A,\Psi,0)
modulo the action of
{G}(P)
, we associate with the solution an elliptic deformation complex:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}(\Ad P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}(\Ad P^{\mathbf{C}})\oplus\Omega^{1}(\Ad P)\oplus\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{2}(\Ad P)\oplus\Omega^{0,1}(\Ad P^{\mathbf{C}})\oplus\Omega^{0,1}({\mathbf{V}}).}

We have real isomorphisms

\Omega^{0,1}(C,{\mathbf{C}})\cong\Omega^{1}(C,\mathbf{R})\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega^{0}(C,{\mathbf{C}})\cong\Omega^{0}(C,\mathbf{R})\oplus\Omega^{2}(C,\mathbf{R}),

with respect to which the map

{\rm d}+{\rm d}^{*}\colon\Omega^{1}(C,\mathbf{R})\to\Omega^{0}(C,\mathbf{R})\oplus\Omega^{2}(C,\mathbf{R})

is identified with

\bar{\partial}^{*}\colon\Omega^{0,1}(C,{\mathbf{C}})\to\Omega^{0}(C,{\mathbf{C}}).

We also use the real isomorphism
\overline{\gamma}\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\cong\Omega^{0,1}({\mathbf{V}})
.
Under these identifications, the operator associated with the deformation complex is

\displaystyle L_{A,\Psi}\colon\Omega^{0,\bullet}(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}})\oplus\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\Omega^{0,\bullet}(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}})\oplus\Gamma({\mathbf{V}}),

\displaystyle L_{A,\Psi}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\partial}_{A}+\bar{\partial}_{A}^{*}&\Gamma_{\Psi}^{*}\\
\Gamma_{\Psi}&\overline{\gamma}^{-1}\bar{\partial}_{A,\Upsilon}\end{pmatrix}+T_{A,\Psi}\eta

where
\Omega^{0,\bullet}=\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{0,1}
, the map
\Gamma_{\Psi}\colon\Omega^{0,\bullet}(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}})\to{\mathbf{V}}
is defined by

\Gamma_{\Psi}(\xi+a)=\rho(\xi)\Psi+\overline{\gamma}^{-1}(\rho(a)\Psi)\quad\text{for }\xi\in\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}}\quad\text{and}\quad a\in K_{C}^{*}\otimes\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}},

and
T_{A,\Psi}\eta
is the derivative of
\eta(A,\Psi)
at
(A,\Psi)
.
The operator
L_{A,\Psi}
is a compact perturbation of the self-adjoint elliptic operator

\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\partial}_{A}+\bar{\partial}_{A}^{*}&0\\
0&\overline{\gamma}^{-1}\bar{\partial}_{A,\Upsilon}\end{pmatrix}.

Therefore,
L_{A,\Psi}
is elliptic and
\ind(L_{A,\Psi})=0
.

5.20
.

An alternative way to obtain the deformation operator is to consider the equation for citical points of the complex Chern–Simons functional modulo the complex gauge group
{G}^{\mathbf{C}}(P)
.
(We assume here that
\Upsilon
is
{\mathbf{C}}
–linear so that the complex Chern–Simons functional is
{G}^{\mathbf{C}}(P)
–invariant.)
A finite-dimensional analog is the deformation complex associated with a critical point of a holomorphic Morse function invariant under the action of a complex Lie group.
From this point of view, the deformation complex is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0,1}(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}})\oplus\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1,0}(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}})\oplus\Omega^{0,1}({\mathbf{V}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1,1}(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}}).}

The first map is the linearization of the
{G}_{\mathbf{C}}(P)
action, the second map is the linearization of the equation with a complex co-gauge condition, and the remaining two maps are complex dual to the first two over
{\mathbf{C}}
.
Observe that
\Omega^{1,0}
is dual to
\Omega^{0,1}
and
\Omega^{0}
is dual to
\Omega^{1,1}
using the wedge product and integration, and similarly
\Omega^{0,1}({\mathbf{V}})
is dual to
\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})
by the pairing

\alpha\in\Omega^{0,1}({\mathbf{V}}),\quad\beta\in\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\mapsto\int_{\Sigma}\Omega(\alpha\wedge\beta).

With respect to these identifications, the above complex is self-dual over
{\mathbf{C}}
.
Under the isomorphisms

\Omega^{0}(\Ad P)\oplus\Omega^{2}(\Ad P)\cong\Omega^{0}(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}}),\quad\Omega^{1}(\Ad P)\cong\Omega^{0,1}(\Ad P_{\mathbf{C}}),\quad\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\cong\Omega^{0,1}({\mathbf{V}})

the operator associated with the above complex agrees with
L_{A,\Psi}
.

The following is a standard application of the implicit function theorem and the existence of slices for the action of the gauge group.

Definition 5.21
.

A solution
(A,\Psi)
to
(5.14)
is
irreducible
if the stabilizer of
(A,\Psi)
in
{G}
is trivial, and
unobstructed
if
\coker L_{A,\Psi}=\{0\})
(equivalently,
\ker L_{A,\Psi}=\{0\}
).

Proposition 5.22
.

Let
(A,\Psi)
be a solution to
(5.14)
and let
G_{A,\Psi}
be the stabilizer of
(A,\Psi)
in
{G}(P)
.
The operator
L_{A,\Psi}
is
G_{A,\Psi}
–equivariant and there exist

(1)

an open neighborhood
U
of
[A,\Psi]
in
{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
,

(2)

a
G_{A,\Psi}
–equivariant open neihborhood
U
of
0
in
\ker L_{A,\Psi}
,

(3)

a
G_{A,\Psi}
–equivariant smooth map
\kappa\colon U\to U

such that
U
is homeomorphic to
\kappa^{-1}(0)/G_{A,\Psi}
.
In particular, if every
[A,\Psi]\in{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
is irreducible and unobstructed, then
{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
is a discrete space.

The determinant line bundle of the family of operators
L_{A,\Psi}
over
{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})
is
{G}(P)
–equivariantly trivial because the
{G}(P)
–equivariant homotopy

L_{A,\Psi;t}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\partial}_{A}+\bar{\partial}_{A}^{*}&t\Gamma_{\Psi}^{*}\\
t\Gamma_{\Psi}&\overline{\gamma}^{-1}\bar{\partial}_{A}+t\Upsilon\end{pmatrix}+tT_{A,\Psi}\eta

connects
L_{A,\Psi}=L_{A,\Psi;1}
with
L_{A,\Psi;0}
, which is the sum of a
{\mathbf{C}}
–linear and
{\mathbf{C}}
–antilinear operator.
For every irreducible unobstructed solution
[A,\Psi]\in{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
, define
\sign(A,\Psi)\in\{-1,1\}
by orientation transport from
\operatorname{det}L_{A,\Psi;0}
to
\operatorname{det}L_{A,\Psi;1}
.

Definition 5.23
.

Suppose that
{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
is compact and all solutions in
{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
are irreducible and unobstructed.
Define

(5.24)

\#{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)=\sum_{[A,\Psi]\in{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)}\sign(A,\Psi).

General Fredholm theory allows us to extend this definition to the case when the moduli space is compact but possibly obstructed; see
[
CGMS02
, Theorem 2.7]
,
[
Bru96
, Proposition 14]
.

Proposition 5.25
.

For every
(\Upsilon,\eta)
such that
{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
is compact and consists of irreducible solutions we can associate then
\#{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)\in\mathbf{Z}
in such a way that:

(1)

If all solutions in
{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
are unobstructed, then
\#{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
agrees with
(5.24)
.

(2)

If
(\Upsilon_{t},\eta_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is a continuous path such that
{M}(\Upsilon_{t},\eta_{t})
is compact and consists of irreducible solutions for all
t\in[0,1]
, then

\#{M}(\Upsilon_{0},\eta_{0})=\#{M}(\Upsilon_{1},\eta_{1}).

In general, the moduli space
{M}(\Upsilon,\eta)
may be non-compact.
This phenomenon was studied in
[
Tau13a
,
HW15
,
WZ21
]
.
The known compactness results assume the following algebraic property of the quaternionic representation
\rho\colon G\to V
.

Hypothesis 5.26
.

Given
\Phi\in V
define
\Gamma_{\Psi}\colon\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{H}\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}\to V
by
\Gamma_{\Psi}(q\otimes\xi)=q\rho(\xi)V
.
Suppose that there exist constants
\delta,c>0
such that for every
\Psi\in V
with
|\Psi|=1
and
|\mu(\Psi)\leqslant\delta

|\mu(\Psi)|\leqslant c|\Gamma_{\Psi}\mu(\Psi)|.

Definition 5.27
.

Let
\Upsilon
be a homogeneous perturbation.
An
\Upsilon
–limiting configuration
is a triple
(B,A,\Psi)
consisting of

•

a nowhere dense closed subset
B\subset C
,

•

a
G
–connection
A
on
P|_{C{\backslash}B}
,

•

a section
\Psi\in\Gamma(C{\backslash}B,{\mathbf{V}})
with
\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
\|\nabla_{A}\Psi\|_{L^{2}}<\infty
,

such that

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{A,\Upsilon}\Psi=0,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{C}}(\Psi)=0,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{R}}(\Psi)=0,

and
|\Psi|
extends to a Hölder continuous function on
C
such that
|\Psi|^{-1}(0)=B
.

The following is an adaptation of a compactness theorem for generalized Seiberg–Witten equations
[
WZ21
]
.
The proof is postponed until
Appendix D
.

5.28
.

Assume that the quaternionic representation
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{Sp}(V)
satisfies
5.26
.
Let
(A_{n},\Psi_{n})
be a sequence of solutions to the
(\Upsilon,\eta)
–perturbed quaternionic vortex equation such that
|\eta(A_{n},\Psi_{n})|
,
|\nabla_{A_{n}}\eta(A_{n},\Psi_{n})|
are uniformly bounded.

(1)

If
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}<\infty
, then after passing to a subsequence and applying gauge transformations
(A_{n},\Psi_{n})
converges in
C^{\infty}
to a solution
(A,\Psi)
.

(2)

If
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}=\infty
, then there exists an
\Upsilon
–limiting configuration
(B,A,\Psi)
such that after passing to a subsequence and applying gauge transformations over
C{\backslash}B

(a)

|\Psi_{n}|/\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}
converges to
|\Psi|
in the
C^{0,\alpha}
topology over
C
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

(b)

A_{n}
converges to
A
in the weak
W^{1,2}
topology over compact subsets of
C{\backslash}B
,

(c)

\Psi_{n}/\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}
converges to
\Psi
in the weak
W^{2,2}
topology over compact subsets of
C{\backslash}B
.

5.4
ADHM vortex equations
