The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{r,k}
of framed instantons on
\mathbf{R}^{4}
with structure group
\mathrm{SU}(r)
and charge
k
is a hyperkähler orbifold.
The ADHM construction exhibits
\mathcal{M}_{r,k}
as a finite-dimensional hyperkähler quotient of a quaternionic representation of
\mathrm{U}(k)
.
In this section we discuss the quaternionic vortex equations associated with this representations.
These equations were proposed by Haydys–Walpuski in relation to gauge theory on special holonomy manifolds
[
HW15
]
; for related discussion see
[
DW19
]
.

Let
G=\mathrm{U}(k)
and

V=(\mathbf{H}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\End({\mathbf{C}}^{k}))\oplus(\mathbf{H}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{C}}^{r},{\mathbf{C}}^{k})).

Define a quaternionic structure on
V
by left quaternionic multiplication on
\mathbf{H}
.
Define a quaternionic representation
\rho\colon H\to\mathrm{Sp}(V)
by the adjoint action of
G
on
\End({\mathbf{C}}^{k})
and the standard action on
{\mathbf{C}}^{k}
, and by
K
acting by right quaternionic multiplication on the first summand, and trivially on the second summand.
The quaternionic moment map for the
G
action is

\displaystyle\mu\colon V\to\mathbf{R}^{3}\otimes{\mathfrak{u}}(k),

\displaystyle\mu(\xi,\Psi)=[\xi\wedge\xi]^{+}+(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}.

The first term combines the wedge product and the Lie bracket on
{\mathfrak{u}}(k)
with the projection
\Lambda^{2}\mathbf{R}^{4}\to\Lambda^{+}\mathbf{R}^{4}=\mathbf{R}^{3}
.
The second term involves projecting
\Psi\Psi^{*}
on
\mathfrak{su}(2)\subset\End(\mathbf{R}^{4})
and identification
\mathfrak{su}(2)\cong\Lambda^{4}\mathbf{R}^{4}=\mathbf{R}^{3}
using the Clifford multiplication.
Let
\Psi=(\alpha,\beta^{*})
under identification
\mathbf{H}={\mathbf{C}}^{2}
, where
\alpha\in\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{C}}^{r},{\mathbf{C}}^{k})
and
\beta\in\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{C}}^{k},{\mathbf{C}}^{r})
.
The complex and real moment maps (multiplied by
i
) are

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{C}}(\xi,\Psi)=[\xi\wedge\xi]+\alpha\beta,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbf{R}}(\xi,\Psi)=[\xi,\xi^{*}]+\alpha\alpha^{*}-\beta^{*}\beta,

where in the first formula we identify
\Lambda^{2}{\mathbf{C}}^{2}={\mathbf{C}}
.

5.29
Atiyah–Hitchin–Drinfeld–Manin.

The hyperkähler quotient of
V
by
G
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{M}_{r,k}
if
r\geqslant 2
and to
\Sym^{k}\mathbf{H}
if
r=1
.

The ADHM representation has a natural flavor symmetry as in
5.7
.
Let

K=\mathrm{Sp}(1)\times\mathrm{SU}(r)\quad\text{and}\quad H=G\times K.

The action of
G
extends to an action of
\mathrm{Spin}^{H}(2)
with the action of
K
preserving the quaternionic structure and commuting with
G
.
The action of
\mathrm{Sp}(1)
on
V
is by right quaternionic multiplication
q\cdot v\mapsto vq^{-1}
.

The resulting quaternionic vortex equation
(5.5)
is concretely described as follows.
We start with the following data:

•

a Riemann surface
C
,

•

a Hermitian vector bundle
N\to C
of rank two, equipped with a
\mathrm{U}(2)
connection,

•

a covariantly constant isomorphism
\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbf{C}}N\cong K_{C}
,

•

a Hermitian vector bundle
E\to C
of rank
r
and trivial determinant line bundle, equipped with an
\mathrm{SU}(r)
connection, and

•

a Hermitian vector bundle
H\to C
of rank
k
.

The equation involves a
\mathrm{U}(k)
connection
A
on
U
and a section
\Psi
of

(5.30)

{\mathbf{V}}=(N\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\End H)\oplus(\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(E,H)(\oplus(\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(H,E)\otimes K).

If
\Psi=(\xi,\alpha,\beta)
, where

\xi\in\Gamma(N\otimes\End H),\quad\alpha\in\Gamma(\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(E,H)),\quad\beta\in\Omega^{1,0}(C,\Hom_{\mathbf{C}}(H,E))),

then the (unperturbed)
(r,k)
ADHM vortex equation is

(5.31)

\begin{split}\bar{\partial}_{A}\alpha=0,\quad\bar{\partial}_{A}\beta=0,\quad\bar{\partial}_{A}\xi&=0,\\
[\xi\wedge\xi]+\alpha\beta&=0,\\
i\ast F_{A}+[\xi,\xi^{*}]+\alpha\alpha^{*}-\ast\beta^{*}\beta&=0.\end{split}

In the second equation,
[\xi\wedge\xi]
combines the wedge product
N\otimes N\to\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbf{C}}N
with the commutator on
\End H
, followed by the isomorphism
\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbf{C}}N\cong T^{*}C
.
In the third equation,
[\xi,\xi^{*}]
combines the Hermitian inner product on
N
with the commutator on
\End H
, so that it takes place in
\Gamma(\End H)
.
The equation is equivariant with respect to the action of the group
{G}(H)
of unitary gauge transformations of
H
.

Our goal is to consider a perturbation of
(5.31)
of the form
5.16
, for a suitable class of
\Upsilon
and
\eta
, and use them to define chambered invariants of
(\Upsilon,\eta)
.
We expect that this is possible for all values of
(r,k)
.
However, the known compactness results for quaternionic vortex equations require the technical assumption from
5.26
, which is satisfied only for
(r,k)=(1,2)
and
(r,k)=(k,2)
.
We focus on these two cases.

5.5
The
(1,2)
ADHM vortex equation

In this section we define chambered invariants of real Cauchy–Riemann operators using the ADHM vortex equation
(5.31)
with
r=1
and
k=2
.

Let
C
be a compact Riemann surface.
Let
H
be a Hermitian vector bundle of rank
2
.
Let
N\to C
be a Hermitian vector bundle of rank
2
equipped with an isomorphism
\gamma\colon N\cong N^{\dagger}
, where
N^{\dagger}=N\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}^{*}
.
Denote by
{C}{R}(N,\gamma)
the space of real Cauchy–Riemann operator which are self-adjoint with respect to
\gamma
.
Every such operator can be written as

{\mathfrak{d}}=\bar{\partial}_{N}+\Upsilon

where
\bar{\partial}_{N}
is a fixed self-adjoint Cauchy–Riemann operator on
N
and
\Upsilon\in\Gamma({H}(N,\gamma))
, see
3.9
.
For every
\Upsilon
as above and
\mathrm{U}(2)
connection
A
on
H
denote by

\bar{\partial}_{A,\Upsilon}=\bar{\partial}_{N,A}+\Upsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}\colon\Gamma(N\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\End_{\mathbf{C}}H)\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,N\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\End_{\mathbf{C}}H)

the induced real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
N\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\End_{\mathbf{C}}H
.

Definition 5.32
.

Let
\Upsilon\in\Gamma({H}(N,\gamma))
and
\eta=(\eta_{\mathbf{C}},\eta_{\mathbf{R}})\in{\rm H}^{1,0}(C)\times\mathbf{R}
.
The
(\Upsilon,\eta)
-perturbed
(1,2)
ADHM vortex equation
for

A\in{A}(H),\quad\xi\in\Gamma(N\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\End_{\mathbf{C}}H),\quad\alpha\in\Gamma(H),\quad\beta\in\Omega^{1,0}(H^{*})

reads

(5.33)

\begin{split}\bar{\partial}_{A}\alpha=0,\quad\bar{\partial}_{A}\beta=0,\quad\bar{\partial}_{A,\Upsilon}\xi&=0,\\
[\xi\wedge\xi]+\alpha\beta&=\eta_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\mathrm{id}_{H},\\
i\ast F_{A}+[\xi,\xi^{*}]+\alpha\alpha^{*}-\ast\beta^{*}\beta&=(\eta_{\mathbf{R}}+\pi\deg(H))\mathrm{id}_{H}.\end{split}

5.34
.

This is the ADHM vortex equation
(5.31)
for
(r,k)=(1,2)
with homogeneous and inhomogeneous pertubations, as in
(5.14)
.
We consider here the subset of homogeneous perturbations given by sections of
{H}(N,\gamma)
rather than the full
{H}({\mathbf{V}},\rho)
.

Let
{W}\subset{C}{R}(N,\gamma)
be the wall of failure of
2
-rigidity defined in
Section 3.3
.

5.35
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}=\bar{\partial}_{N}+\Upsilon
and
\eta\in{\rm H}^{1,0}(C)\times\mathbf{R}
.
Suppose that
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}(N,S){\backslash}{W}
and
\eta\notin\{0\}\times(\pi/2)\mathbf{Z}
.
The signed count of solutions
\#{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},\eta,d)
to the
(\Upsilon,\eta)
-perturbed
(1,2)
ADHM equation for the bundle
H
with
\deg(H)=d
is well-defined, as in
5.25
, and defines an element

•

\#{M}_{1,2}(\cdot,d)\in{\rm H}^{0}({C}{R}(N,\gamma){\backslash}{W};\mathbf{Z})
if the genus of
C
is positive,

•

\#{M}_{1,2}(\cdot,d)\in{\rm H}^{0}(({C}{R}(N,\gamma){\backslash}{W})\times(\mathbf{R}{\backslash}(\pi/2)\mathbf{Z});\mathbf{Z})
if the genus of
C
is zero.

We begin with a description of limiting configurations for the
(1,2)
ADHM vortex equation (in the sense of
5.27
).

Proposition 5.36
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}=\bar{\partial}_{N}+\Upsilon
.
If
(B,A,\Psi)
is an
\Upsilon
-limiting configuration for the
(1,2)
ADHM vortex equation
(5.33)
, then there exist a Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}
over
C{\backslash}B
, a bundle inclusion

\rho\colon{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\hookrightarrow(\End_{\mathbf{C}}H)|_{C{\backslash}B},

and sections
\xi_{0}\in\Gamma(C{\backslash}B,N\otimes{\mathfrak{l}})
and
\xi_{1}\in\Gamma(C,N)
such that

(1)

\nabla_{A}\rho=0
,

(2)

\xi_{0}
satisfies
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}(\xi_{0})=0
and is of class
W^{1,2}
on
C{\backslash}B
,

(3)

|\xi_{0}|
extends to a Hölder continuous function on
C
such that
|\xi_{0}|^{-1}(0)=B
,

(4)

\xi_{1}
satisfies
{\mathfrak{d}}(\xi_{1})=0
on
C
,

(5)

\xi=\rho(\xi_{0})+\xi_{1}\otimes_{C}\mathrm{id}_{H}
.

Moreover, the set
B
is finite.

Proof.

Defne
\hat{\xi}_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
to be the trace-free and trace part of
\xi
, so that
\Psi=(\hat{\xi}_{0}+\xi_{1},\alpha,\beta)
.
Equation
\mu(\Psi)=0
implies that
\alpha=\beta=0
and
\hat{\xi}_{0}
has two eigenvalues
\pm\lambda\in N
.
(This is how one proves that the hyperkähler quotient of the ADHM
1,2
representation is the symmetric product
\Sym^{4}\mathbf{R}^{4}
.)

Locally over
C{\backslash}B
we can choose eigenvectors
\Psi_{\pm}
in
H
such that
\hat{\xi}\Psi_{\pm}=\pm\lambda\Psi_{\pm}
and
|\Psi_{\pm}|=1
. Trace-free endomorphisms which are diagonal with respect to the basis
\Psi_{-},\Psi_{+}
define a line subbundle of
\End_{\mathbf{C}}H
, which is the image of
\rho\colon{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\to\End_{\mathbf{C}}H
for some Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}
.
By construction,
\hat{\xi}_{0}=\rho(\xi_{0})
, and equation
(\bar{\partial}_{N,A}+\Upsilon)(\hat{\xi}_{0})=0
is equivalent to
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}(\xi_{0})=0
.
It follows from
4.11
that
B
is finite.
Since
\xi_{1}
is a
W^{1,2}
section of
N
defined over
C{\backslash}B
and satisfying
{\mathfrak{d}}(\xi_{1})=0
, the unique continuation principle implies that
\xi_{1}
extends to all of
C
.
The other propertiers of
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
follow from the definition of a limiting configuration.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 5.35
.

First, we show that if
\eta_{\mathbf{C}}\neq 0
, then the moduli space
{M}({\mathfrak{d}},\eta,d)
consists of irreducible solutions.
Suppose that
(A,\xi,\alpha,\beta)
is a reducible solution to
(5.33)
.
By considering possible stabilizers of
A
in the gauge group
{G}
, and how these stabilizers act on
(\xi,\alpha,\beta)
, we see that there are two possibilities.
The first is that
(\alpha,\beta)=(0,0)
; taking the trace of the second equation shows that this is impossible.
The second possibility is that there exists an orthogonal splitting
H=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
which is covariantly constant with respect to
A
and such that
\xi(L_{i})\subset L_{i}
and
\alpha\in\Gamma(L_{1})
,
\beta\in\Omega^{1,0}(L_{1}^{*})
.
In that case,
[\xi\wedge\xi]=0
and
\alpha\beta
is a bundle homomorphism
H\to K_{C}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}H
of rank one, so we cannot have
\alpha\beta=\eta_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H}
.
Therefore, every solution is irreducible.

(The genus zero case is proved similarly.
If
\eta_{\mathbf{C}}=0
and
\eta_{\mathbf{R}}\neq 0
, then
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(0,0)
by integrating the third equation.
On the other hand, if
E=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
and
A=A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
as above, then one of
\alpha
or
\beta
must vanish by the second equation.
The third equation gives us
i\ast F_{A_{2}}=\eta_{\mathbf{R}}+\pi\deg(H)
, which contradicts with
\eta_{\mathbf{R}}\notin(\pi/2)\mathbf{Z}
.)

To prove compactness, assume that
(A_{n},\Psi_{n})
is a sequence of solutions, with
\Psi_{n}=(\xi_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n})
.
By
Theorem 5.28
, if
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}<\infty
, then after passing to a subsequence and applying gauge transformations
(A_{n},\Psi_{n})
converges to a solution.
Suppose, by contradiction, that
\lim_{n}\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}=\infty
.
By
Theorem 5.28
there exists a limiting configuration
(B,A,\Psi)
.
By
5.36
,
B
is finite and there exists a Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}
over
C{\backslash}B
together with a non-zero
W^{1,2}
section
\zeta_{0}
satisfying
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}(\zeta_{0})
.
This contradicts the assumption that
{\mathfrak{d}}
is
2
-rigid.
Therefore,
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}<\infty
for any sequence of solutions and
{M}({\mathfrak{d}},\eta,d)
is compact.
This means that we are in a situation in which
5.25
can be applied.

If
C
has positive genus, any two choices
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in({\rm H}^{1,0}(C)\oplus\mathbf{R}){\backslash}\{(0,0)\}
can be connected by a path avoiding
(0,0)
which shows that
\#{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},\eta,d)=\#{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},d)
.
Given a path
({\mathfrak{d}}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
{\mathfrak{d}}_{t}\notin{W}
, we can lift it to a path
(\Upsilon_{t},\eta_{t})
as above.
The preceding discussion implies that
{M}({\mathfrak{d}}_{t},\eta_{t},d)
is compact and consists of irreducible solutions for every
t\in[0,1]
.
Therefore,
5.25
implies that

\#{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}}_{0},\eta_{0},d)=\#{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}}_{1},\eta_{1},d),

so that it is constant on connected components of
{C}{R}(N,\gamma){\backslash}{W}
.
The genus zero case is similar except that
{\rm H}^{1,0}(C)=0
and we need to take
\eta_{\mathbf{R}}\notin(\pi/2)\mathbf{Z}
.
∎

5.6
The
(2,1)
ADHM vortex equation

In this section we define chambered invariants of real Cauchy–Riemann operators using the ADHM vortex equation
(5.31)
with
(r,k)=(2,1)

The construction of perturbations in this case is somewhat more involved than in the case
(r,k)=(1,2)
discussed earlier, as the algebra of the
(2,1)
ADHM representation has a less direct geometric interpretation.
For that reason, it is convenient to write
(5.31)
in a different form than in the previous section, emphasising the role of spinors and Dirac operators.

We begin with a discussion of spin and spin
c
Dirac operators in dimension two.
For a brief summary of spinors in low dimensions and their relation to quaternions see
Appendix C
.
Let
C
be a Riemann surface and let
E\to C
be a Hermitian vector bundle with structure group
\mathrm{SU}(2)=\mathrm{Sp}(1)
equipped with an
\mathrm{SU}(2)
connection.
A spin structure on
C
is equivalent to a Hermitian line bundle
S^{+}
and an isometry
S^{+}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}S^{+}\cong K_{C}
.
The spinor bundle is

S=S^{+}\oplus S^{-}\quad\text{with }S^{-}=S^{+}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K^{*}=(S^{+})^{*}.

The Levi-Civita connection on
K_{C}
induces a spin connection on
S
.
Two different spinor bundles
S
and
S^{\prime}
are isomorphic as unitary bundles but as bundles with connections
S^{\prime}=S\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}
where
{\mathfrak{l}}
is a real line bundle with a flat
\{\pm 1\}
–connection.
Set

{\mathbf{S}}=E\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}S\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{S}}^{\pm}=E\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}S^{\pm}

and consider the spin Dirac operator twisted by the connection on
E

\displaystyle D\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{S}})\to\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}),

\displaystyle D=\begin{pmatrix}0&\bar{\partial}_{E}^{*}\\
\bar{\partial}_{E}&0\end{pmatrix},

where
\bar{\partial}_{E}
is the Cauchy–Riemann operator twisted by the connection on
E

\bar{\partial}_{E}\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}^{+})\to\Omega^{0,1}({\mathbf{S}}^{+})=\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}^{-}).

The Dirac operator
D
is
{\mathbf{C}}
-linear and self-adjoint.
Since
{\mathbf{S}}
is the complex tensor product of two quaternionic line bundles, it inherits a real structure

\tau\colon{\mathbf{S}}\to{\mathbf{S}},

which is a
{\mathbf{C}}
-antilinear isomorphism satisfying
\tau^{2}=\mathrm{id}
, defined as the tensor product of the
{\mathbf{C}}
-antilinear multiplication by
j
on
E
and
S
.
The Dirac operator
D
commutes with
\tau
and preserves the spliting into
\pm 1
eigenspaces of
\tau
.
Let

{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}}=\operatorname{Re}(E\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}S)

be the
+1
eigenspace.
Denote the induced self-adjoint
\mathbf{R}
-linear operator by

D_{\mathbf{R}}\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}})\to\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}}).

Definition 5.37
.

Let
\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}})
be the subbundle of self-adjoint
{\mathbf{C}}
-linear endomorphisms which commute with
\tau
.
For every
\Upsilon\in\Gamma(\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}}))
set

D_{\Upsilon}=D+\Upsilon\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{S}})\to\Gamma({\mathbf{S}})

and denote by

D_{\Upsilon,\mathbf{R}}\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}})\to\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}}).

the induced real operator.

5.38
.

The bundle
\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}})
does not depend on the choice of the spin structure.
Therefore,
\Upsilon\in\Gamma(\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}}))
defines a perturbation of the spin Dirac operator for every spin structure.

The next proposition follows from linear algebra discussed in
Appendix C
.

Proposition 5.39
.

Define an operator
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\Upsilon}
by the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma({\mathbf{S}}^{-})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{\Upsilon,\mathbf{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{d}}_{\Upsilon}}

where the vertical arrows are induced by the projections
{\mathbf{S}}\to{\mathbf{S}}^{\pm}
.
Then
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\Upsilon}
is a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
{\mathbf{S}}^{+}
which is self-adjoint with respect to the isomorphism

\gamma\colon{\mathbf{S}}^{+}\to({\mathbf{S}}^{+})^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}

induced by
S^{+}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}S^{+}\cong K_{C}
and
\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbf{C}}E\cong\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}
.
Conversely, every
\gamma
-self-adjoint real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
{\mathbf{S}}^{+}
is of this form for a unique
\Upsilon\in\Gamma(\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}}))
.

Proof.

For
\Upsilon=0
this follows from the relation between the Dirac operator and the Cauchy–Riemann operator in dimension
2
, see
C.1
.
The general case then follows from the isomorphism
\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}})\cong\Hom({\mathbf{S}}^{+},{\mathbf{S}}^{-})
described in
C.2
.
∎

From now on, we will implicitly identify the space
{C}{R}({\mathbf{S}}^{+},\gamma)
of
\gamma
-self-adjoint real Cauchy–Riemann operators on
{\mathbf{S}}^{+}
with the space of Dirac operators on
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}}
of the form
D_{\Upsilon,\mathbf{R}}
.
This is an affine space modeled on
\Gamma(\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}}))
.

5.40
.

The section
\Upsilon
can be recovered from
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\Upsilon}
as follows.
Let
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\Upsilon}=\bar{\partial}_{E}+{\mathfrak{n}}
for
{\mathfrak{n}}\in\Gamma(\Hom({\mathbf{S}}^{+},{\mathbf{S}}^{-}))
.
Let
{\mathfrak{n}}={\mathfrak{n}}_{c}+{\mathfrak{n}}_{a}
be the decomposition into
{\mathbf{C}}
-linear and
{\mathbf{C}}
-antilinear parts.
Denote by

\overline{\gamma}\colon{\mathbf{S}}\to{\mathbf{S}}

the
{\mathbf{C}}
-antilinear isomorphism which combines multiplication by
j
on
E
with the isomorphism
S^{\pm}\cong S^{\pm}
given by the Hermitian metric.
Then
\Upsilon
is expressed in terms of
{\mathfrak{n}}
by

\Upsilon=\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{\gamma}{\mathfrak{n}}_{a}&\overline{\gamma}{\mathfrak{n}}_{c}\overline{\gamma}\\
{\mathfrak{n}}_{c}&-{\mathfrak{n}}_{a}\overline{\gamma}\end{pmatrix}.

Next, we discuss spin
c
structures on
C
; such a structure corresponds to the choice of a Hermitian line bundle
\operatorname{det}(W)\to C
of even degree.
If
W=W^{+}\oplus W^{-}
is the spinor bundle, then

W^{-}=K^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}W^{+}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{det}(W)=K^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}(W^{+})^{2}.

A
\mathrm{U}(1)
connection
A
on
\operatorname{det}(W)
induces a unique spin
c
connection on
W
such that the induced connection on
\operatorname{det}(W)
agrees with
A
.
Set

{\mathbf{W}}=E\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}W\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{W}}^{\pm}=E\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}W^{\pm}.

Given such
A
and a connection on
E
, the corresponding twisted Dirac operator on
{\mathbf{W}}
is

\displaystyle D_{A}\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{W}})\to\Gamma({\mathbf{W}}),

\displaystyle D_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\bar{\partial}_{A}^{*}\\
\bar{\partial}_{A}&0\end{pmatrix},

where

\bar{\partial}_{A}\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{W}}^{+})\to\Omega^{0,1}(C,{\mathbf{W}}^{+})=\Gamma({\mathbf{W}}^{-})

is the Cauchy–Riemann operator twisted by
A
and the connection on
E
.
This operator can be perturbed in the following way.

Definition 5.41
.

Fix a spin structure on
C
with spinor bundle
S
.
Since
W
is obtained from
S
by tensoring by a complex line bundle, a section
\Upsilon
of
\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}})
can be interpreted as a section of the bundle of
{\mathbf{C}}
-linear self-adjoint endomorphisms of
{\mathbf{W}}
.
Given a connection
A
on
\operatorname{det}W
, let

D_{A,\Upsilon}=D_{A}+\Upsilon\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{W}})\to\Gamma({\mathbf{W}}).

Definition 5.42
.

Let
C
be a Riemann surface, let
E\to C
be a complex vector bundle with structure group
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, and let
W\to C
be a spin
c
structure.
Let
\Upsilon\in\Gamma(\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}}))
and
\eta=(\eta_{\mathbf{C}},\eta_{\mathbf{R}})
where
\eta\in{\rm H}^{1,0}(C)
and
\eta_{\mathbf{R}}\in\mathbf{R}
.
The
(\Upsilon,\eta)
-perturbed
(2,1)
ADHM vortex equation
for

A\in{A}(\operatorname{det}W),\quad\text{and}\quad\Psi=(\alpha,\beta)\in\Gamma({\mathbf{W}}),

reads

(5.43)

\begin{split}D_{A,\Upsilon}\Psi&=0,\\
\mu_{\mathbf{C}}(\Psi)&=\eta_{\mathbf{C}},\\
\ast F_{A}+i\mu_{\mathbf{R}}(\Psi)&=\eta_{\mathbf{R}}+2\pi\deg(W),\end{split}

where

\mu_{\mathbf{C}}(\Psi)=\alpha^{*}\beta,\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{\mathbf{R}}(\Psi)=i(|\alpha|^{2}-|\beta|^{2}).

5.44
.

These equations are equivalent to a perturbation of the ADHM vortex equation
(5.31)
for
r=2
,
k=1
.
The equations for
\xi
in
(5.31)
decouple so that
\xi
can be ignored.
The remaining equations can be identified with
(5.43)
by taking
W^{+}=S^{+}\otimes H
, replacing
E
by
E^{*}
, and
\beta
by
\beta^{*}
.
The point of that last conjugation is that
\mathbf{H}
can be considered as
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
in two different ways by left- or right-multiplication by
i
, so that the associated bundle
{\mathbf{S}}
has two natural complex structures; the description used in
(5.14)
corresponds to one of them, and in
(5.43)
to the other.
As for perturbations, by
5.39
we can consider
\Gamma(\End_{\tau}({\mathbf{S}}))
as a subspace of the space of homogeneous perturbations introduced in
5.9
.
We use Dirac operators as they interact with the algebraic structure of the
(2,1)
ADHM representation in a more natural way than Cauchy–Riemann operators.

Let
{W}\subset{C}{R}({\mathbf{S}}^{+},\gamma)
be the wall of failure of
2
-rigidity
defined in
Section 3.3
.

5.45
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}\in{C}{R}({\mathbf{S}}^{+},\gamma){\backslash}{W}
.
Let
D_{\Upsilon,\mathbf{R}}
be the Dirac operator corresponding to
{\mathfrak{d}}
under
5.39
.
Let
\eta\in{\rm H}^{1,0}(C)\times\mathbf{R}{\backslash}\{(0,0)\}
.
The signed count of solutions
\#{M}_{2,1}({\mathfrak{d}},\eta,d)
to the
(\Upsilon,\eta)
-perturbed
(2,1)
ADHM equation for the spin
c
structure with
\deg W^{+}=d
is well-defined, as in
5.25
, and defines an element

•

\#{M}_{1,2}(\cdot,d)\in{\rm H}^{0}({C}{R}({\mathbf{S}}^{+},\gamma){\backslash}{W};\mathbf{Z})
if the genus of
C
is positive,

•

\#{M}_{1,2}(\cdot,d)\in{\rm H}^{0}(({C}{R}({\mathbf{S}}^{+},\gamma){\backslash}{W})\times(\mathbf{R}{\backslash}\{0\});\mathbf{Z})
if the genus of
C
is zero.

Since the proof uses
Theorem 5.28
, we need to understand limiting configurations for the
(2,1)
ADHM vortex equation.

Proposition 5.46
.

If
(B,A,\psi)
is an
\Upsilon
-limiting configuration for the
(2,1)
ADHM vortex equation
(5.43)
, then there exist a Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}
over
C{\backslash}B
and an isomorphism

\rho\colon W|_{C{\backslash}B}\to S|_{C{\backslash}B}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}

such that
\nabla_{A}\rho=0
and the section
\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}=(\mathrm{id}_{E}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\rho)\Psi
has the following properties:

(1)

\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}
takes values in
{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathbf{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}
,

(2)

\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}
is of class
W^{1,2}
on
C{\backslash}B
,

(3)

|\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}|
extends to a Hölder continuous function on
C
such that
|\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}|^{-1}(0)=B
,

(4)

D_{\Upsilon}^{\mathfrak{l}}\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}=0
.

Moreover, the set
B
is finite.

5.47
.

The bundle
S\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}
is the spinor bundle of a spin structure on
C{\backslash}B
.
Therefore, a limiting configuration induces a reduction of the spin
c
structure
W
to a spin structure outside
B
.
In particular,
\deg W
is equal to a signed count of points in
B
.
This is a
2
-dimensional analog of
[
Hay19
, Theorem 4]
.

Proof.

This is a special case of a theorem of Haydys describing limiting configurations of generalized Seiberg–Witten equations
[
Hay14
]
; see also
[
DW20
, Section 2.3]
.
Let
L
be the complex line bundle satisfying
W=S\otimes L
.
Trivialize
L
locally over a contractible subset
U\subset C{\backslash}B
so that
\Psi
is identified with a section of
{\mathbf{S}}
.
Since the hyperkähler quotient of
{\mathbf{C}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{H}
by
\mathrm{U}(1)
is
\operatorname{Re}({\mathbf{C}}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\mathbf{H})/\mathbf{Z}_{2}
, there is a gauge transformation of
L|_{U}
which maps
\Psi
to
\operatorname{Re}({\mathbf{S}})
.
This gauge transformation is unique up to
\pm 1
.
Change the trivialization by that gauge transformation.
In the new trivialization, let

A={\rm d}-a^{*}+a\quad\text{with }a\in\Omega^{0,1}(U),

so that

D_{A,\Upsilon}=D_{\Upsilon}+\gamma(a)\quad\text{with }\gamma(a)=\begin{pmatrix}0&a^{*}\\
a&0\end{pmatrix}.

Since
D_{\Upsilon}
preserves
\operatorname{Re}({\mathbf{S}})
and
\gamma(a)
maps
\operatorname{Re}({\mathbf{S}})
to
\operatorname{Im}({\mathbf{S}})
, equation
D_{A,\Upsilon}\Psi=0
implies that
a=0
.
Therefore, locally
A
agrees with the spin connection on
S
.
Globally, by patching these local trivializations together, we obtain a real line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}\to C{\backslash}B
with monodromy
\pm 1
, together with an isomorphism
\rho\colon W|_{C{\backslash}B}\to S|_{C{\backslash}B}\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}
which is covariantly constant with respect to
A
and such that
\Psi_{\mathbf{R}}=(\mathrm{id}_{E}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}\rho)\Psi
is real.
The other three properties follow from the analogous properties of
\Psi
.
It follows from
4.11
that
B
is finite.
∎

Proof of
Theorem 5.45
.

The proof is the same as that of
Theorem 5.35
.
Given
(\Upsilon,\eta)
as above, we need to show that the moduli space of solutions
{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},\eta,d)
is compact and consists of irreducible solutions, in which case
\#{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},\eta,d)\in\mathbf{Z}
is well-defined and invariant under deformations
({\mathfrak{d}}_{t},\eta_{t})
with the same properties.
If
(A,\Psi)
is a solution to
(5.43)
, it follows from the second and third equation (by integrating) that for
\eta\neq 0
we have
\Psi\neq 0
.
Therefore, every solution in
\#{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},\eta,d)
is irreducible.
To prove that
{M}_{1,2}({\mathfrak{d}},\eta,d)
is compact if
{\mathfrak{d}}\notin{W}
we proceed in the same way as in the proof of
Theorem 5.35
, using
Theorem 5.28
and
5.36
which relates limiting configurations to the
(2,1)
ADHM vortex equations to elements of
\ker{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
for some ramified Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{l}}
.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of the index formula for
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}

Here is the extension of
Theorem 3.44
promised in
3.45
.

A.1
.

Let
C
be a closed connected Riemann surface.
Let
V
be a complex vector bundle over
C
.
If

{\mathfrak{d}}
is a real Cauchy–Riemann operator on
V
and

{\mathfrak{l}}
is a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
,
then

{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{C},V\otimes{\mathfrak{l}}\rparen
is Fredholm with

\ind{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}=2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)-\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V.

The discussion in
Section 3.4.2
already shows that
{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathfrak{l}}
is Fredholm.
The proof of the index formula relies on the following.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
{\mathfrak{l}}
is a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
.

(1)

\mathring{{L}}\coloneqq{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}
be a holomorphic line bundle over
\mathring{C}
(with Dolbeault operator obtained by twisting the trivial Dolbeault operator by
{\mathfrak{l}}
).

(2)

The unique(!) orientation-preserving isometry
{\mathfrak{l}}^{2}\cong{\underline{\mathbf{R}}}
determines a
\mathring{\beta}\in{\rm H}^{0}\lparen\mathring{C},\mathring{{L}}^{2}\rparen
.

(3)

\mathring{{L}}
extends to a holomorphic line bundle
{L}
over
C
such that:

\mathring{\beta}
extends to
\beta\in{\rm H}^{0}(C,{L}^{2})
, and

\beta
is transverse to the zero section;
moreover:
{L}
is unique up to unique isomorphism.

(4)

If
\bar{\partial}
is a Dolbeault operator on
V
and
{V}
denotes the corresponding holomorphic vector bundle,
then

\ker\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{l}}\cong{\rm H}^{0}\lparen C,{V}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}(-\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\rparen\cong{\rm H}^{0}\lparen C,{V}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}^{*}\rparen.

Proof.

(1)
and
(2)
are obvious.

It suffices to prove
(3)
for the ramified Euclidean line bundle
{\mathfrak{m}}
over
D
from the proof of
3.33
.
The isomorphism

\tau\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\cong\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}
of complex line bundles defined by

\tau\lparen(z,w)\otimes\lambda\rparen\coloneqq\lparen z,\lambda w\rparen
is an isomorphism of holomorphic line bundles.
Identifying
{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}=\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}
and
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}^{\otimes 2}=\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}
,

\mathring{\beta}(z)=z\in{\rm H}^{0}\lparen\mathring{D},\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}\rparen
.
Evidently,
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}
and
\mathring{\beta}
extend from
\mathring{D}
to
D
.

To prove
(4)
,
observe that
if
s\in\ker\lparen\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{l}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace W^{1,2}\Gamma\lparen\mathring{D},{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\rparen\to L^{2}\Omega^{0,1}\lparen\mathring{D},{\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\rparen\rparen
,
then

s(z)=z^{1/2}\otimes\lparen z^{1/2}\cdot f(z)\rparen

with
f\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace D\to{\mathbf{C}}
holomorphic;
therefore:
\tau_{*}s(z)=zf(z)
.
Consequently, the obvious
inclusion
\ker\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{l}}\hookrightarrow{\rm H}^{0}\lparen C,{V}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}\rparen
factors through an isomorphism

\ker\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{l}}\cong{\rm H}^{0}\lparen C,{V}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}(-\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}))\rparen.

Since
\beta
vanishes transversely in
\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
,

{L}^{2}\cong{O}_{\mathbf{C}}(\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}))
.
Therefore,

{L}(-\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}))\cong{L}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}({L}^{2})^{*}\cong{L}^{*}.\qed

Proof of
Theorem A.1
: index formula.

By
3.3
,
it suffices to prove the index formula if
{\mathfrak{d}}=\bar{\partial}
is a Dolbeault operator.
Denote by
{V}
the holomorphic vector bundle corresponding to
(V,\bar{\partial})
.
By Riemann–Roch and with
{L}
as in
A.2

(3)
,

\dim{\rm H}^{0}(C,{V}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}^{*})-\dim{\rm H}^{1}(C,{V}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}^{*})=2\deg V+\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V\cdot\chi(C)-\#\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})\cdot\rk_{\mathbf{C}}V.

By
A.2

(4)
,

\ker\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{l}}\cong{\rm H}^{0}\lparen C,{V}\otimes{L}^{*}\rparen
.
By Serre duality,

{\rm H}^{1}(C,{V}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}^{*})={\rm H}^{0}(C,{V}^{\dagger}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}^{*}(\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}))

with
{V}^{\dagger}\coloneqq{V}^{*}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}K_{C}
.

{\rm H}^{0}(C,{V}^{\dagger}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}^{*}(\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}))
consists of meromorphic sections of
{V}^{\dagger}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}^{*}
with at most simple poles at
\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})
.
Therefore,
there is an inclusion

{\rm H}^{0}(C,{V}^{\dagger}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}^{*}(\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}}))\hookrightarrow\ker\bar{\partial}_{L^{2}}^{\dagger,{\mathfrak{l}}}.

In fact,
3.49
implies that this map is an isomorphism.
This proves the index formula.
∎

A.3
.

A.2
has a partial converse:
if
{L}
is a holomorphic bundle over
C
and
\beta\in{\rm H}^{0}(C,{L}^{2})
is transverse to the zero section,
then

{\mathfrak{l}}\coloneqq\{(x,v)\in{L}:v^{2}\in\mathbf{R}_{\geqslant 0}\cdot\beta(x)\}

with the Euclidean inner product
\langle(x,v),(x,w)\rangle\coloneqq\frac{vw}{\beta(x)}
is a ramified Euclidean line bundle over
C
with
\mathrm{Br}({\mathfrak{l}})=Z(\beta)
.

A.4
.

Here is a description of holomorphic maps
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
of degree two (alternative to
3.33
and familiar from algebraic geometry):

(1)

If
{L}
is a holomorphic bundle over
C
and
\beta\in{\rm H}^{0}(C,{L}^{2})
is transverse to the zero section,
then

\tilde{C}\coloneqq\{(x,v)\in{L}:v^{2}=\beta(x)\}

is a Riemann surface, and

\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
defined by
\pi(x,v)\coloneqq x
is a holomorphic map of degree two.
A moment’s thought reveals that

(A.5)

\pi_{*}{O}_{\tilde{C}}={O}_{C}\oplus{L}^{*}.

The summands are (anti-)invariant under the action of the involution
\tau
of
\tilde{C}
defined by
\tau(x,v)\coloneqq(x,-v)
.

(2)

If
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to C
is a holomorphic map of degree two,
then it is a branched double cover.
In particular, there is an involution
\tau
of
\tilde{C}
which swaps the sheets of
\pi
.
Consider the anti-invariant subbundle

{L}^{*}\coloneqq\lparen\pi_{*}{O}_{\tilde{C}}\rparen^{-}.

Set
{L}\coloneqq({L}^{*})^{*}
.
The holomorphic section
\beta\in{\rm H}^{0}(C,{L}^{2})
defined by

\beta(x)(\lambda^{2})\coloneqq\lparen\lambda(\tilde{x})\rparen^{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}\quad\textnormal{for}\quad\tilde{x}\in\pi^{-1}(x)

is transverse to the zero section.
Moreover,
the map
\phi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace\tilde{C}\to\{(x,v)\in{L}:v^{2}=\beta(x)\}
defined by
\phi(\tilde{x})\coloneqq(\pi(\tilde{x}),\mathrm{ev}_{\tilde{x}})

is biholomorphic.

A.2
and
A.3

bridge the above discussion and
3.33
.
Moreover,

(A.5)
provides a further (perspective on the) proof of
A.2

(4)
:

\ker\bar{\partial}^{\mathfrak{l}}\cong\lparen\ker\pi^{*}\bar{\partial}\rparen^{-}\cong{\rm H}^{0}\lparen\tilde{C},\pi^{*}{V}\rparen^{-}\cong{\rm H}^{0}\lparen C,{V}\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}{L}^{*}\rparen.

Appendix B
Local wall-crossing formulae

The following explains the analogue of
2.4
if
{W}\subset{P}
is a proper wall.

B.1
.

Let
{P}
be a Banach manifold.
Let
{W}\subset{P}
be a proper wall in
{P}
.
Let
\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}\to{P}
,
\nu\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{N}\to{E}
and
\sigma\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{S}\to{E}
be as in
2.6
.
Set

{W}^{*}\coloneqq{W}{\backslash}{B}\quad\text{with}\quad{B}\coloneqq\left\lparen\im(\pi\circ\nu)\cup\im(\pi\circ\sigma)\cup\pi(\mathrm{Crit}(\pi))\right\rparen.

Denote by
{\mathfrak{o}}
the local system on
{W}^{*}
induced by the local system
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}
of orientations of
\operatorname{det}T\pi
on
{E}
.
The following hold:

(1)

There is a homomorphism

\sharp\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{\rm H}^{0}({W}^{*};G\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathfrak{o}})\to\Hom({\rm H}_{1}({P},{P}{\backslash}{W}),G)\cong{\rm H}^{1}({P},{P}{\backslash}{W};G)

such that
(2.5)
holds for every
C^{1}
path
{\mathbf{p}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace[0,1]\to{P}
is a
C^{1}
with
{\mathbf{p}}(0),{\mathbf{p}}(1)\notin{W}
and transverse to
\pi
,
\pi\circ\nu
, and
\pi\circ\sigma
.
(The sum in
(2.5)
is finite because of
2.7
.)

(2)

There is an injective homomorphism

\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta_{\mathrm{loc}}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\rm H}^{0}({W}^{*};G\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathfrak{o}})

such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm H}^{0}({W}^{*};G\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathfrak{o}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm H}^{1}({P},{P}{\backslash}{W};G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\mathrm{loc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sharp}

(3)

\Delta_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\sharp
assemble into the exact sequence

\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta_{\mathrm{loc}}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}{\rm H}^{0}({W};G\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathfrak{o}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sharp}}{{\to}}{\rm H}^{1}({P};G).

B.2
.

The homomorphism
\Delta_{\mathrm{loc}}
can be computed as follows.
Let
p\in{W}^{*}
.
Choose a
C^{1}
path
{\mathbf{p}}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace[-1,1]\to{P}
transverse to
\pi
,
\pi\circ\nu
, and
\pi\circ\sigma
.
There is an
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
such that
{\mathbf{p}}^{-1}({W})\cap[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]=0
and

\Delta_{\mathrm{loc}}([{\rm C}{\rm I}])(p)=\Delta([{\rm C}{\rm I}])([{\mathbf{p}}])={\rm C}{\rm I}({\mathbf{p}}(\varepsilon))-{\rm C}{\rm I}({\mathbf{p}}(-\varepsilon)).

B.3
.

An exercise in sheaf theory constructs a sheaf
{F}
on
{W}
and an exact sequence with
{\rm H}^{0}({W};{F})
in place of
{\rm H}^{0}({W}^{*};G\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}}{\mathfrak{o}})
without any hypothesis on
{W}
.
From this perspective, the point of
Theorem B.1
is to give a manageable description of this wall-crossing sheaf
{F}
assuming
{W}
is a proper wall in
{P}
.

Theorem B.1
would follow from
2.4
if
{B}
were closed.
Since
{B}
might not be closed,
the proof of
Theorem B.1
requires the following preparations.

Lemma B.4
.

Assume the situation of
Theorem B.1
.
Let
p\in{W}^{*}
.
Set
\{e\}\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(p)
.
There are
open neighborhoods
p\in U\subset{P}
,
e\in V\subset{E}
such that:

(1)

\pi|_{V}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace V\to U
is an embedding.

(2)

Every connected component of
\pi(V)
intersects
{W}^{*}
.

(3)

\pi^{-1}(U){\backslash}V\subset\im\nu\cup\im\sigma
.

Proof.

Since
T_{e}\pi\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace T_{e}{E}\to T_{p}{P}
is injective,
there are open neighborhoods
p\in U\subset{P}
,
e\in V\subset{E}
such that
(1)
and
(2)
holds.

If
(3)
cannot be arranged to hold by shrinking
U
and
V
,
then there is a sequence
(e_{n})
in
{E}{\backslash}(\im\nu\cup\im\sigma)
which does not converge to
e
but with
(\pi(e_{n}))
converging to
p
.
Since
\pi|_{{E}{\backslash}(\im\nu\cup\im\sigma)}\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{E}{\backslash}(\im\nu\cup\im\sigma)\to{P}{\backslash}\lparen\im\lparen\pi\circ\nu\rparen\cup\im\lparen\pi\circ\sigma\rparen\rparen
is proper,
after passing to a subsequence,

(e_{n})
converges to
e^{\prime}\in{E}
.
Since
\pi(e^{\prime})=p
and
\pi|_{{E}{\backslash}(\im\nu\cup\im\sigma)}
is injective,

e^{\prime}=e
: a contradiction.
∎

Proof of
Theorem B.1
.

Set
{P}\coloneqq{P}{\backslash}{B}
.
By the Sard–Smale Theorem,

{\rm H}^{0}({P};G)\to{\rm H}^{0}({P}^{*};G)
is an isomorphism.
Therefore,

\mathrm{WCF}({P},{W};G)\cong\mathrm{WCF}({P}^{*},{W}^{*};G).

Let
\{U_{\alpha}\subset{P}:\alpha\in A\}
be a set of open subsets with
{W}^{*}\subset\bigcup_{\alpha\in A}U_{\alpha}
.
Set
U_{\alpha}^{*}\coloneqq U_{\alpha}{\backslash}{B}
.
Since
{W}
is closed,
\{{P}{\backslash}{W}\}\cup\{U_{\alpha}\subset{P}:\alpha\in A\}
is an open cover of
{P}^{*}
.
A diagram chase in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm H}^{0}({P}^{*};G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm H}^{0}({P}^{*}{\backslash}{W}^{*};G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{WCF}({P}^{*},{W}^{*};G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{\alpha\in A}{\rm H}^{0}(U_{\alpha}^{*};G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{\alpha\in A}{\rm H}^{0}(U_{\alpha}^{*}{\backslash}{W}^{*};G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{\alpha\in A}\coker\lparen{\rm H}^{0}(U_{\alpha}^{*};G)\to{\rm H}^{0}(U_{\alpha}^{*}{\backslash}{W}^{*};G)\rparen}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{\alpha,\beta\in A}{\rm H}^{0}(U_{\alpha\beta}^{*};G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{\alpha,\beta\in A}{\rm H}^{0}(U_{\alpha\beta}^{*}{\backslash}{W}^{*};G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}}

reveals that
\tilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
is injective.

By

,
it is possible to choose

and

such that

,

,

, and

.

.
By

,
this induces an injective linear map

Appendix C
Spinors and quaternions

This appendix summarizes algebraic properties of spinors in dimension
3
used in
Section 5
.

Let
S
be the spinor representation of
\mathrm{Spin}(3)
.
We identify
S
with the space of quaternions
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathrm{Spin}(3)
with the group of unit quaternions
\mathrm{Sp}(1)\subset\mathbf{H}
in the following way.
The double cover
\mathrm{Sp}(1)\to\mathrm{SO}(3)
is given by the right action of
\mathrm{Sp}(1)
on imaginary quaternions
\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{R}^{3}
by
q(x)=\bar{q}^{-1}xq
.
The right action of
\mathrm{Sp}(1)
on
S=\mathbf{H}
is by right quaternionic multiplication.
It preserves three complex structures given by left multiplication by
i,j,k
.
The Clifford multiplication
\gamma\colon\mathbf{R}^{3}\to\End(S)
is given by

\gamma(e_{1})x=xi,\quad\gamma(e_{2})x=xj,\quad\gamma(e_{3})x=xk.

With these conventions, the Clifford multiplication by the volume form is

\gamma(e_{1}\wedge e_{2}\wedge e_{3})=\gamma(e_{1})\gamma(e_{2})\gamma(e_{3})=kji=1.

Fix the complex structure on
\mathbf{H}
given by left multiplication by
i
.
We have a complex isomorphism

\displaystyle{\mathbf{C}}^{2}\to\mathbf{H}

\displaystyle(z,w)\mapsto z+wj.

With respect to this identification, left multiplication by
j
is

j(z,w)=(-\bar{w},\bar{z}),

and the action of
\mathrm{Sp}(1)
agrees with the standard right
representation of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
.
The Clifford multiplication is given by the Pauli matrices

\gamma(e_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\
0&-i\end{pmatrix},\quad\gamma(e_{2})=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\gamma(e_{3})=\begin{pmatrix}0&i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix}.

The Dirac operator acting on maps
\mathbf{R}^{3}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
is

D=\gamma(e_{1})\partial_{1}+\gamma(e_{2})\partial_{2}+\gamma(e_{3})\partial_{3}

We can relate the spinors in dimension
3
to spinors in dimension
2
by considering the subgroup
\mathrm{U}(1)\subset\mathrm{Sp}(1)
of unit complex numbers.
The right action of
\mathrm{U}(1)
on
\mathbf{H}={\mathbf{C}}^{2}
is

\lambda(z,w)=(\lambda z,\lambda^{-1}w)\quad\text{for }\lambda\in\mathrm{U}(1).

The restriction of the right action of
\mathrm{Sp}(1)
on
\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{H}
to
\mathrm{U}(1)
is given by

\lambda(x_{1}i+x_{2}j+x_{3}k)=x_{1}i+\lambda^{-2}x_{2}j+\lambda^{-2}x_{3}k,

so if we identify

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}\oplus{\mathbf{C}}=\operatorname{Im}\mathbf{H},

\displaystyle(t,z)\mapsto ti+zj,

then the action of
\mathrm{U}(1)
is

\lambda(t,z)=(t,\lambda^{-2}z),

Proposition C.1
.

Writing a map
\mathbf{R}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{2}
as a pair of maps
\mathbf{R}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
and using coordinates
t
on
\mathbf{R}
and
z=x+iy
on
{\mathbf{C}}
, the Dirac operator is

D=\begin{pmatrix}i\partial_{t}&-\partial_{x}+i\partial_{y}\\
\partial_{x}+i\partial_{y}&-i\partial_{t}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}i\partial_{t}&-\partial_{z}\\
\bar{\partial}_{z}&-i\partial_{t}\end{pmatrix}.

The following facts about real structures are used in the discussion of the
(2,1)
ADHM vortex equation in
Section 5.6
,
Let
E
and
S
be quaternionic Hermitian vector spaces of quaternionic dimension one.
Their complex tensor product has a real structure
\tau\colon E\otimes S\to E\otimes S
, that is a complex antilinear map such that
\tau^{2}=\mathrm{id}
, given by

\tau=j_{E}\otimes j_{S},

where
j_{E}
and
j_{S}
are multiplication by
j
on
E
and
S
.
Let

\operatorname{Re}(E\otimes S)=\{\psi\in E\otimes S:\tau(\psi)=\psi\}.

be the real part of
E\otimes S
.
As a real vector space
E\otimes S
decomposes into

E\otimes S=\operatorname{Re}(E\otimes S)\oplus i\operatorname{Re}(E\otimes S).

Let
S=S^{+}\oplus S^{-}
for a complex line
S^{+}
and
S^{-}=(S^{+})^{*}
, with the quaternionic structure given by

\displaystyle j_{S}\colon S^{+}\oplus S^{-}\to S^{+}\oplus S^{-}

\displaystyle j_{S}(\alpha,\beta)=(-\beta^{*},\alpha^{*})

with the star denoting the metric dual
S^{\pm}\to S^{\mp}
with respect to the Hermitian inner product.
Extend
j_{E}\colon E\to E
to complex antilinear maps

\overline{\gamma}\colon E\otimes S^{\pm}\to E\otimes S^{\mp}

by taking tensor product with the metric dual.
They satisfy
\overline{\gamma}^{2}=-\mathrm{id}
.
The real structure is

\displaystyle\tau\colon(E\otimes S^{+})\oplus(E\otimes S^{-})\to(E\otimes S^{+})\oplus(E\otimes S^{-})

\displaystyle\tau=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\overline{\gamma}\\
\overline{\gamma}&0.\end{pmatrix}

Proposition C.2
.

(1)

We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}(E\otimes S)

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in(E\otimes S^{+})\oplus(E\otimes S^{-}):\beta=\overline{\gamma}(\alpha)\},

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in(E\otimes S^{+})\oplus(E\otimes S^{-}):\alpha=-\overline{\gamma}(\beta)\},

and the projections
\operatorname{Re}(E\otimes S)\to E\otimes S^{\pm}
are isomorphisms of
\mathbf{R}
–vector spaces.

(2)

A
{\mathbf{C}}
–linear map
\Upsilon\colon E\otimes S\to E\otimes S
which commutes with
\tau
is uniquely determined by the induced
\mathbf{R}
–linear map

\Upsilon_{\mathbf{R}}\colon E\otimes S^{+}\cong\operatorname{Re}(E\otimes S)\to\operatorname{Re}(E\otimes S)\cong E\otimes S^{-}.

Moreover,
\Upsilon
is self-adjoint with respect to the Hermitian inner product on
E\otimes S
if and only if
\overline{\gamma}\Upsilon_{\mathbf{R}}
is self-adjoint with respect to the Euclidean inner product on
E\otimes S^{+}
.

Proof.

Ageneral map
P\colon E\otimes S\to E\otimes S
can be written as

\Upsilon=\begin{pmatrix}\Upsilon_{+}^{+}&\Upsilon_{-}^{+}\\
\Upsilon_{+}^{-}&\Upsilon_{-}^{-}\end{pmatrix}

with respect to decomposition
S=S^{+}\oplus S^{-}
.
Such a map preserves
\tau
if and only if

\Upsilon_{+}^{+}\overline{\gamma}=\overline{\gamma}\Upsilon_{-}^{-}\quad\text{and}\quad\Upsilon_{-}^{+}\overline{\gamma}=-\overline{\gamma}\Upsilon_{+}^{-}.

The induced real operator is

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mathbf{R}}\colon E\otimes S^{+}\to E\otimes S^{-},

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mathbf{R}}=\Upsilon_{+}^{-}+\Upsilon_{-}^{-}\overline{\gamma}.

If
\Upsilon
is
{\mathbf{C}}
–linear then it can be reconstructed from
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{R}}
in the following way.
Decompose
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{R}}\colon E\otimes S^{+}\to E\otimes S^{-}
into
{\mathbf{C}}
–linear and
{\mathbf{C}}
–antilinear parts
\Upsilon_{\mathbf{R}}=\Upsilon_{c}+\Upsilon_{a}
.
Then

\Upsilon=\begin{pmatrix}-\overline{\gamma}\Upsilon_{a}&\overline{\gamma}\Upsilon_{c}\overline{\gamma}\\
\Upsilon_{c}&-\Upsilon_{a}\overline{\gamma}\end{pmatrix}.\qed

Appendix D
Compactness for generalized Seiberg–Witten equations

The quaternionic vortex equation is a dimensional reduction of the three-dimensional generalized Seiberg–Witten equation over
M=C\times S^{1}

[
Doa17
, Section 3]
.
We will prove
Theorem 5.28
by extending the compactness theorem for generalized Seiberg–Witten equations
[
WZ21
]
.
Unfortunately, this extension requires discussing some technical details of
[
WZ21
]
; the method of the proof still works but some of the estimates and statements have to be adjusted.
Rather than rewriting the full proof, we will assume that the reader is familiar with
[
WZ21
]
and comment on the necessary modifications.
In particular, we will use the notation introduced in
[
WZ21
, Section 1.1]
and cite propositions, theorems, equations, or definitions with a number x.xx from
[
WZ21
]
simply as [WZ x.xx].

Throughout this section, let
\rho\colon G\to\mathrm{Sp}(V)
be a quaternionic representation as in
Section 5.1
.
Moreover, we assume
5.3
,
5.7
, and
5.26
.
Let
M
be a closed oriented
3
-manifold.
Given a spin
H
structure on
M
, we associate with it a principal
G
-bundle
P\to M
and a vector bundle
{\mathbf{V}}\to M
with fiber
V
, similarly to the
2
-dimensional construction in
Section 5.1
.
A connection
A
on
P
defines the induced Dirac operator

D_{A}\colon\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})

and we have a quaternionic moment map

\mu\colon{\mathbf{V}}\to\Lambda^{2}T^{*}M\otimes\ad P.

For details, see
[
WZ21
, Section 1.1]
.

Definition D.1
.

The
generalized Seiberg–Witten equation
for a triple

A\in{A}(P),\quad\Psi\in\Gamma({\mathbf{V}}),\quad\zeta\in\Gamma(\ad P)

reads

\begin{split}&(D_{A}+\rho(\zeta))\Psi=0,\\
&F_{A}+\ast{\rm d}_{A}\zeta=\mu(\Psi).\end{split}

The following perturbation of the equation is relevant to the discussion of quaternionic vortex equations.

Definition D.2
.

Let
{G}
be the gauge group of
P
.
Suppose that
\Upsilon\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\End({\mathbf{V}})
is
{G}
-invariant,
\eta\mskip 0.5mu\colon\thinspace{A}(P)\times\Gamma({\mathbf{V}})\to\Omega^{2}(\ad P)
is
{G}
-equivariant, and

(1)

\Upsilon(A,\Psi)
is self-adjoint and commutes with the action of the gauge group,

(2)

{\rm d}_{A}\eta(A,\Psi)=0
,

(3)

|\Upsilon(A,\Psi)|,|\nabla_{A}\Upsilon(A,\Psi)|,|\eta(A,\Psi)|,|\nabla_{A}\eta(A,\Psi)|
are uniformly bounded.

(Note that
\nabla_{A}\Upsilon=\nabla_{A_{0}}\Upsilon
for any fixed connection
A_{0}
because
\Upsilon
commutes with
\ad P
.)

The
(\Upsilon,\eta)
–perturbed generalized Seiberg–Witten equation
for
(A,\Psi,\zeta)
reads

(D.3)

\begin{split}&(D_{A}+\Upsilon(A,\Psi)+\rho(\zeta))\Psi=0,\\
&F_{A}+\ast{\rm d}_{A}\zeta+\eta(A,\Psi)=\mu(\Psi).\end{split}

It is necessary to include the field
\zeta
to make the equation elliptic modulo the action of the gauge group.
However, the next proposition shows that without loss of generality we may assume that
\zeta=0
.

Proposition D.4
.

If
(A,\Psi,\zeta)
satisfies
(D.3)
, then

\rho(\zeta)\Psi=0\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm d}_{A}\zeta=0.

In particular, if
(A,\Psi)
is irreducible, then
\zeta=0
.

Proof.

Apply
{\rm d}_{A}
to the second equation.
By the Bianchi identity and
[
DW20
, Proposition B.4]
,

{\rm d}_{A}^{*}{\rm d}_{A}\zeta-\rho^{*}((D_{A}\Psi)\Psi^{*})=0.

Therefore, by the first equation,

{\rm d}_{A}^{*}{\rm d}_{A}\zeta+\rho^{*}((\rho(\zeta)\Psi)\Psi^{*}+(\Upsilon\Psi)\Psi^{*})=0.

Taking the inner product with
\zeta
and integrating yields

\|{\rm d}_{A}\zeta\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\rho(\zeta)\Psi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\langle\Upsilon\Psi,\rho(\zeta)\Psi\rangle_{L^{2}}=0.

The third term is zero because
\rho(\zeta)\Upsilon
is a skew-adjoint operator:

(\rho(\zeta)\Upsilon)^{*}=\Upsilon^{*}\rho(\zeta)^{*}=-\Upsilon\rho(\zeta)=-\rho(\zeta)\Upsilon.\qed

Set
\zeta=0
in
(D.3)
.
If
\Psi\neq 0
, then after rescaling the equation by
\varepsilon=\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}^{-1}
we can write it as an equation for the triple
(A,\Psi,\varepsilon)
:

(D.5)

\begin{split}&(D_{A}+\Upsilon(A,\Psi))\Psi=0,\\
&\varepsilon^{2}(F_{A}+\eta(A,\Psi))=\mu(\Psi),\\
&\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}=1.\end{split}

The following generalizes the compactness theorem
[
WZ21
, Theorem 1.28]
to the perturbation
(D.5)
of the generalized Seiberg–Witten equation.

Proposition D.6
.

If
(A_{n},\Psi_{n},\varepsilon_{n})
is a sequence of solutions of
(D.5)
, then, after passing to a subsequence and applying gauge transformations,
(A_{n},\Psi_{n},\varepsilon_{n})
converges in the
C^{\infty}
topology to a solution
(A,\Psi,\varepsilon)
of
(D.5)
.

D.7
.

If
(A_{n},\Psi_{n},\varepsilon_{n})
is a sequence of solutions to
(D.5)
such that Hypothesis 1.22 from
[
WZ21
]
holds and
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
, then there exist a closed, nowhere dense subset
Z\subset M
, a connection
A
over
M{\backslash}Z
, and section a
\Psi\in\Gamma(M{\backslash}Z,{\mathbf{V}})
with the following properties.

(1)

A
and
\Psi
satisfy

\displaystyle(D_{A}+\Upsilon(A,\Psi))\Psi=0,

\displaystyle\mu(\Psi)=0,

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}=1.

(2)

The function
|\Psi|
extends to a Hölder continuous on
M
such that
Z=|\Psi|^{-1}(0)
.

(3)

After passing to a subsequence and applying gauge transformations over
M{\backslash}Z
,

(a)

|\Psi_{n}|
converges to
|\Psi|
in the
C^{0,\alpha}
topology over
M
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

(b)

A_{n}
converges to
A
in the weak
W^{1,2}
topology over every compact subset of
M{\backslash}Z
,

(c)

\Psi_{n}
converges to
\Psi
in the weak
W^{2,2}
topology over every compact subset of
M{\backslash}Z
.

Theorem D.7
implies a compactness theorem for quaternionic vortex equations.

Proof of
Theorem 5.28
assuming
Theorem D.7
.

Let
M=S^{1}\times C
.
Without loss of generality assume that solutions
(A_{n},\Psi_{n})
on
C
are irreducible solutions such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}=\infty
.
Set
\hat{\Psi}_{n}=\Psi_{n}/\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}
.
Consider the pull-back of
(A_{n},\hat{\Psi}_{n})
to
S^{1}\times C
(which, by abuse of notation, we denote by the same symbols) as a sequence of solutions to

(D.5)
with
\varepsilon_{n}=1/\|\Psi_{n}\|_{L^{2}}

These solutions satisfy the
S^{1}
-invariance property

(D.8)

\nabla_{t}(A-A_{0})=0\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla_{t}\hat{\Psi}=0,

where
t
is the coordinate on
S^{1}
and
A_{0}
is a fixed connection pulled back from
C
.

By
Theorem D.7
, there exist a closed, nowhere dense subset
{Z}\subset M
, a connection
A
over
M{\backslash}{Z}
, and a section
\Psi\in\Gamma(M{\backslash}{Z},{\mathbf{V}})
with the following properties:

(1)

A
and
\Psi
satisfy

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}_{A,\Upsilon}\Psi=0,

\displaystyle\mu(\Psi)=0,

\displaystyle\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}=1,

where

{\mathbf{D}}_{A,\Upsilon}=i\nabla_{t}+D_{A}+\Upsilon(A,\Psi)

is the
3
-dimensional Dirac operator acting on sections of
{\mathbf{V}}
over
M
.

(2)

The function
|\Psi|
extends to a Hölder continuous on
M
such that
{Z}=|\Psi|^{-1}(0)
.

(3)

After passing to a subsequence and applying gauge transformations over
M{\backslash}{Z}
,

(a)

|\Psi_{n}|
converges to
|\Psi|
in the
C^{0,\alpha}
topology over
M
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

(b)

A_{n}
converges to
A
in the weak
W^{1,2}
topology over every compact subset of
M{\backslash}{Z}
,

(c)

\Psi_{n}
converges to
\Psi
in the weak
W^{2,2}
topology over every compact subset of
M{\backslash}{Z}
.

The weak convergence implies that for every
\hat{a}
and
\hat{\psi}
,

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{t}(A-A_{0}),\hat{a}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle\nabla_{t}(A_{n}-A_{0}),\hat{a}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{t}\Psi,\hat{\psi}\rangle_{L^{2}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle\nabla_{t}\Psi_{n},\hat{\psi}\rangle_{L^{2}}=0,

so that
(A,\Psi)
satisfy
(D.8)
and that
{Z}=Z\times S^{1}
for a closed subset
Z\subset C
.
After applying a gauge transformation which puts
A
in a temporal gauge,
(A,\Psi)
are pulled back from a configuration on
C
.
∎

In the remaining part of this section we discuss how to adapt the proof of
[
WZ21
, Theorem 1.28]
to the perturbed equation.
The main tool in the proof of
[
WZ21
, Theorem 1.28]
is the Lichnerowicz–Weitzenböck formula.
The perturbation terms
\Upsilon=\Upsilon(A,\Psi)
and
\eta=\eta(A,\Psi)
contribute to new terms in this formula.

Proposition D.9
.

For every
(A,\Psi)
,

(D_{A}+\Upsilon)^{2}\Psi=\nabla_{A}^{*}\nabla_{A}\Psi+\gamma(F_{A})\Psi+{\mathfrak{R}}\Psi+D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi)+\Upsilon(D_{A}+\Upsilon)\Psi,

where
{\mathfrak{R}}
is the zeroth order operator defined in
[
WZ21
, Definition2.1]
and depending on the Riemann curvature of
M
.
In particular, if
(A,\Psi)
is a solution to
(D.5)
, then

\nabla_{A}^{*}\nabla_{A}\Psi-\gamma(\eta)\Psi+\varepsilon^{-2}\gamma(\mu(\Psi))\Psi+{\mathfrak{R}}\Psi+D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi)=0,

Proof.

We have

0=(D_{A}+\Upsilon)^{2}\Psi=D_{A}^{2}\Psi+D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi)+\Upsilon(D_{A}+\Upsilon)\Psi

and the formula follows from the standard Weitzenböck formula for the Dirac operator and the second equation in
(D.5)
, see
[
WZ21
, Proposition 2.2]
.
∎

The new term involving
\eta
is harmless.
The term involving
\Upsilon
is more problematic at it involes the first derivative of
\Psi
.
It can be estimated pointwise using Young’s inequality

(D.10)

|D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi)|\lesssim\delta|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+\delta^{-1}|\Psi|^{2}

for any
\delta>0
.
Alternatively, using integration by parts, we can trade the integral of the derivative term for a boundary integral.

Proposition D.11
.

If
(A,\Psi)
satisfies

(D_{A}+\Upsilon)\Psi=0,

then for every open set
U\subset M
with smooth boundary

\int_{U}\langle D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi),\Psi\rangle\lesssim\int_{U}|\Psi|^{2}+\int_{\partial U}|\Psi|^{2}.

Proof.

Let
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
be a local orthonormal frame of
TM
and set
\nabla_{A,i}=\nabla_{A,e_{i}}
.

\displaystyle\int_{U}\langle D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi),\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}\int_{U}\langle\nabla_{A,i}(\Upsilon\Psi),\gamma(e_{i})\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int_{U}\langle\Upsilon\Psi,\nabla_{A,i}(\gamma(e_{i})\Psi)\rangle-\sum_{i}\int_{\partial U}\langle\Upsilon\Psi,\gamma(e_{i})\Psi\rangle(e_{i}\lrcorner\mathrm{vol})

\displaystyle=\int_{U}\langle\Upsilon\Psi,D_{A}\Psi+R\Psi\rangle-\sum_{i}\int_{\partial U}\langle\Upsilon\Psi,\gamma(e_{i})\Psi\rangle(e_{i}\lrcorner\mathrm{vol})

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{U}|\Psi|^{2}+\int_{\partial U}|\Psi|^{2},

where
R=\gamma(\sum_{i}\nabla_{i}e_{i})
is a Riemannian curvature term and in the last line we use
D_{A}\Psi=-\Upsilon\Psi
and
|\Upsilon|\leqslant C
.
∎

In what follows, let
(A,\Psi,\varepsilon)
be a solution of
(D.5)
.
In the case
\Upsilon=0
and
\eta=0
, section 2 of
[
WZ21
]
establishes a priori bounds on
A
and
\Psi
in terms of the frequency function defined below.

Definition D.12
.

Let
r_{0}
be the injectivity radius of
M
.
Given
x\in M
and
r\in(0,r_{0}]
let
B_{r}(x)\subset M
be the geodesic ball of radius
r
centered at
x
.
Define

\displaystyle m_{x}(r)=\frac{1}{4\pi r^{2}}\int_{\partial B_{r}(x)}|\Psi|^{2},

\displaystyle D_{x}(r)=\frac{1}{4\pi r}\int_{B_{r}(x)}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+2\varepsilon^{-2}|\mu(\Psi)|^{2}.

The
frequency function
associated with
(A,\Psi,\varepsilon)
is

N_{x}(r)=\frac{D_{x}(r)}{m_{x}(r)}.

Proposition D.13
.

Let
(A,\Psi,\varepsilon)
be a solution of
(D.5)
.
The a priori bounds on
A
and
\Psi
in terms of
m
and
N
from
[
WZ21
, Propositions 2.9–2.23]
, proved in the case
\Upsilon=0
and
\eta=0
, hold also in the general case.

Proof.

Most of the proofs in
[
WZ21
, Section 2]
rely on the Lichnerowicz–Weitzenböck formula, and therefore are affected by the new terms appearing in
D.9
.
The new terms are estimated using the bounds on
\eta
and
\Upsilon
,
(D.10)
,
D.11
, and rearranging some of the inequalities.
Below we comment on the necessary modifications in the proof of every estimate in
[
WZ21
, Section 2]
.

[WZ 2.9]

The Lichnerowicz–Weitzenbock formula from
D.9
produces extra terms corresponding to
\Upsilon
and
\eta
, which can be estimated using
(D.10)
,
D.11
, and the
L^{\infty}
bound on
\eta
.
This leads to

\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}r}\int_{\partial B_{r}(x)}|\Psi|^{2}\geqslant\int_{\partial B_{r}(x)}\left(\frac{2}{r}-c_{1}r-c_{2}\right)|\Psi|^{2}+\int_{B_{r}(x)}(2-c_{3}r^{2})\int_{B_{r}(x)}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2},

for constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
.
The right-hand side is positive for small
r
, which completes the proof.

[WZ 2.10]

In this proof, we multiply the Lichnerowicz–Weitzenböck formula by the function
\chi^{2}G_{y}
where
\chi
is a cut-off function and
G_{y}=G(y,x)
with
G
being the Green kernel for the Laplacian on
B_{r}(x)
and
y\in B_{r/2}(x)
.

D.9
again produces two extra terms:

\int_{B_{r}(x)}\chi^{2}G_{y}\langle\gamma(\eta)\Psi,\Psi\rangle\leqslant cr^{2}\|\chi\Psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2},

and, using (
D.10
),

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(x)}\chi^{2}G_{y}\langle D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi),\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta\int_{B_{r}(x)}\chi^{2}G_{y}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+\delta^{-1}\int_{B_{r}(x)}\chi^{2}G_{y}|\Psi|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\delta\int_{B_{r}(x)}\chi^{2}G_{y}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+\delta^{-1}r^{2}\|\chi\Psi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}.

For
\delta
sufficiently small, the first term on the right-hand side can be rearranged and absorbed by the integral

\int_{B_{r}(x)}\chi^{2}G_{y}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}

which appears on the left-hand side of the main inequality in the proof of Proposition 2.10.

[WZ 2.11]

The original proof uses [WZ 2.12], [WZ 2.14], and
[
Doa17
, Proposition B.4]
.
While [WZ 2.14] is independent of the equation, the other two formulae obtain additional terms from the Lichnerowicz–Weitzenbock formula and the
\eta
term in
F_{A}=\mu(\Psi)-\eta
.
To prove the estimate, we arrange the term in a different way than in the proof of [WZ 2.12].
We use the following convention:
R
is any remainder term satisfying

|R|\lesssim|\Psi|+|\nabla_{A}\Psi|

and
A\ast B
denotes any bilinear algebraic operation involving tensors
A
and
B
.
Both
R
and
\ast
can denote different terms and operations in different lines.
By [WZ 2.14],

[\nabla_{A}^{*}\nabla_{A},\nabla_{A}]\Psi=\rho({\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A})\Psi+F_{A}\ast\nabla_{A}\Psi+R.

Therefore, by
D.9
,

(D.14)

\begin{split}\nabla_{A}^{*}\nabla_{A}\nabla_{A}\Psi&=[\nabla_{A}^{*}\nabla_{A},\nabla_{A}]\Psi-\gamma(F_{A})\nabla_{A}\Psi-\gamma(\nabla_{A}F_{A})\Psi-\nabla_{A}(D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi))+R\\
&=\rho({\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A})\Psi+F_{A}\ast\nabla_{A}\Psi-\gamma(F_{A})\nabla_{A}\Psi-\gamma(\nabla_{A}F_{A})\Psi-\nabla_{A}(D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi))+R.\end{split}

Next, we take the inner product (
D.14
) with
\nabla_{A}\Psi
and estimate all terms.
By
[
Doa17
, Proposition B.4]
,

\displaystyle\langle\rho({\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A})\Psi,\nabla_{A}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A},\rho^{*}(\nabla_{A}\Psi\Psi^{*})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle{\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A},{\rm d}_{A}^{*}\mu(\Psi)\rangle+\langle{\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A},D_{A}\Psi\ast\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{2}|{\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}\langle{\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A},\eta\rangle-\langle{\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A},\Upsilon\Psi\ast\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim-\varepsilon^{2}|{\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A}|^{2}+|F_{A}||\Psi|^{2}+|F_{A}|.

We similarly estimate

\displaystyle-\langle\gamma(\nabla_{A}F_{A})\Psi,\nabla_{A}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\nabla_{A}F_{A},\nabla_{A}\mu(\Psi)\rangle

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon^{2}|\nabla_{A}F_{A}|^{2}-\varepsilon^{2}\langle\nabla_{A}F_{A},\nabla_{A}\eta\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim-\varepsilon^{2}|\nabla_{A}F_{A}|^{2}+1.

The term involving
\Upsilon
can be estimated by

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{A}(D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi)),\nabla_{A}\Psi\rangle

\displaystyle\lesssim|\nabla_{A}^{2}\Psi||\nabla_{A}\Psi|+|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+|\Psi||\nabla_{A}\Psi|

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta|\nabla_{A}^{2}\Psi|^{2}+\delta^{-1}|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+|\Psi||\nabla_{A}\Psi|.

We choose
\delta>0
small.
Let
x\in M
,
r>0
, and let
\chi
be a cut-off function supported in
B_{r/2}(x)
as in the proof of [WZ 2.11].
Taking the inner product of (
D.14
) with
\nabla_{A}\Psi
, multiplying by
r\chi^{2}
and integrating over
B_{r}(x)
, we obtain

r\int_{B_{r}(x)}\chi^{2}(|\nabla_{A}^{2}\Psi|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}|\nabla_{A}F_{A}|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}|{\rm d}_{A}^{*}F_{A}|^{2}\lesssim r\int_{B_{r}(x)}\chi^{2}(1+|\Psi|^{2}+|\nabla\Psi|^{2}+|F_{A}||\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}+\delta|\nabla^{2}_{A}\Psi|^{2}).

Only the last two terms on the right-hand side are problematic.
The one involving
F_{A}
is estimated in the same way as in the proof of [WZ 2.11].
The second term can be moved to the left-hand side provided
\delta
is sufficiently small.
This leads to the desired inequality from [WZ 2.11].

[WZ 2.17], [WZ 2.18]
follow once [WZ 2.10], [WZ 2.11] are proved.

[WZ 2.19]
follows from the previous estimates combined with [WZ 2.20].
The proof of [WZ 2.20] uses [WZ 2.21] and [WZ 2.23].
[WZ 2.23] does not use the equation so it still holds.
However, the proof of [WZ 2.21] relies on the Lichnerowicz–Weitzenbock formula which now acquires additional terms.
The first term involving
\Upsilon
is

\mu(D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi),\Psi)

whose contribution to the proof of [WZ 2.20] can be estimated by

(D.15)

r\int_{B_{r}(x)}\chi^{2}(|F_{A}|+|\eta|)|\Psi|(|\Psi|+|\nabla_{A}\Psi|).

where
x
,
r
, and
\chi
are as before.
One of the assumptions of [WZ 2.20] is that
m_{x}(r)=1
which implies that
|\Psi|
is bounded.
The term
|F_{A}||\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}
is estimated as before.
The other terms in
(D.15)
are harmless and do not affect the proof of [WZ 2.20].
Similarly, the new term in [WZ 2.21] involving
\eta
has the form

2\varepsilon^{-2}\mu(\gamma(\eta)\Psi,\Psi)

and can be estimated by
|\Psi|
.

Finally, in the proof of [WZ 2.20] we use the formula for
\nabla_{A}^{*}\nabla_{A}\mu(\Psi)
from [WZ 2.19] and use the original equation
\mu(\Psi)=F_{A}

In our setup,
\mu(\Psi)=F_{A}+\eta
, so to obtain estimate on the integral of
\Gamma_{\Psi}F_{A}
we use

|\Gamma_{\Psi}F_{A}|^{2}\lesssim|\Gamma_{\Psi}(F_{A}+\eta)|^{2}+|\Gamma_{\Psi}\eta|^{2}.

The estimate on the first term follows from [WZ 2.19] as in the original proof while the second term is uniformly bounded.

∎

The key step in the proof of the compactness theorem in
[
WZ21
]
is the monotonicity formulae [3.5, 3.12, 3.13] for the functions
m
and
N
.
In the perturbed setup, the monotonicity formulae have to be modified.

Proposition D.16
.

There exist constants
r_{0},c>0
with the following property.
Let
(A,\Psi,\varepsilon)
be a solution of
(D.5)
.
For every
x\in M
and
0\leqslant s\leqslant r\leqslant r_{0}
, the functions
m_{x}
and
N_{x}
satisfy

(D.17)

\displaystyle m_{x}(s)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2m_{x}(r),

(D.18)

\displaystyle N_{x}(s)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2N_{x}(r)+cr,

and

(D.19)

\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{N_{x}(s)-cr}m_{x}(s)\leqslant m_{x}(r)\leqslant 2\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{4N_{x}(r)+cr}m_{x}(s).

Proof.

The proof of the monotonicity formula [WZ 3.5] relies on formulae [WZ 3.6, 3.10, 3.11].
Similar formulae hold in the general case, except the estimates on the remainder terms
{\mathfrak{r}}_{D^{\prime}},{\mathfrak{r}}_{D},{\mathfrak{r}}_{m^{\prime}}
appearing in these formulae are weaker.

[WZ 3.6]
The remainder term is now estimated by

(D.20)

|{\mathfrak{r}}_{D^{\prime}}|\lesssim D_{x}(r)+m_{x}(r).

Indeed, we use the same argument as in the proof of [WZ 3.6] using the tensor
T
defined therein.
In our setup, the estimate on
|\nabla^{*}T|
, based on the Lichnerowicz–Weitzenböck formula, has new contributions from
\eta
and
\Upsilon
, which are estimated as follows.
The terms involving
\eta
do not pose any problem as the contribution to
\nabla^{*}T(e_{i})
is bounded by

\langle\gamma(\eta)\Psi+\sum_{\rho}(\eta(e_{i},e_{j}))\Psi,\nabla_{A,e_{j}}\Psi\rangle\lesssim|\Psi||\nabla_{A}\Psi|,

which is the same as the original bound on
|\nabla^{*}T|
in [WZ 3.8].
The contribution of
\Upsilon
to
\nabla^{*}T
is

\langle D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi),\nabla_{A}\Psi\rangle\lesssim|\Psi||\nabla_{A}\Psi|+|\nabla_{A}\Psi|^{2}.

Therefore, the total contribution to the integral

\frac{1}{4\pi r^{2}}\int_{B_{r}(x)}2r_{x}\nabla^{*}T(\partial_{r})

is estimated, up to a constant, by

\frac{1}{r}\int_{B_{r}(x)}|\nabla\Psi|^{2}+|\Psi|^{2}\lesssim D_{x}(r)+r^{2}m_{x}(r),

where to estimate the integral of
|\Psi|^{2}
we use [WZ 2.9].

[WZ 3.10]
The same formula holds but now the remainder term is estimated by

|{\mathfrak{r}}_{D}|\lesssim rm_{r}(x).

Indeed, we follow the same proof as in [WZ 3.10] and estimate the new terms involving
\eta
and
\Upsilon
.
By
D.11
, their contribution to the formula is bounded, up to a constant, by

\frac{1}{r}\int_{B_{r}(x)}|\Psi|^{2}+\frac{1}{r}\int_{\partial B_{r}(x)}|\Psi|^{2}\lesssim r^{2}m_{x}(r)+rm_{x}(r),

where we use [WZ 2.9] and the definition of
m_{x}(r)
.

[WZ 3.11]
The same formula holds but now the remainder term is estimated by

|{\mathfrak{r}}_{m^{\prime}}|\lesssim m_{x}(r)

This is because the proof uses [WZ 3.10] which now has an estimate with a lower power of
r
.
In particular, we have

m_{x}^{\prime}(r)\geqslant-cm_{x}(r).

Therefore,
e^{cr}m_{x}
is a non-decreasing function of
r
, which implies
(D.17)
.

To prove
(D.18)
, we proceed as in the proof of [WZ 3.3] and use the modified estimates on the remainder terms:

\displaystyle N_{x}^{\prime}(r)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}_{D^{\prime}}}{m_{x}(r)}-\frac{4{\mathfrak{r}}_{D}N_{x}(r)}{rm_{x}(r)}-\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}_{m^{\prime}}N_{x}(r)}{m_{x}(r)}

\displaystyle\geqslant-c(1+N_{x}(r)).

Therefore,

(e^{cr}N_{x})^{\prime}(r)=e^{cr}(cN_{x}(r)+N_{x}^{\prime}(r))\geqslant-ce^{cr}\geqslant-c,

which implies that the function

r\mapsto e^{cr}N_{x}(r)+cr

is non-decreasing on
[0,r_{0}]
.
If
0<s\leqslant r\leqslant r_{0}
and
r_{0}
is small, then

N_{x}(s)\leqslant e^{c(r-s)}N_{x}(r)+c(r-s)\leqslant 2N_{x}(r)+cr.

Finally, to prove
(D.19)
, we use [WZ 3.11] with a weaker estimate on the remainder established above.
For every
t\in[s,r]
,

(\log m_{x})^{\prime}(t)=\frac{m_{x}^{\prime}(t)}{m_{x}(t)}=\frac{2N_{x}(t)}{t}+\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}_{m^{\prime}}(t)}{m_{x}(t)}\leqslant\frac{4N_{x}(r)+cr}{t}+c.

Integrating over
t\in[s,r]
and exponentiating yields

\frac{m_{x}(r)}{m_{x}(s)}\leqslant e^{c(r-s)}\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{4N_{x}(r)+cr}\leqslant 2\left(\frac{r}{s}\right)^{4N_{x}(r)+cr},

which implies the upper bound on
m_{x}(r)
.
The lower bound is proved in the same way by integrating

(\log m_{x})^{\prime}(t)=\frac{m_{x}^{\prime}(t)}{m_{x}(t)}=\frac{2N_{x}(t)}{t}+\frac{{\mathfrak{r}}_{m^{\prime}}(t)}{m_{x}(t)}\geqslant\frac{N_{x}(s)-cr}{t}-c.

∎

The monotonicity formula is used in
[
WZ21
]
to prove a lower bound in terms of
|\Psi(x)|
on the regularity scale

r_{A}(x)=\sup\left\{r\in[0,r_{0}]:r\int_{B_{r}(x)}|F_{A}|^{2}\leqslant c_{F}\right\},

where
c_{F}>0
is a fixed positive constant chosen so that the a priori estimates from
[
WZ21
, section 3]
hold.
This lower bound is the content of [WZ 3.2].
This is the key step in the proof of the compactness theorem.
Together with [WZ 2.11], it implies a uniform upped bound on the Hölder norm of
|\Psi|
and the convergence of
A
,
\Psi
on
M{\backslash}Z
, where
Z
is the zero set of the limit of
|\Psi_{n}|
in the Hölder norm.

Since [WZ 2.11] holds in our setting by
D.13
, the same argument can be used provided that that [WZ 3.2] holds as well.
In addition to [WZ 2.9], which is still true by
D.13
, the proof of [WZ 3.2] uses [WZ 3.15] and [WZ. 3.16].
While the statements of these propositions have to be modified, the proofs are the same as those of [WZ 3.15] and [WZ 3.16] except that we have to use
(D.19)
.

Proposition D.21
.

For all
0<s\leqslant r\leqslant r_{0}
and
\delta>0
, if

s\leqslant r\left(\frac{|\Psi(x)|^{2}}{cm_{x}(r)}\right)^{1/\delta},

then

N_{x}(s)\leqslant\delta+cr.

Proposition D.22
.

For every
r\in(0,r_{0}/4]
,
s\in(0,2r]
, and
y\in B_{r}(x)
, if

N_{x}(4r)\leqslant 1,

then

N_{y}(s)\leqslant c(N_{x}(4r)+r).

With these modifications to [WZ 3.15] and [WZ 3.16], the rest of the proof of [WZ 3.2] follows, and therefore also [WZ 3.20] and the convergence of
|\Psi_{n}|
,
A_{n}
, and
\Psi_{n}
.
Finally, the proof [WZ 4.1], which implies that
Z
is nowhere dense, is almost unchanged.
It uses of the Lichnerowicz–Weitzenböck formula by integrating
\langle\nabla_{A}^{*}\nabla_{A}\Psi,\Psi\rangle
over a neighborhood
Z_{\varepsilon}
of
Z
.
The new terms, however, do not change the final inequality because they are estimated by integrals

\int_{Z_{2\varepsilon}}|\eta||\Psi|^{2}+|D_{A}(\Upsilon\Psi)||\Psi|\lesssim\int_{Z_{2\varepsilon}}|\Psi|^{2}+|\nabla_{A}\Psi||\Psi|

which already appear on the right-hand side of the inequality in the proof of [WZ 4.1].
